Chapter 1 (Milnor §1)

1V1.1

the inverse function theorem on manifolds

stronger

to confirmA smooth map between manifolds of equal dimension with invertible derivative at a point

proved, not quotedany class C^n with n ≥ 1complete, not finite-dimensional
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §1, Inverse Function Theorem

For a smooth $f : U \to \mathbb R^k$ with $U$ open in $\mathbb R^k$, Milnor states on p. 4 that if the derivative $df_x : \mathbb R^k \to \mathbb R^k$ is nonsingular then $f$ carries every sufficiently small open set $U'$ about $x$ diffeomorphically onto the open set $f(U')$. He gives no proof, referring to Apostol and to Dieudonné, and warns that $f$ may fail to be injective in the large even if $df_x$ is nonsingular everywhere. The manifold version is never stated: on p. 7 he simply says that at a regular point $f$ carries a neighbourhood of $x$ in $M$ diffeomorphically onto an open subset of $N$.

One of the few results in the book quoted from the literature rather than proved.

Verified edition V1.1

Let $M$ and $N$ be smooth manifolds without boundary modelled on finite-dimensional real vector spaces of the same dimension, $f : M \to N$ smooth on a neighbourhood of $x$, and suppose the derivative $df_x$ is an invertible linear map. Then $f$ is a local diffeomorphism at $x$: there are open sets $U \ni x$ and $V \ni f(x)$ such that $f$ restricts to a diffeomorphism $U \to V$. Hidden hypotheses: none beyond the smoothness-on-a-neighbourhood convention. Deviation (stronger): the formal statement allows real or complex scalars, maps of class $C^n$ for any $n \ge 1$ (including $C^\infty$ and analytic maps; the local inverse is then of the same class), and a model space of $M$ that is only required to be complete, not finite-dimensional. Note: Mathlib listed this theorem as an open TODO; it was proved here from the Euclidean inverse function theorem by the chart argument of the entertaining edition, Chapter 1, Theorem 4.1.

Milnor.isLocalDiffeomorphAt_of_isInvertible_mfderiv

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor quotes the Euclidean case from Apostol and Dieudonné; the formal statement is proved, and it is the manifold case, which he uses from p. 7 on without stating it.
  • Milnor's $f$ is smooth; the formal statement allows real or complex scalars and class $C^n$ for any $n \ge 1$, the local inverse being of the same class.
  • Equal dimensions are replaced by invertibility of $df_x$ as a continuous linear map, and the model space of $M$ need only be complete, not finite-dimensional.
  • The hypothesis is that $f$ is $C^n$ on a neighbourhood of $x$, not merely at $x$: for $n = \infty$ the weaker form gives no single open set on which $f$ is $C^\infty$.
2V1.3

finiteness

stronger

to confirmAt a regular value of a smooth map from a compact manifold to a manifold with closed

points of $N$ closedany class C^n with n ≥ 1
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §1, p. 8

Milnor observes, in italics and without a label, that if $M$ is compact and $y \in N$ is a regular value then $f^{-1}(y)$ is a finite set, possibly empty. His reason is that $f^{-1}(y)$ is a closed subset of the compact $M$, hence compact, and discrete because $f$ is injective near each of its points.

He treats it as an immediate consequence of the inverse function theorem, in one sentence of proof.

Verified edition V1.3

Let $M$ and $N$ be manifolds without boundary. If $M$ is compact, points of $N$ are closed, $\dim M = \dim N$, and $y$ is a regular value of the smooth $f : M \to N$, then $f^{-1}(y)$ is finite. Hidden hypothesis: points of $N$ closed (true when $N$ is Hausdorff).

Milnor.IsRegularValue.finite_preimage

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal statement names the hypothesis that points of $N$ are closed; Milnor's manifolds lie in Euclidean space, so this is automatic for him and he uses it silently.
  • Milnor's $f$ is smooth; the formal statement needs only class $C^n$ for some $n \ge 1$, and allows complex as well as real scalars.
  • Neither statement assumes $M$ or $N$ connected, nonempty, or second countable.
3V1.4

local constancy

stronger

to confirmWith both manifolds Hausdorff, the number of preimages is constant on a neighbourhood

no restriction on the nearby $y'$$M$ and $N$ Hausdorff
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §1, p. 8

For $f : M \to N$ with $M$ compact and $y$ a regular value, Milnor writes $\#f^{-1}(y)$ for the number of preimages and says it is locally constant as a function of $y$, adding the parenthesis that $y$ is to range only over regular values. The explanation he gives is a neighbourhood $V$ of $y$ with $\#f^{-1}(y') = \#f^{-1}(y)$ for every $y' \in V$, built as the intersection of the images of disjoint neighbourhoods $U_1, \dots, U_k$ of the preimages, minus the image of the rest of $M$.

His bracketed construction of $V$ puts no condition on $y'$, so his own proof gives the equality at every $y'$ in $V$.

Verified edition V1.4

If moreover $M$ and $N$ are Hausdorff, then for every $y'$ in some neighbourhood of $y$, $\#f^{-1}(y') = \#f^{-1}(y)$. Deviation (stronger): the parenthesis on p. 8 in which Milnor introduces the count speaks of regular values, but the sentence that follows it produces a neighbourhood $V$ of $y$ and asserts the equality for every $y' \in V$ without restriction, which is what the formal statement proves.

Milnor.IsRegularValue.eventually_ncard_preimage_eq

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's parenthesis restricts the argument of $\#f^{-1}$ to regular values; the formal conclusion holds for every $y'$ in the neighbourhood, which is what his construction of $V$ actually delivers.
  • Hausdorffness of $M$ (to separate the finitely many preimages) and of $N$ (so that the image of the complement of the $U_i$ is closed) are hypotheses here and silent in the book.
  • The companion statement, that the nearby $y'$ are themselves regular values, is not in the book; it is proved from the local inverses that the same argument produces.
4V1.5

the counting principle

restated

to confirmOn a connected set of regular values, if one point is hit then all are.

a proof step made a theoremarbitrary $M$ and $N$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §1, p. 9

In the last paragraph of the proof of the fundamental theorem of algebra Milnor argues that the regular values of $f$, being the sphere minus finitely many points, form a connected set, so the locally constant function $\#f^{-1}(y)$ is constant there; since it cannot vanish identically it vanishes nowhere, and $f$ is onto. He states no theorem: the argument is written for the particular map $f : S^2 \to S^2$ coming from the polynomial.

Nothing in the paragraph uses polynomials or spheres beyond compactness, equal dimension and connectedness of the set of regular values.

Verified edition V1.5

With $M$, $N$ without boundary, $M$ compact, $M$, $N$ Hausdorff, $\dim M = \dim N$: if $R \subseteq N$ is a connected set of regular values containing a point of the image of $f$, then $R$ is contained in the image. If the set of all regular values is connected and meets the image, $f$ is onto.

Milnor.subset_range_of_isPreconnectedMilnor.surjective_of_isPreconnected_regularValues

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor states nothing here: the reasoning is three sentences inside the proof for $f : S^2 \to S^2$, and the formal version isolates it as a theorem about arbitrary $M$ and $N$.
  • The formal statement comes in a local form, for any preconnected set $R$ of regular values meeting the image, and a global form, which is the sentence Milnor uses.
  • The last step, that critical values are in the image by definition, is left implicit in the book and is discharged explicitly in the global form.
  • As in V1.3 and V1.4, $M$ and $N$ are required Hausdorff and $f$ only of class $C^n$ with $n \ge 1$.
5V1.6

the fundamental theorem of algebra

as in the book

to confirmEvery nonconstant complex polynomial has a root.

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §1, The fundamental theorem of algebra

Every nonconstant complex polynomial $P(z)$ has a zero. Milnor proves it by passing from the plane to the unit sphere $S^2 \subset \mathbb R^3$: stereographic projection $h_+$ from the north pole turns $P$ into a map $f : S^2 \to S^2$ fixing the north pole, which is smooth there because in the chart $h_-$ it becomes $z^n$ divided by the polynomial with conjugated and reversed coefficients. The critical points are the zeros of $P'$, finite in number, so the regular values form a connected set, and the counting argument makes $f$ onto.

The proof is offered as the first application of regular values, at the end of §1.

Verified edition V1.6

Every complex polynomial of positive degree has a root. The statement is exactly ∀ P : Polynomial ℂ, 0 < P.degree → ∃ z, P.IsRoot z. Deviation (route, not content): the compact surface is the Riemann sphere $\mathbb C \cup \{\infty\}$ with the two charts $z$ and $1/z$ (Milnor.RiemannSphere), not the unit sphere with stereographic projection; the two are diffeomorphic (a standard fact, not formalized here and not needed). The point $\infty$ is put into the bad set rather than shown to be regular. The intermediate facts are checked as separate statements: the polynomial map is smooth (contMDiff_polyMap), its critical values are finite (critVals_finite), the complement of a finite set in the plane is connected (isPreconnected_compl_critVals), the image is infinite (infinite_range_eval).

Not formalized in Chapter 1: the bridge between Milnor's definition of tangent space (image of the derivative of a parametrization) and Mathlib's (a copy of the model space); the two-link arm.

Milnor.fundamental_theorem_of_algebra

Chapter 2 (Milnor §2)

6V2.2

Lemma 1

hypotheses explicit

to confirmThe preimage of a regular value is a submanifold of the complementary dimension.

dimension carried by a model space $Z$$m \ge n$ becomes the existence of $Z$boundaryless models named
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Lemma 1

If $f : M \to N$ is a smooth map between manifolds of dimensions $m \ge n$ and $y \in N$ is a regular value, then $f^{-1}(y) \subseteq M$ is a smooth manifold of dimension $m - n$. The proof completes $f$ by a linear map $L : \mathbb R^k \to \mathbb R^{m-n}$ nonsingular on the null space of $df_x$, inverts $F = (f, L)$ near $x$, and observes that $f^{-1}(y)$ becomes the hyperplane $y \times \mathbb R^{m-n}$.

That $M$ and $N$ are finite-dimensional and have no boundary is part of his definition of a manifold, so he states neither.

Verified edition V2.2

Let $f : M \to N$ be smooth between manifolds without boundary modelled on $E$ and $F$, with $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$, and $y$ a regular value. Then $f^{-1}(y)$ is a slice submanifold of $M$ modelled on $Z$, hence a smooth manifold of dimension $\dim M - \dim N$. Hidden hypothesis: the dimension bookkeeping is carried by the space $Z$.

Milnor.isSliceSubmanifold_preimage

What differs (the comparison)

  • The dimension count $m - n$ is carried by a model space $Z$ with $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$; Milnor's hypothesis $m \ge n$ is precisely the existence of such a $Z$.
  • Finite-dimensionality of $E$ and $F$ and boundarylessness of both models are named, being part of Milnor's definition of a manifold rather than of his statement.
  • The conclusion is that $f^{-1}(y)$ is a slice submanifold in the sense of V2.1; the smooth structure and the smooth inclusion come from there.
  • The refined form, which names the second component of the chart, is not in the book; Lemma 3 is proved from it.
7V2.3

Lemma 2

restated

to confirmIts tangent space is the kernel of the derivative.

elementwise, not an equality of spacessecond clause is V2.12
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Lemma 2

For a regular value $y$ of $f : M \to N$ and $M' = f^{-1}(y)$, the null space of $df_x : TM_x \to TN_y$ is precisely the tangent space $TM'_x \subseteq TM_x$; hence $df_x$ carries the orthogonal complement of $TM'_x$ in $TM_x$ isomorphically onto $TN_y$.

He proves it from the square $M' \to M \to N$: $df_x$ kills $TM'_x$ because $f$ is constant on $M'$, and a dimension count finishes both claims.

Verified edition V2.3

In that situation, for $p \in f^{-1}(y)$ and $v$ a tangent vector of $M$ at $p$: $v$ is the image of a tangent vector of $f^{-1}(y)$ under the inclusion if and only if $df_p(v) = 0$. That is, the tangent space of the level set is the kernel of $df_p$. Deviation (form): stated elementwise rather than as an equality of subspaces, to avoid coercions between tangent spaces of different manifolds.

Milnor.mem_range_mfderiv_val_iff

What differs (the comparison)

  • The tangent spaces of $M$ and of $f^{-1}(y)$ are modelled on different spaces, $E$ and $Z$, so the formal statement compares vectors instead of asserting $\operatorname{im} d\iota_p = \ker df_p$.
  • Milnor's second sentence, that $df_x$ maps the orthogonal complement of $TM'_x$ isomorphically onto $TN_y$, is a row of its own, V2.12: it needs an inner product on the model space, which an abstract $M$ does not carry.
  • The injectivity of $d\iota_p$, which Milnor gets free from $TM'_x \subseteq TM_x$, is proved for every slice submanifold and is what makes the dimension count available.
8V2.5

Lemma 3

hypotheses explicit

to confirm$\{g \ge 0\}$ is a manifold with boundary $\{g = 0\}$ when $0$ is a regular value of $g$.

dimension carried by a model space $Z$boundary prescribed as in V2.4
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Lemma 3

Let $M$ be a manifold without boundary and $g : M \to \mathbb R$ a smooth function with $0$ as a regular value. Then the set of $x \in M$ with $g(x) \ge 0$ is a smooth manifold with boundary equal to $g^{-1}(0)$.

He gives no proof, saying only that it is just like the proof of Lemma 1.

Verified edition V2.5

Let $M$ be a manifold without boundary modelled on $E$, $\dim Z + 1 = \dim E$, $g : M \to \mathbb R$ smooth with $0$ a regular value. Then $\{g \ge 0\}$ is a half-slice submanifold with boundary $\{g = 0\}$.

Milnor.isHalfSliceSubmanifold_of_regularValue

What differs (the comparison)

  • The dimension of the boundary is carried by a model space $Z$ with $\dim Z + 1 = \dim E$, which in particular forces $\dim M \ge 1$; Milnor leaves the count implicit.
  • The conclusion is stated with the half-slice charts of V2.4, with $B = \{g = 0\}$ prescribed in advance rather than read off the charts.
  • Milnor's "just like the proof of Lemma 1" is made literal: at a boundary point the slice chart of V2.2 for $g$ has second component $g$, and swapping the factors puts $g$ first.
  • Regularity of $0$ is used only where $g(x) = 0$; at points with $g(x) > 0$ any chart of $M$, straightened by a linear isomorphism and translated, is a half-slice chart.
9V2.6

Lemma 4

narrower

to confirmFor a regular value of a map defined on the whole ambient manifold, the preimage inside

$X$ presented as $\{g \ge 0\}$$y$ regular for $f$ on all of $M$transversality replaces $f|\partial X$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Lemma 4

Let $f : X \to N$ be smooth from an $m$-manifold with boundary to an $n$-manifold, with $m > n$. If $y \in N$ is a regular value both of $f$ and of the restriction $f|_{\partial X}$, then $f^{-1}(y) \subseteq X$ is a smooth $(m-n)$-manifold with boundary, and $\partial(f^{-1}(y))$ is exactly $f^{-1}(y) \cap \partial X$.

In the local model $f : \mathbb H^m \to \mathbb R^n$ he uses the regularity of $f|_{\partial \mathbb H^m}$ only to know that the null space of $df_x$ is not contained in $\mathbb R^{m-1} \times 0$.

Verified edition V2.6

Let $M$ and $N$ be manifolds without boundary modelled on $E$ and $F$, and let $Z$, $Z'$ be spaces with $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$ and $\dim Z' + 1 = \dim Z$ (the dimension bookkeeping, as in V2.2). Let $f : M \to N$ be smooth, $y$ a regular value of $f$ on all of $M$, $g : M \to \mathbb R$ smooth, and suppose the transversality condition: for every $x$ with $f(x) = y$ and $g(x) = 0$ there is a tangent vector $v$ with $df_x(v) = 0$ and $dg_x(v) \ne 0$. Then inside the manifold $f^{-1}(y)$ (with the structure of V2.2, modelled on $Z$), the sets $\{g \ge 0\}$ and $\{g = 0\}$ form a half-slice submanifold modelled on the half-space over $Z'$: $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g \ge 0\}$ is a manifold with boundary $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}$. Deviation (form): Milnor's hypothesis "$y$ is a regular value of $f|_{\partial X}$" is replaced by the transversality condition displayed, which is what that hypothesis is used for in his proof (the dimension count in the entertaining edition, Chapter 2, Lemma 4.1). The manifold with boundary is presented as $\{g \ge 0\}$ inside a manifold without boundary; this suffices for every application in the formal text (V2.10, V4.1 and V5.3 use it), but not for Milnor's statement for an arbitrary manifold with boundary. Deviation (hypothesis): $y$ must be a regular value of $f$ on the whole of $M$, not only on $\{g \ge 0\}$ as in Milnor; this holds in every application.

Milnor.isHalfSliceSubmanifold_levelSet

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $X$ is an arbitrary $m$-manifold with boundary; the formal $X$ is $\{g \ge 0\}$ inside a boundaryless $M$ on which $f$ is defined, which covers every later use but not his statement.
  • His hypothesis that $y$ is regular for $f|_{\partial X}$ becomes the transversality condition $df_x(v) = 0$, $dg_x(v) \ne 0$, which is what his proof extracts from it.
  • $y$ must be a regular value of $f$ on all of $M$, not only on $X$ as in the book; this holds in every application in the formal text.
  • Milnor's $m > n$ is the existence of $Z'$ with $\dim Z' + 1 = \dim Z$; unlike Lemma 3 this statement needs no regular value of $g$, since transversality already forces $dg_x \ne 0$ where it matters.
10V2.7

the disk

hypotheses explicit

to confirmThe closed unit ball of $\mathbb R^{m+1}$ is a manifold with boundary the unit sphere

ambient dimension at least $1$inner product space, not $\mathbb R^m$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Example

The unit disk $\mathbb D^m$, consisting of the $x \in \mathbb R^m$ with $1 - \sum x_i^2 \ge 0$, is a smooth manifold with boundary equal to $S^{m-1}$.

It is offered as the example produced by Lemma 3, with no verification of the regular value.

Verified edition V2.7

For every $m \ge 0$, the closed unit ball of $\mathbb R^{m+1}$ is a half-slice submanifold, modelled on the half-space over $\mathbb R^m$, with boundary the unit sphere $S^m$, via $g(x) = 1 - \|x\|^2$. (The ambient space has positive dimension; for $\mathbb R^0$ the ball is a point and the statement would be false.)

Milnor.isHalfSliceSubmanifold_unitDisk

What differs (the comparison)

  • The ambient space is written $\mathbb R^{m+1}$ with $m \ge 0$: the half-slice model needs a factor $\mathbb R$, so the statement would fail for the point $\mathbb D^0 \subseteq \mathbb R^0$.
  • The ball is taken in an arbitrary finite-dimensional real inner product space, and the model space $Z$ for the boundary is named; Milnor's $\mathbb D^m$ is the case $Z = \mathbb R^{m-1}$.
  • That $0$ is a regular value of $1 - \|x\|^2$ is proved, by exhibiting $v = -\tfrac t2 x$ with $dg_x(v) = t$ on the sphere, where Milnor simply invokes Lemma 3.
15V2.9

Lemma 6, the retraction formula

stronger

to confirmFor smooth $P$, Milnor's formula $x + t(x)u(x)$ is a smooth map of norm one wherever

no finite dimension assumedstated on the open set $\Omega$$g$ need not map the disk to itself
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Lemma 6

In the proof of Lemma 6 Milnor writes the retraction explicitly: for a smooth $g : \mathbb D^n \to \mathbb D^n$ without fixed points, $f(x) = x + tu$ with $u = (x - g(x))/\|x - g(x)\|$ and $t = -x \cdot u + \sqrt{1 - x \cdot x + (x \cdot u)^2}$, the expression under the square root being strictly positive, and asserts that $f$ is a smooth map $\mathbb D^n \to S^{n-1}$ with $f(x) = x$ for $x \in S^{n-1}$.

He calls this an explicit computation and leaves the verification to the reader.

Verified edition V2.9

For $P : E \to E$ and $u = (x - Px)/\|x - Px\|$, $t = -\langle x,u\rangle + \sqrt{1 - \|x\|^2 + \langle x,u\rangle^2}$, $f(x) = x + t\,u$: on the set where $x \ne Px$ and the radicand is positive, $\|f(x)\| = 1$; this set is open when $P$ is continuous (isOpen_good), and $f$ is smooth on it when $P$ is smooth (contDiffOn_retr); if moreover $\|x\| = 1$ and $\|Px\| \le 1$ then $f(x) = x$; and if $P$ maps the closed unit ball into itself without fixed points, the ball lies in that set. This is Milnor's "it is easy to verify", done: the positivity of the radicand on the sphere uses $\langle x, Px\rangle = 1$, $\|x\| = 1$, $\|Px\| \le 1 \Rightarrow Px = x$.

Milnor.Retraction.norm_retrretr_eq_selfclosedBall_subset_goodcontDiffOn_retr

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal statements hold in any real inner product space, with no finite-dimensionality, for an arbitrary $g : E \to E$, and on the whole open set $\Omega$ where the formulas make sense.
  • Positivity of the radicand, which Milnor asserts, is the separate claim that the ball lies in $\Omega$: there $\Delta \le 0$ forces $\|x\| = 1$ and $\langle x, g(x)\rangle = 1$, hence $g(x) = x$.
  • That $f$ fixes the sphere needs only $\|x\| = 1$ and $\|g(x)\| \le 1$, through Cauchy–Schwarz; smoothness of $f$ needs $g$ smooth, and openness of $\Omega$ only $g$ continuous.
16V2.10

Lemma 5 for the disk, and the smooth fixed point theorem

narrower

to confirmEvery smooth map of the whole space that maps the closed unit ball into itself has a

Lemma 5 only for the disk$g$ smooth on all of $E$cutoff avoids boundary domains
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Lemma 6

Any smooth map $g : \mathbb D^n \to \mathbb D^n$ has a fixed point. It follows from Lemma 5 (p. 13), that a compact manifold with boundary admits no smooth map to its boundary leaving the boundary pointwise fixed: a fixed-point-free $g$ would produce the retraction $f$, contradicting the special case that the identity of $S^{n-1}$ extends to no smooth map $\mathbb D^n \to S^{n-1}$.

Lemma 5 is proved, following Hirsch, by choosing a regular value $y \in \partial X$ of $f$: then $f^{-1}(y)$ is a compact $1$-manifold with the single boundary point $y$, while such a manifold has an even number of boundary points.

Verified edition V2.10

Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional real inner product space with closed unit ball $D$. Every map $P : E \to E$ which is smooth on all of $E$ and maps $D$ into itself has a fixed point in $D$. Proof as in the book: if not, the retraction $f$ of V2.9, extended to all of $E$ by a smooth cutoff and corestricted to the sphere by V2.8, has a regular value $y$ on the sphere by Brown (V3.3); the transversality condition of V2.6 holds at $y$ because $f$ is the identity on the sphere, which is checked through V2.3 and the chain rule; so by V2.6 the level set $f^{-1}(y) \cap D$ is a compact 1-manifold with boundary $\{y\}$, contradicting V.A′. Deviation (form): Milnor's Lemma 5 is stated for any compact manifold with boundary; only the disk is needed and only the disk is proved. Milnor's Lemma 6 concerns a smooth map of the disk to itself; here $P$ is assumed smooth on all of $E$ (nothing is lost for the book, since V2.11 covers every continuous self-map of the ball). The domain of the retraction is all of $E$ rather than the disk, which is why no manifolds with boundary appear as domains; the cutoff makes this possible.

Milnor.exists_fixedPoint_of_contDiff

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's Lemma 5 holds for any compact manifold with boundary; only the case of the disk is formalized, inside this proof, and nothing in the book needs more.
  • Milnor's $g$ is smooth on $\mathbb D^n$, which for him means smooth on a neighbourhood; the formal $g$ is assumed smooth on all of $E$, and V2.11 shows that nothing is lost for the book.
  • The retraction is extended over $E$ by a smooth cutoff so that no manifold with boundary is ever a domain, and it is regarded as a map into the sphere through V2.8.
  • $\mathbb R^n$ is replaced by a finite-dimensional real inner product space, and the degenerate case $\dim E = 0$ is disposed of separately, the ball being then a single point.
17V2.11

Brouwer's fixed point theorem

restated

to confirmEvery continuous self-map of the closed unit ball has a fixed point.

$G$ continuous only on $\mathbb D$smooth approximation, not polynomialinner product space, not $\mathbb R^n$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Brouwer Fixed Point Theorem

Any continuous function $G : \mathbb D^n \to \mathbb D^n$ has a fixed point. The theorem is reduced to Lemma 6 by approximation: if $\|G(x) - x\|$ has minimum $\mu > 0$ on $\mathbb D^n$, the Weierstrass theorem supplies a polynomial map $P_1$ with $\|P_1 - G\| < \varepsilon$ there, and $P = P_1/(1 + \varepsilon)$ is a smooth self-map of $\mathbb D^n$ whose fixed point contradicts the minimum once $\varepsilon$ is small.

He points out that this pattern, proving a statement about continuous maps by approximating them with smooth ones, recurs later (§8, Problem 4).

Verified edition V2.11

Every map $G$ that is continuous on the closed unit ball of a finite-dimensional real inner product space and maps it into itself has a fixed point. Proof as in the book, with one substitution: the polynomial approximation is replaced by an approximation by smooth maps, obtained from the Stone–Weierstrass theorem applied to the subalgebra of continuous functions on the ball that extend to smooth functions on $E$ (smoothSubalgebra, exists_contDiff_approx); the rescaling by $1 + \mu/2$ and the final estimate are Milnor's. Hidden hypothesis: none; the statement is for a general finite-dimensional inner product space, of which $\mathbb R^n$ is the case in the book.

Milnor.exists_fixedPoint

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $G$ is a map $\mathbb D^n \to \mathbb D^n$; the formal $G$ is defined on $E$ and assumed only to be continuous on $\mathbb D$ and to carry it into itself, which is the same statement.
  • $\mathbb R^n$ is replaced by a finite-dimensional real inner product space, of which $\mathbb R^n$ is the case in the book.
  • The polynomial approximation from the Weierstrass theorem is replaced by an approximation by maps smooth on all of $E$; the final estimate and the rescaling by $1 + \mu/2$ are Milnor's.
84V2.12

Lemma 2, second clause

hypotheses explicit

to confirmFor a regular value $y$ of a smooth $f : M \to N$ and $p \in f^{-1}(y)$, with the model

inner product on the source's modeltangent space realised inside $E$bundled as a linear isomorphism
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Lemma 2

The second sentence of Lemma 2: for a regular value $y$ of $f : M \to N$ and $M' = f^{-1}(y)$, once the null space of $df_x$ is known to be the tangent space $TM'_x$, it follows that $df_x$ maps the orthogonal complement of $TM'_x$ in $TM_x$ isomorphically onto $TN_y$. Milnor states it in one clause and leaves the dimension count to the reader. "Orthogonal complement" needs no comment from him: his manifolds are subsets of $\mathbb R^k$, so every tangent space is a linear subspace of a Euclidean space and comes with its inner product.

The first clause is V2.3, and in the book the two are one lemma with one two-line proof.

Verified edition V2.12

In the situation of V2.2 and V2.3, write $T \subseteq E$ for the tangent space of the level set at $p \in f^{-1}(y)$, realised inside the model space $E \cong T_pM$ as the range of the derivative of the inclusion (tangentSubspace). Then $df_p$ is injective on the orthogonal complement $T^\perp$ and carries it onto $TN_y$, so it restricts to a linear isomorphism $T^\perp \to TN_y$ (normalEquiv, whose underlying map is $df_p$ itself). This is the second sentence of Milnor's Lemma 2 (p. 11), the one that follows "therefore" from the description of the kernel. Hidden hypothesis: an inner product on the model space $E$ of the source, in place of the bare norm of V2.2 and V2.3; the orthogonal complement is taken in $E$. Milnor has this for nothing, his manifolds being subsets of $\mathbb R^k$; the target $N$ needs no inner product, and $M$, $N$ and the boundaryless model are otherwise as general as in V2.3. Proof as in the book: the dimension count Milnor leaves to the reader. Since $y$ is a regular value, $T = \ker df_p$ has dimension $\dim E - \dim F$, so $\dim T^\perp = \dim F$; injectivity on $T^\perp$ is the first clause (V2.3) together with $T \cap T^\perp = 0$, and surjectivity follows because an injective map between spaces of equal finite dimension is onto.

Not formalized in Chapter 2: the general Lemma 5 (for a compact manifold with boundary other than the disk) and Claim 3.1 of the entertaining edition (well-definedness of the boundary), neither of which anything in the book uses.

---

Milnor.tangentSubspaceMilnor.mfderiv_injOn_tangent_orthogonalMilnor.mfderiv_surjOn_tangent_orthogonalMilnor.normalEquiv

What differs (the comparison)

  • The one hypothesis Milnor does not state is an inner product on the model space $E$ of the source, in place of the bare norm of V2.2 and V2.3. He has it for nothing, his manifolds being subsets of $\mathbb R^k$; the target $N$ needs none.
  • "The orthogonal complement of $TM'_x$ in $TM_x$" becomes the orthogonal complement of the subspace $T \subseteq E$ of V2.3, the range of the derivative of the inclusion read in the model space, because the tangent spaces of $M$ and of $f^{-1}(y)$ are modelled on different spaces.
  • The dimension count is written out: at a regular value $df_p$ is onto, so $T = \ker df_p$ has dimension $\dim E - \dim F$ and $\dim T^\perp = \dim F$; injectivity on $T^\perp$ is the first clause together with $T \cap T^\perp = 0$, and surjectivity follows because an injective map between spaces of equal finite dimension is onto.
  • Injectivity and surjectivity are also available separately and elementwise, which is the form the proofs use; Milnor asserts only the isomorphism.

Chapter 3 (Milnor §3)

11V3.1

Sard's theorem

stronger

to confirmThe critical values of a smooth map between open subsets of finite-dimensional spaces

abstract spaces, any Haar measuredegenerate dimensions split off
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §3, Theorem of Sard

Let $f : U \to \mathbb R^p$ be smooth with $U$ open in $\mathbb R^n$, and let $C$ be the set of $x \in U$ at which the rank of $df_x$ is less than $p$. Then $f(C) \subseteq \mathbb R^p$ has measure zero. Milnor notes that the cases $n \le p$ are comparatively easy but gives one proof covering all of them, by induction on $n$ in three steps, the third by counting cubes.

He restates the theorem here, having announced it in §2 (p. 10); a footnote credits the proof to Pontryagin, simplified by assuming $f$ infinitely differentiable.

Verified edition V3.1

Let $E$, $F$ be finite-dimensional real normed spaces, $U \subseteq E$ open, $f : U \to F$ smooth. Let $C$ be the set of points of $U$ where $df$ is not surjective. Then $f(C)$ has measure zero for every Haar measure on $F$. Deviation (stronger): stated for abstract finite-dimensional spaces and arbitrary Haar measures rather than for $\mathbb R^n \to \mathbb R^p$ with Lebesgue measure. This was a necessity, not a flourish: the proof is an induction on $\dim E$ that passes to hyperplanes, which are not literally $\mathbb R^{n-1}$.

Milnor.sard

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $\mathbb R^n \to \mathbb R^p$ with Lebesgue measure becomes arbitrary finite-dimensional real normed spaces with an arbitrary Haar measure on the target.
  • The generality is forced by the proof rather than sought: the induction on $\dim E$ passes to hyperplanes of $E$, which are not literally $\mathbb R^{n-1}$.
  • Milnor's condition $\mathrm{rank}\,df_x < p$ appears as non-surjectivity of $df_x$, the same condition; $E$ and $F$ must lie in one universe of the type theory, a formality.
  • The case $\dim F = 0$ must be excluded by hand, since a Haar measure on a zero-dimensional space is a point mass and not the zero measure; $\dim E = 0$ is handled separately.
12V3.3

Brown's theorem on manifolds

hypotheses explicit

to confirmThe regular values of a smooth map from a second countable manifold are dense.

second countability of $M$ assumed
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, Corollary (A. B. Brown)

For a smooth $f : M \to N$ from an $m$-manifold to an $n$-manifold, Milnor calls $x$ critical when $df_x : TM_x \to TN_{f(x)}$ has rank less than $n$, and calls $N \setminus f(C)$ the set of regular values. Since $M$ is covered by countably many neighbourhoods each diffeomorphic to an open subset of $\mathbb R^m$, he concludes that the set of regular values of $f$ is everywhere dense in $N$.

The countable cover is asserted rather than assumed: for his manifolds, subsets of Euclidean space, it comes for free.

Verified edition V3.3

Let $M$ be a second countable smooth manifold, $N$ a smooth manifold, both without boundary, $f : M \to N$ smooth. Then the regular values of $f$ are dense in $N$. Hidden hypothesis: $M$ second countable, which is Milnor's "covered by countably many neighbourhoods" and is automatic for subsets of $\mathbb R^k$. The target $N$ carries no measure; the proof uses one on the model space of its charts.

How the formal proof follows the book, and where it does not. The proof is Milnor's three steps, with his coordinate change $h(x) = (w(x), x_2, \dots, x_n)$ formalized as a structure (Milnor.Sard.Straightening, exists_straightening) built on V1.1. Step 1 (step1_local) uses Fubini exactly as the book does, with the block-matrix remark proved as a separate claim. Step 2 (step2_local) is the book's. Step 3 (measure_image_vanishSet_inter_eq_zero) replaces Milnor's cube-counting by the statement that a Hölder map of exponent $k+1$ sends a set of Hausdorff dimension $n$ to a set of dimension at most $n/(k+1)$, which Mathlib has; the Taylor estimate behind it (norm_sub_le_of_iteratedFDeriv_eq_zero) is proved by induction from the mean value inequality. Two degenerate cases the book does not mention are handled separately in sardStatement. For $p = 0$ every linear map onto the zero space is surjective, so there are no critical points and the theorem holds trivially (critSet_eq_empty_of_subsingleton); the case must nevertheless be split off, because the steps of the proof use that points have measure zero, and a Haar measure on a zero-dimensional space is a point mass. For $n = 0$, and a target of positive dimension (the other case having been split off), the set $f(C)$ has at most one point, which has measure zero.

---

Milnor.dense_regularValues

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's countable cover by chart neighbourhoods becomes a hypothesis: $M$ is assumed second countable, which is automatic for the subsets of $\mathbb R^k$ he works with.
  • The target carries no measure; the measure lives on the model space of a chart of $N$, so the conclusion is stated purely as density of a subset of $N$.

The Appendix (Milnor pp. 54–56)

13V.A

Milnor.OneManifold.even_ncard_boundary

stronger

to confirmA compact topological 1-manifold with boundary has an even, finite number of boundary

topological, not smoothclassification not used, see V.A2every chart sends $B$ to $0$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor Appendix, Theorem

Milnor proves that every smooth connected 1-manifold is diffeomorphic to the circle $S^1$ or to an interval of real numbers, so that up to diffeomorphism there are exactly four connected 1-manifolds. The boundary count is nowhere stated in the Appendix: it is used in the text, on p. 14, in the form that a compact 1-manifold is a union of finitely many circles and arcs and so has an even number of boundary points, with a footnote pointing to the Appendix for the proof.

The proof rests on parametrization by arc length and on Lemma 1 (p. 54), that two such parametrizations overlap in at most two components, two components forcing $S^1$.

Verified edition V.A

Let $M$ be a compact Hausdorff topological space with a charted-space structure modelled on a space $H$ homeomorphic to the half-line $[0,\infty)$, and let $B \subseteq M$ be a set such that every chart of the atlas sends exactly the points of $B$ in its domain to coordinate $0$. Then $B$ is finite and has an even number of points.

Milnor.OneManifold.even_ncard_boundary

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's 1-manifolds are smooth and his proof uses arc length; the formal theorem assumes only a topological 1-manifold with boundary, which is all the book's applications need.
  • The classification is not used here, and this proof does not state it: what it gives is that the component of a boundary point is the image of an arc whose coordinate interval is closed, and the circle case never arises, the parametrization being always started at a boundary point. The classification itself is now proved, in the smooth setting (V.A2), and the even count derived from it a second time (V.A3); this topological theorem is left untouched and, through V.A′, is still what Chapters 2 and 4 cite.
  • The boundary is given as a set $B$, with the hypothesis that every chart of the atlas, not only the chart at a given point, sends exactly the points of $B$ to coordinate $0$.
  • Compactness and Hausdorffness are used explicitly: the first for finiteness of $B$ and for a finite cover by chart arcs, the second to identify limits in the overlap lemma.
14V.A′

Milnor.IsHalfSliceSubmanifold.even_ncard_boundary

stronger

to confirmThe same for the compact 1-manifolds with boundary produced by Lemma 4.

topological proof underneathambient model space arbitrary
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §2, p. 14

In the proof of Lemma 5 Milnor needs that $f^{-1}(y)$, a compact 1-manifold whose boundary is its intersection with $\partial X$, has an even number of boundary points, and gets this from the classification: such a manifold is a union of finitely many circles and arcs. A footnote refers to the Appendix for the proof. The manifolds he applies it to are the level sets produced by his Lemma 4 inside a manifold with boundary.

He never isolates the parity statement as a lemma; it recurs in the homotopy lemma of §4, in Lemma 1 of §5, and in §6.

Verified edition V.A′

In the language of Chapter 2: let $M$ be a Hausdorff smooth manifold (over any model with corners; its model space need not be finite-dimensional). If $X \subseteq M$ is a compact half-slice submanifold with boundary $B \subseteq X$, modelled on the half-space over a zero-dimensional $Z$ (that is, a compact 1-manifold with boundary), then $B$ is finite with an even number of points.

What is stronger than the book. Milnor proves the classification of smooth 1-manifolds and deduces the boundary count. The formal theorem assumes only a topological 1-manifold with boundary: charts are homeomorphisms, no smooth structure, no arc length. This is strictly more general and is what the book actually uses (nothing downstream needs the diffeomorphism type).

What is weaker than the book. These two theorems do not state the classification. What is proved on the way is that a component containing a boundary point is homeomorphic to a closed interval (exists_closed_arc: an arc from a boundary point extends until its coordinate interval is closed, and then its domain is the whole component). The circle case never arises, because the parametrization is always started at a boundary point, and for the boundary count a component without boundary points contributes zero whatever it is. The classification itself, "every compact connected 1-manifold is $S^1$ or $[0,1]$", is V.J below, and V.J also draws the boundary count from it; but that is a statement about smooth one-manifolds, so it neither subsumes V.A nor is subsumed by it.

How the formal proof differs from Milnor's, and why. Three changes, each made to remove infrastructure the proof would otherwise need. (1) Arc-length parametrizations are replaced by arcs: homeomorphisms from open subsets onto intervals of $\mathbb R$ under which the boundary corresponds to the endpoints (Milnor.OneManifold.Arc). Milnor's "the derivative of $g^{-1} \circ f$ is $\pm 1$" becomes "a continuous injective map of an interval is strictly monotone", which is in Mathlib. (2) Milnor's Lemma (the overlap of two parametrizations has at most two components, and two components force a circle) is replaced by: the overlap of an arc from a boundary point with any arc is a single interval (overlap_ordConnected). The proof is the book's four-edges count, in the form "each end of a component of the overlap tends to an unattained endpoint of the other arc's interval, and two components cannot share one" (end_lemma, clash_upper, clash_lower), with the boundary point ruling out the extra components. (3) The maximal parametrization is obtained not by Zorn's lemma but by a finite induction: a finite cover by chart arcs exists by compactness, and each extension step (exists_extension) absorbs at least one more chart. The parity of the count is then read off from a fixed-point-free involution on the boundary, "the other end of my component" (exists_partner, even_ncard_of_involution).

Hidden hypotheses made explicit. That every chart, not only the chart at the point, sends the boundary to $0$ (the BoundaryCompatible hypothesis; for the manifolds built in Chapter 2 this holds by construction; the proof applies it only to the preferred chart at each point, so it could be weakened accordingly; and for a smooth one-manifold with a half-line model it is not a hypothesis at all but a theorem, V.C, so V.A applies to smooth one-manifolds with no side condition whatever). Hausdorffness of $M$, used in the end lemma, to identify two limits, and again to see that the domain of an arc whose coordinate interval is closed, being compact, is closed in $M$, hence (being also open) the whole component (IsFrom.source_eq_of_mem). Compactness of $M$, used three times: to see that the boundary is finite (it is closed, hence compact, and each boundary point has an arc neighbourhood containing no other boundary point, finite_boundary); for the finite cover by chart arcs; and to show that an arc whose coordinate interval is half-open, $[0, c)$, does not yet exhaust the component of its initial point (a component is closed, hence compact, and its image in the coordinate would then contain its supremum).

Size. 1,778 lines for the two theorems above: 1,704 for the topological theorem, 74 for the specialization. The extension step alone (exists_extension_aux, the gluing of two arcs with continuity checked at the seam) is 380 lines; it is the honest content of Milnor's sentence "$f$ and $g \circ L$ piece together to yield the required extension".

The second half: the classification of smooth one-manifolds.

Throughout this half, $M$ is a smooth manifold charted on a model with corners $I$ over a real normed space $E$ which is one-dimensional, $\operatorname{finrank}_{\mathbb R} E = 1$, and "boundary" means Mathlib's boundary of $M$ relative to $I$ — which V.C shows is the set of points whose coordinate in any chart lies on the frontier of the model's range, so that it is the boundary in the naive sense and needs no compatibility hypothesis. The model itself is not assumed boundaryless: V.C classifies its range into three shapes, the line, a closed half-line and a nondegenerate compact interval, and all three stay alive throughout, the third making $M$ a one-manifold with corners. Smooth means $C^\infty$ except where $C^1$ is noted. The nine items follow the nine stages of the development, in the order in which they are used rather than in the order they were written; the Lean names all live in the namespace Milnor.OneManifold.Smooth unless another is given, and the files are Milnor/Appendix/TwoComponents.lean and Milnor/Appendix/Smooth/.

Milnor.IsHalfSliceSubmanifold.even_ncard_boundary

What differs (the comparison)

  • Only $X$ is assumed compact and $M$ merely Hausdorff, and the model space of $M$ is an arbitrary real normed space, not required finite-dimensional or boundaryless.
  • Being a 1-manifold is expressed by the half-space model $\mathbb R \times Z$ having $Z$ a single point; the smooth structure of $X$ is then discarded and the topological theorem applied.
  • As in the topological form, this proof does not go through the classification Milnor proves: it gives only the parity of the boundary count, which is the sole consequence the book uses. The classification is proved separately (V.A2) and yields the same count a second time, with each component identified and the pairing named (V.A3); this theorem is untouched and is the one Chapters 2 and 4 cite.
100V.B

Milnor's Appendix Lemma 1, the topological half

to confirmLet $A$ and $A'$ be arcs of a Hausdorff space relative to a set $B$ — homeomorphisms of

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.B

Let $A$ and $A'$ be arcs of a Hausdorff space $M$ relative to a set $B$, in the sense of V.A's Arc — homeomorphisms of open subsets of $M$ onto bounded nontrivial intervals of $\mathbb R$ under which $B$ corresponds to the endpoints — and let $S$ be the set of $A$-coordinates of the points lying in both sources. Its components are its hulls, the maximal intervals it contains, which V.A's file already shows to cover $S$, to be pairwise equal or disjoint and to be relatively open in $A$'s interval. Verified: (i) if both ends of one component are interior to $A$'s interval — an inner component — then the transition carries it onto the whole of $A'$'s interval, and it is the only component, so $S$ is a single interval; (ii) any other component reaches an end of $A$'s interval; (iii) hence there are at most two components, the two ends not being available twice; and (iv) two distinct components reach opposite ends of $A$'s interval. This is the general form of Milnor's Appendix Lemma 1 with its affine clause removed, and it is his picture: the two-component case, which V.A excludes by fiat by always starting an arc at a boundary point, is here counted, and it is the circle. Deviation (route of proof): the idea V.A's file lacks is to single out the inner component. It runs off both ends of $A'$'s interval at once, so its transition image is all of that interval and injectivity leaves no room for a second component; the two "clash" lemmas of V.A are therefore not needed here, and V.A's normalization — an arc starting at a boundary point — turns out to have been doing nothing essential. Byproducts: an inner component occurs exactly when $A'$ sits inside $A$ (then $A'$'s interval is open and $A'.\mathrm{source} \subseteq A.\mathrm{source}$), and an inner component forces $A'$ to contain no point of $B$ at all. Hypotheses: $M$ Hausdorff, which V.A's end lemma already needs for uniqueness of limits, and the two arcs' own data. No charts, no smoothness, no compactness, no dimension, no BoundaryCompatible.

101V.C

the one-dimensional model, the real coordinate of a chart, and BoundaryCompatible discharged

to confirmLet $M$ be a $C^1$ manifold charted on a model with corners whose model vector space is

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.C

Verified, for $M$ as above and $C^1$ at least. (i) The three shapes. A closed convex subset of $\mathbb R$ with nonempty interior is the line, a closed half-line either way, or a nondegenerate compact interval; and there is a linear identification $E \cong \mathbb R$, normalised so that the model range $\operatorname{range} I$ is the line, a half-line $[a, \infty)$, or a nondegenerate $[a, b]$ — never $(-\infty, a]$. The third shape is a one-manifold with corners, and it is admitted throughout: no statement of this half excludes it. (ii) Derivatives along the range are unique, so derivWithin is unambiguous even at an endpoint of it. (iii) The real coordinate chartCoord of an arbitrary chart, whose image is an open set cut by the model range. (iv) The boundary is chart-independent: a point in the source of any chart of the atlas is a boundary point exactly when its real coordinate lies on the frontier of the model range — Mathlib's $C^1$ theorem, transported to the coordinate. (v) Hence V.A's BoundaryCompatible hypothesis is a theorem, with no extra hypothesis at all, for a smooth one-manifold whose model range is a half-line and with the boundary taken to be Mathlib's; this is the "cheap spin-off" the design predicted, and it is what makes V.A apply to smooth one-manifolds unconditionally. (vi) The two normalised local models: every boundary point has a chart-derived partial homeomorphism onto $[0, \varepsilon)$ sending it to $0$, smooth both ways, and every interior point one onto $(-\varepsilon, \varepsilon)$. Hypotheses: $\operatorname{finrank}_{\mathbb R} E = 1$ and $C^1$; not Hausdorff, not compact, not boundaryless. Deviations (all restrictions of shape, and flagged in the Lean docstrings): only (v) and the half-line identification are restricted to the half-line shape — V.A's datum, a homeomorphism of $H$ with $[0,\infty)$, simply does not exist for a compact-interval model range. Three things the design warned of, all handled: the compact-interval case stays live; charts at interior points are shrunk away from the model's own endpoints before $\varepsilon$ is chosen; and the sign of the identification with $\mathbb R$ is normalised twice, globally and again at an endpoint, the local sign mattering because it is what keeps the far endpoint out of a normalised chart.

102V.D

smooth arcs

to confirmLet $M$ be a $C^n$ manifold, $n \ge 1$, with one-dimensional model vector space, and call

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.D

A smooth arc is an arc of V.A whose coordinate is $C^n$ on its source as a map of manifolds into $\mathbb R$ and whose parametrization is $C^n$ on its interval as a map from $\mathbb R$ into $M$ — so the whole Arc API of V.A is inherited. Verified: the interval of any arc is convex with nonempty interior, so derivWithin is unambiguous at its endpoints; the transition between two smooth arcs is $C^n$ on the relatively open piece of the interval where it is defined, and that piece is V.A's overlap; the two transition derivatives multiply to $1$, so neither vanishes; a smooth arc is an immersion, its parametrization having nowhere-vanishing derivative read in any chart of the maximal atlas, endpoints included; and smooth arcs exist around every point, with interval $[0, \varepsilon)$ at a boundary point and $(-\varepsilon, \varepsilon)$ elsewhere, built from V.C's two local models. Hypotheses: the structure itself and the transition statements assume nothing of the dimension; the chart and immersion statements and the two existence theorems need $\operatorname{finrank}_{\mathbb R} E = 1$ and $C^1$; only the identification with overlap needs $M$ Hausdorff, and only in order to be able to mention it. All three shapes of V.C stay alive. Deviation (what was expected to be hard and was not): the design flagged one-sided ($C^\infty$ on a half-open interval, Whitney style) smoothness as the technical heart of the stage, and it cost nothing — Mathlib's ContMDiffOn is already relative to the given set, so at an endpoint it is the one-sided condition. The one real wrinkle was that a transition is defined only on a relatively open piece of the interval, so a derivative is computed there and then transported to the whole of it.

103V.E

the length element, and unit-speed arcs

to confirmLet $M$ be a Hausdorff $\sigma$-compact $C^\infty$ manifold with one-dimensional model

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.E

Milnor reparametrizes by arc length, which presupposes a Riemannian metric; his manifolds sit in $\mathbb R^k$ and he uses the Euclidean one without comment. In dimension one a Riemannian metric is a length element: a positive smooth density transforming by the absolute value of the derivative of a transition. That is the one piece of general theory the classification needs and Mathlib does not have, and it is built here. Verified: (i) a density is indexed not by charts but by local real parameters — a RealChart, a partial homeomorphism of an open subset of $M$ onto a subset of $\mathbb R$ with unique derivatives, $C^n$ both ways — of which both a chart read in V.C's coordinate and a smooth arc of V.D are instances, which is what lets a length element be evaluated along an arc with no intermediate layer; (ii) the transition derivatives of real charts form a cocycle and therefore never vanish, and a transition derivative is itself $C^m$ where the charts are $C^{m+1}$ — the chain rule on a possibly half-open set, which is the engine of the whole stage; (iii) a $C^\infty$ one-manifold which is Hausdorff and $\sigma$-compact carries a $C^\infty$ length element, namely $\rho_c(u) = \sum_i \lambda_i(c^{-1}u)\,|\tau'_{c \to \kappa_i}(u)|$ for a smooth partition of unity $\lambda$ subordinate to the chart sources and $\kappa_i$ the real coordinate of the chart at $i$; (iv) the one-sided inverse function rule — if $g$ inverts $f$ on $t$, is continuous there, and $f$ has nonzero derivative within $s$ at $g v$, then $g$ has derivative $1/f'$ within $t$ at $v$, both derivatives allowed to be one-sided; (v) arc length $\sigma(v) = \int_c^v \rho$ on a compact interval is $C^\infty$, strictly increasing, and has a $C^\infty$ inverse with derivative $1/\rho$, at the endpoints included; and (vi) Milnor's Lemma 1.1 — his "by a straightforward change of variables" — that every point lies on a unit-speed arc sending it to $0$, with interval $[0, \varepsilon)$ at a boundary point and $(-\varepsilon, \varepsilon)$ elsewhere, where unit-speed means the length element read in the arc's own parameter is identically $1$. Hidden hypotheses: existence of the length element, (iii), needs exactly four things — $\operatorname{finrank}_{\mathbb R} E = 1$, $C^\infty$ (Mathlib's partitions of unity are built from smooth bumps, and a transition derivative loses a degree, so a $C^n$ manifold would give only a $C^{n-1}$ element), $M$ Hausdorff and $M$ $\sigma$-compact, these last two being the hypotheses of Mathlib's subordinate partition of unity. Those two are used nowhere else in this stage or the next, and $\sigma$-compactness is used nowhere else in the whole classification: see V.I. No boundarylessness, and all three shapes of V.C stay alive. Deviations (two soft spots of the design, both met head-on rather than dodged): the full $\sigma$-compact generality of (iii) was achieved, so the fallback to a finite cover was not needed; and the one-sided smooth inverse was proved, not avoided — Mathlib has only the two-sided rule, which says nothing at an endpoint, so (iv) is proved from the elementary identity relating the two slopes, and smoothness of the inverse then follows by bootstrapping on the order. Two devices made the endpoints painless: all the analysis is done on a compact interval inside the arc's interval, where Mathlib's fundamental theorem of calculus holds at the endpoints and compactness gives continuity of the inverse for free, and only at the end is the arc restricted to a relatively open window; and the window is chosen in the new parameter, since arc length need not be symmetric about the base point.

104V.F

Milnor's Appendix Lemma 1 in full, and the extension theorem

to confirmLet $M$ be a Hausdorff one-manifold with a length element. The transition between two

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.F

Verified. (i) The affine half of Lemma 1. For two unit-speed arcs the derivative of the transition, taken within the possibly half-open interval, has absolute value $1$ at every parameter of the overlap, endpoints included — one line from the cocycle rule, the density being $1$ in a unit-speed parameter; being continuous and omitting $0$ it is constantly $+1$ or constantly $-1$ on each component of the overlap; and so the transition is $s \mapsto \varepsilon s + k$ there, with $\varepsilon = \pm 1$. (ii) Joined to V.B, that is Milnor's Appendix Lemma 1 in full: the overlap has at most two components, on each of which the transition is $s \mapsto \pm s + k$, and if there are two they reach opposite ends. (iii) The extension theorem, in the strong form the whole design is for: the arc glued from two unit-speed arcs is again a unit-speed smooth arc, its source is the union of the two sources, and its coordinate equals the old one on the whole of the first source. Hypotheses: the affine half needs a length element and unit speed and nothing else — no dimension, no Hausdorffness, no compactness, no boundarylessness; Lemma 1 and the two extension theorems add $M$ Hausdorff, because V.B and V.A's overlap API are stated for Hausdorff spaces, and the extension theorems inherit V.A's normalization, that the first arc starts at a boundary point with coordinate $0$ and interval $[0, c)$. Deviation, and it is the one that matters for V.G–V.I: V.A's topological extension theorem cannot be reused with smoothness added. Its conclusion is an existential that forgets the witness's coordinate, and from the statement alone one can recover only that the transition is an increasing homeomorphism fixing $0$ — it could be $s \mapsto s^2$. So the theorem is restated for smooth arcs, reusing the apparatus beneath it verbatim and transcribing the parts of its proof that carry over. About 75 lines of the original then disappear: both one-sided limit computations at the junction and the trichotomy proofs of continuity there, and they disappear because with unit-speed arcs the two branches are the same affine function on the whole overlap, not merely near the seam, so there is no junction to be continuous at. The strong form is not a bonus but forced: the transition being $s \mapsto \pm s + k$, and the normalization pinning down both the sign and the constant, the old parameter is the new parameter on the old source. This is the sharp reason Milnor states his lemma for arc-length parametrizations and not for arbitrary charts.

abs_transDeriv_eq_oneexists_sign_transDerivexists_affine_transmilnor_appendix_lemma_oneexists_extension_aux_unitSpeedexists_extension_unitSpeed
105V.G

Milnor's first case: a compact one-manifold with boundary is $[0,1]$

to confirmA compact Hausdorff connected $C^\infty$ one-manifold with a boundary point $p$ is

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.G

Let $M$ be a compact Hausdorff smooth one-manifold with a boundary point $p$. Verified: the finite-cover induction of Milnor's Step 2, run with unit-speed arcs, produces a unit-speed smooth arc from $p$ with coordinate $0$ and interval $[0, \ell]$, $\ell > 0$, whose source is the whole connected component of $p$ and which identifies the boundary inside that component as $\{p, A^{-1}(\ell)\}$; if $M$ is moreover connected, its source is all of $M$, the arc rescaled by $1/\ell$ is a diffeomorphism $M \to [0,1]$ in Mathlib's sense over the model $\mathbb R^1_\partial$, and the boundary of $M$ is exactly the two points $p$ and $A^{-1}(\ell)$, of cardinality $2$. The last conclusion is Milnor's own corollary, and where V.A counts the boundary points modulo two it identifies them. Hypotheses: $\operatorname{finrank}_{\mathbb R} E = 1$, $C^\infty$, $M$ Hausdorff (used twice, by V.E for the length element and by V.F's extension theorem), and $M$ compact. Three things worth recording. Compactness is used three times and $\sigma$-compactness would not do for two of them — to extract a finite subcover by sources of unit-speed arcs, which is what makes the induction terminate, and to know that the component of $p$ is compact, which is what forbids a proper open sub-arc from exhausting it; only the third use, feeding V.E, is $\sigma$-compactness in disguise. This is why the compact cases need no countability hypothesis. Connectedness is needed nowhere in the exhaustion: the honest conclusion is about the component of $p$, which is the form V.J wants. And the conversion to Mathlib's Diffeomorph through its two Icc bridges cost about twenty lines, the cheapest part of the stage rather than the dearest. Deviation: V.A's finite-cover induction is restated rather than reused, for three independent reasons — its conclusion is a topological arc and the induction feeds its own output back in; its extension step is V.A's, which does not record that the coordinate is preserved, so V.F's is the one the loop calls; and its hypothesis BoundaryCompatible demands a homeomorphism of the model space with a half-line, which does not exist when the model range is a compact interval, a shape V.C deliberately admits. Nothing in V.A's file is edited.

106V.H

Milnor's second case: a compact boundaryless one-manifold is the circle

to confirmA nonempty compact Hausdorff connected $C^\infty$ one-manifold with empty boundary is

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.H

Let $M$ be a nonempty compact Hausdorff connected smooth one-manifold with empty boundary. Verified: there are a length element, a period $T > 0$ and a map $\gamma : \mathbb R \to M$ which is an arc-length parametrization near every time, is $T$-periodic and onto, is injective on $[0, T)$, has $T$ as its least period, and satisfies $\gamma s = \gamma t$ exactly when $s - t$ is an integer multiple of $T$ — in words, such an $M$ is a closed geodesic of length $T$ (Milnor's Step 5). And $M$ is $C^\infty$-diffeomorphic to Mathlib's Circle over the model $\mathbb R^1$, in both directions. Hypotheses: $\operatorname{finrank}_{\mathbb R} E = 1$, $C^\infty$, $M$ Hausdorff (used three times: V.E, V.F, and essentially in the propagation lemma, for uniqueness of limits), $M$ compact (used twice, and $\sigma$-compactness would not do for either — to make the curve accumulate as its time approaches the end of its interval, which is what lets it be continued, and to make it accumulate at $+\infty$, which is what forces it to return and produces the period), preconnected (only for surjectivity) and nonempty (only to choose a base point). No second countability, no orientability, no BoundarylessManifold instance; emptiness of the boundary is about $M$, not about the model, so the model range may still be a half-line or a compact interval. Deviation, and this is the substantive correction of the whole programme: the exhaustion by arcs does not exist in the boundaryless case. Every arc of a manifold with empty boundary has an open interval, while V.F's extension theorem needs the first arc's interval to be half-open, that is, to start at a boundary point; so V.G's induction has no boundaryless analogue in the shape the design assumed. The replacement is to drop injectivity: a unit-speed curve is a map of the line agreeing near each time with the arc-length parametrization of a unit-speed arc, and the engine is a propagation lemma — two unit-speed curves agreeing near one time agree at every time — proved by a connectedness argument run in the parameter of one arc rather than in the time, because the set of times of local agreement need not be closed, and the obstruction is exactly that an overlap can have two components. With curves, continuation needs no gluing of injective arcs and no two-component bookkeeping at all. Two more things fell out. The geometric fact singled out as the one part of Milnor's Step 5 with no counterpart in V.A's file — that two times with the same value have matching directions — is proved by excluding a reflection through local injectivity. And V.B turns out not to be used here: what V.H needs from V.A's file is the relative openness of hulls and V.F's affine half, not the counting of components; V.B remains the right statement of Milnor's lemma, but the design's claim that V.H depends on it was wrong. Two further deviations of route. Mathlib has no quotient-manifold theory, so $\mathbb R / T\mathbb Z$ is not available and the descent goes through local inverses of $t \mapsto \exp(2\pi i t / T)$, whose local diffeomorphism property is obtained without computing a chart of the circle: the two coordinate functions $z \mapsto \operatorname{re} z$, $z \mapsto \operatorname{im} z$ pull back to $\cos$ and $\sin$, which cannot both have vanishing derivative, and rescaling whichever does not gives a smooth left inverse of the derivative. And Mathlib's tangent spaces carry no norm, so "a nonzero linear map between one-dimensional spaces is invertible" had to be stated for topological modules with the inverse supplied as data — which that left inverse provides.

107V.I

Milnor's third and fourth cases: the non-compact ones are $[0,\infty)$ and $\mathbb R$

to confirmA connected Hausdorff $\sigma$-compact non-compact $C^\infty$ one-manifold is

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.I

Let $M$ be a connected Hausdorff $\sigma$-compact smooth one-manifold which is not compact. Verified: if its boundary is empty, $M$ is $C^\infty$-diffeomorphic to $\mathbb R$ over the model $\mathbb R$; if it has a boundary point $p$, $M$ is $C^\infty$-diffeomorphic to $[0, \infty)$ over the model $\mathbb R^1_\partial$, and then $p$ is its only boundary point. On the way: such an $M$ is parametrized by an open interval of times in the boundaryless case — the honest form, since without compactness the interval need not be the whole line, as $(0,1)$ shows — and by a half-open one in the other; every nonempty open interval of the line is diffeomorphic to the line and every $[0, b)$ or $[0, \infty)$ to $[0,\infty)$, by explicit elementary formulas with their explicit logarithmic or rational inverses, so no inverse function theorem is used anywhere in this stage; and no value of a unit-speed curve is a boundary point, the single fact that organises the whole argument. What had to be built: $[0,\infty)$ as a one-chart $C^\infty$ manifold with boundary over $\mathbb R^1_\partial$ — Mathlib has $[a,b]$ only, and there through two charts — with the two bridges, inclusion into $\mathbb R$ and projection from it, that make a map into or out of it smooth exactly when its real expression is; and SmoothArc.shrink, the restriction of a smooth arc to an open subinterval with the parameter shifted, which is what turns the half-open arc issuing from a boundary point into the centred arc with symmetric open interval that V.H's curve machinery accepts. Hidden hypothesis, and where it bites: $\sigma$-compactness is used once and only once, to build the length element of V.E. Everything after that — the arcs, the curves, the propagation lemma, the maximality of the interval of reached times, the continuation step and both classifications — uses only Hausdorffness, connectedness and the failure of compactness. And that one use is exactly where the hypothesis is necessary: see the note after V.J. Milnor's manifolds sit in Euclidean space and get it for free, and he uses it silently, the metric he restricts to $M$ being a Riemannian metric. Deviations from the expected route, both instructive: a repeated value gives a period only once the curve is known to run on the whole line, which here it need not, so injectivity is instead proved from the fact that a repeated value makes the image of one compact interval of times both compact and open, hence all of a preconnected $M$, hence $M$ compact; and a unit-speed curve cannot take a boundary value at all, so it cannot run on a closed half-line of times — the boundary point has to be adjoined by hand, and showing that no second boundary point appears in the closure is the long step, and the only place non-compactness is used in that case. (If a second one did appear, $M$ would be covered by three compact pieces, which is V.G.)

108V.J

Milnor's Appendix theorem, its exclusivity, and the slice form

to confirmMilnor's Appendix theorem. A connected Hausdorff $\sigma$-compact nonempty $C^\infty$

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V.J

Milnor's Appendix theorem. Let $M$ be a connected Hausdorff $\sigma$-compact nonempty $C^\infty$ one-manifold. Then $M$ is $C^\infty$-diffeomorphic to exactly one of

| | boundary empty | boundary nonempty | |:--|:--|:--| | compact | the circle (V.H) | $[0, 1]$ (V.G) | | not compact | $\mathbb R$ (V.I) | $[0, \infty)$ (V.I) |

each model carrying its own model with corners — $\mathbb R^1$ on the circle, $\mathbb R^1_\partial$ on the two half-line-type intervals, $\mathbb R$ on the line — and the case being read off from whether $M$ is compact and whether its boundary is empty. Nothing is assumed about the boundary, about orientability or about second countability, and no compatibility hypothesis is assumed, V.C having proved chart-independence of the boundary. Exclusivity is a statement about the four models alone and is proved separately, all six exclusions: compactness separates $\{$circle, $[0,1]\}$ from $\{[0,\infty), \mathbb R\}$, which settles four of them, a diffeomorphism being in particular a homeomorphism; and the number of boundary points settles the remaining one in each compactness class, by Mathlib's theorem that a diffeomorphism carries boundary onto boundary — stated for two different models with corners — which makes that number a diffeomorphism invariant, its values on the four models being $0$, $2$, $1$, $0$.

The slice form, and the boundary count a second time. In Chapter 2's language: let $X$ be a compact half-slice submanifold with boundary $B$ of a Hausdorff ambient manifold, modelled on the half-space over a one-point $Z$ — that is, a compact one-manifold with boundary in the sense of V.A′. Then every connected component of $X$ is diffeomorphic to the circle or to $[0,1]$; the components carrying a boundary point are exactly those of the second kind; each of those meets $B$ in exactly two points; and $B$ is finite of even cardinality. The last conclusion is V.A′ again, and the point of this item is that it now follows from the classification, as in the book, the pairing being realised by the two ends of a single arc-length parametrization of a component rather than by an abstract parity argument. V.A′ is not cited and is left untouched; what is reused from V.A's file is only the pure combinatorics, that a fixed-point-free involution of a finite set has even cardinality. What had to be built, three bridges the project will want again. (1) The connected components of a one-manifold are open, hence are manifolds in their own right, which is what lets a theorem needing connectedness be applied to a disconnected manifold, and which the design never mentions; it needs nothing but $\operatorname{finrank}_{\mathbb R} E = 1$ and smoothness, since V.D gives every point a neighbourhood which is the source of an arc and the source of an arc is preconnected. (2) The boundary of the model half-line is a single point, which Mathlib could not supply, having no half-line manifold. (3) The boundary count for a possibly disconnected compact one-manifold, whose finiteness is the harder half and could not reuse V.A, because V.A wants a compatibility hypothesis available only when the model range is a half-line. No bridge between the project's half-slice charts and Mathlib's boundary had to be built, Chapter 2 having proved it, nor any ambient/intrinsic translation; the transport of the counting statement follows V.A′'s file line for line. Two things the design got right paid off: stating V.G's exhaustion for the component of a boundary point made the disconnected case cheap, and giving each model its own model with corners cost nothing.

$\sigma$-compactness is necessary. The classification is false without a countability hypothesis. The long line — two copies of the closed long ray $\omega_1 \times [0,1)$ with the lexicographic order topology, glued along their least elements — is a connected Hausdorff smooth one-manifold without boundary which is neither compact nor $\sigma$-compact, and it is diffeomorphic, indeed not even homeomorphic, to none of the four models, since it is not second countable and all four are. So the two non-compact cases must assume $\sigma$-compactness, and they do. In the compact cases nothing extra is needed: compact plus Hausdorff plus a finite-dimensional model gives second countability by Mathlib's own theorem. And the failure is located exactly: it is the length element that does not exist, since a manifold carrying a Riemannian metric is metrizable, so the long line carries none and Milnor's arc-length reparametrization has nothing to start from. Milnor's manifolds sit in $\mathbb R^k$, hence are second countable, and he uses this silently.

Size of the classification. 9,276 lines for the nine stages: 463 for V.B, and 8,813 in the nine files of Milnor/Appendix/Smooth/ for V.C–V.J — 820 (V.C), 630 (V.D), 595 and 720 (V.E), 929 (V.F), 421 (V.G), 1,712 (V.H), 2,202 (V.I), 784 (V.J). The two largest files are the two whose route had to be invented rather than transcribed, V.H and V.I. With V.A and V.A′ the Appendix is 11,054 lines in twelve files. The design pass estimated the classification at about 4,000 lines in nine stages; it took nine stages and rather more than twice the lines. See §11 for the comparison.
