Chapter 4 (Milnor §4)

19V4.3

the Homotopy Lemma

restated

to confirmIf two smoothly homotopic maps from a compact manifold have a common regular value,

homotopy on $M \times \mathbb R$Hausdorff and second countable on $M$even sum, not a congruence
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §4, Homotopy Lemma

Take two maps $f, g : M \to N$ that are smoothly homotopic, the two manifolds having equal dimension and $M$ being compact with empty boundary. Then at any $y \in N$ that is regular for each of them, the counts $\#f^{-1}(y)$ and $\#g^{-1}(y)$ have the same parity.

A smooth homotopy for him is a smooth map on $M \times [0,1]$, a manifold with boundary two copies of $M$ by the footnote on p. 20.

Verified edition V4.3

Let $M$ be a compact Hausdorff second countable smooth manifold without boundary, $N$ a Hausdorff smooth manifold without boundary of the same dimension, $F : M \times \mathbb R \to N$ smooth, $f = F(\cdot, 0)$, $g = F(\cdot, 1)$. If $y$ is a regular value of both $f$ and $g$ then $\#f^{-1}(y) + \#g^{-1}(y)$ is even. Proof as in the book. Case 1 ($y$ also regular for $F$; homotopy_lemma_of_isRegularValue): by V4.1 and V4.2 the set $F^{-1}(y) \cap (M \times [0,1])$ is a compact 1-manifold with boundary $f^{-1}(y) \times \{0\} \cup g^{-1}(y) \times \{1\}$, so V.A′ gives the parity. Case 2: by V1.4 in its strengthened form (eventually_isRegularValue_and_ncard: regular values near a regular value are regular with the same count) there is a neighbourhood of $y$ on which both counts are constant, and by V3.3 it contains a regular value of $F$. Hidden hypotheses: Hausdorff and second countable, as in V1.4 and V3.3.

Milnor.homotopy_lemma

What differs (the comparison)

  • A smooth homotopy is taken to be a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$, not on $M \times [0,1]$, so its domain has no boundary and Lemma 4 and Brown's theorem apply to it directly.
  • Milnor's homotopies extend to this form after reparametrizing $t$, and the level set is cut down to $M \times [0,1]$ by hand, so the two notions of smoothly homotopic agree.
  • $M$ is assumed Hausdorff and second countable and $N$ Hausdorff; second countability is used only for Brown's theorem applied to $F$ on the noncompact $M \times \mathbb R$.
  • The conclusion is the evenness of $\#f^{-1}(y) + \#g^{-1}(y)$ rather than a congruence; both fibres are finite by compactness and regularity.
20V4.4

the Homogeneity Lemma

narrower

to confirmOn a connected manifold, any point can be carried to any other by a diffeomorphism

$N$ boundaryless, all points interiorisotopy over $\mathbb R$, not $[0,1]$Hausdorff on $N$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §4, Homogeneity Lemma

Given any two points $y$, $z$ in the interior of a smooth connected manifold $N$, some diffeomorphism $h$ of $N$ takes $y$ to $z$ and is smoothly isotopic to the identity. Smoothly isotopic in the sense defined on p. 21: joined to the identity by a smooth homotopy on $N \times [0,1]$ every stage of which is again a diffeomorphism.

He proves it by the time-one flow of a compactly supported vector field on a chart ball, remarking that for $N = \mathbb S^n$ a rotation suffices.

Verified edition V4.4

Let $N$ be a connected Hausdorff smooth manifold without boundary. For any $y, z \in N$ there is a diffeomorphism $h : N \to N$ with $h(y) = z$ and a smooth map $H : N \times \mathbb R \to N$ with $H(\cdot, 0) = \mathrm{id}$ and $H(\cdot, 1) = h$, every stage $H(\cdot, t)$ being a diffeomorphism of $N$: a smooth isotopy from the identity to $h$, which is Milnor's conclusion (the relation Milnor.Isotopic). Deviation (proof, not statement): Milnor moves $y$ by the time-one flow of a compactly supported vector field, and isotopes by the flow. Mathlib has no smooth dependence of flows on initial conditions, so the flow is replaced by the explicit map $u \mapsto u + \varphi(u)\,c$ of the model space, $\varphi$ a bump function equal to $1$ at the chart image of $y$ and $c$ the small displacement to the chart image of $z$ (Milnor.Bump.shift). This map is injective by the mean value inequality ($\|\varphi\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\,\|c\| < 1$; shift_injective), surjective by the contraction principle applied to $u \mapsto w - \varphi(u)c$ (shift_surjective), and its inverse is smooth by the inverse function theorem, since $d(u + \varphi c) = 1 + d\varphi \otimes c$ is a perturbation of the identity of norm less than one (shift_symm_contDiff). Transported through the chart and extended by the identity it is a diffeomorphism of $N$ (Milnor.transport, contMDiff_transport), and $u + \sigma(t)\varphi(u)c$ with $\sigma$ the smooth transition function is the isotopy (contMDiff_transport_shift); each of its stages is again a transported bump shift, hence a diffeomorphism. The relation "isotopic" is reflexive, transitive (compose the isotopies at each time), and symmetric (apply $h^{-1}$ to the reversed isotopy); it holds on a neighbourhood of each point by the construction just described (eventually_isotopic); so its classes are open, and a connected space has one class. Hidden hypothesis: Hausdorff, used once, to see that the chart image of a closed ball is closed in $N$. That every stage is a diffeomorphism is not needed in this chapter, but it is used in V5.5.

Milnor.isotopic_of_preconnected

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor allows $N$ to have a boundary and takes $y, z$ in its interior; the formal $N$ is boundaryless, so the restriction to interior points does not arise.
  • The isotopy is a smooth map on $N \times \mathbb R$ rather than $N \times [0,1]$; that every stage is a diffeomorphism is part of the formal conclusion, as in Milnor's definition.
  • Hausdorff is assumed on $N$, used once to see that the chart image of a closed ball is closed. Preconnected also allows $N$ empty, which the given $y, z$ exclude.
  • In the proof the flow of the field $u \mapsto \varphi(u)c$ is replaced by a single Euler step, the explicit map $u \mapsto u + \varphi(u)c$ of the model space, which changes no hypothesis.
21V4.6

the Theorem of §4, first assertion

hypotheses explicit

to confirmThe parity of the preimage count of a map from a compact manifold to a connected one

Hausdorff and second countable on $M$Hausdorff on $N$even sum, not a congruence
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §4, Theorem

Under the standing hypotheses of the section — $M$ compact and boundaryless, $N$ connected, the two of equal dimension, $f : M \to N$ smooth — the number of preimages has the same parity at any two regular values $y$, $z$: $\#f^{-1}(y) \equiv \#f^{-1}(z) \pmod 2$.

The Theorem carries no letter and no number; its standing hypotheses on $M$ and $N$ are set out in the sentence just before it.

Verified edition V4.6

Let $M$ be a compact Hausdorff second countable smooth manifold without boundary, $N$ a connected Hausdorff smooth manifold without boundary of the same dimension, $f : M \to N$ smooth. If $y$ and $z$ are regular values of $f$ then $\#f^{-1}(y) + \#f^{-1}(z)$ is even. Proof as in the book: V4.4 gives $h$ isotopic to the identity with $h(y) = z$; $z$ is a regular value of $h \circ f$ by V4.5, with $(h \circ f)^{-1}(z) = f^{-1}(y)$; the map $(x, t) \mapsto H(f(x), t)$ is a smooth homotopy from $f$ to $h \circ f$; V4.3 applies.

Milnor.even_ncard_add_ncard_of_isRegularValue

What differs (the comparison)

  • $M$ is assumed Hausdorff and second countable and $N$ Hausdorff; for Milnor's submanifolds of $\mathbb R^k$ these hold automatically and are never mentioned.
  • Connectedness of $N$ is used in the weaker form of preconnectedness, which also allows $N$ empty, and the conclusion is stated as the evenness of a sum rather than a congruence.
  • Compactness of $N$ is not assumed, matching Milnor's parenthetical on p. 20 that one may as well assume it but need not.
22V4.8

the Theorem of §4, second assertion

restated

to confirmSmoothly homotopic maps have the same degree modulo 2.

homotopy on $M \times \mathbb R$Hausdorff and second countable on $M$empty $N$ handled separately
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §4, Theorem

The second assertion of the same unlettered Theorem: the common residue class $\deg_2(f)$ is unchanged when $f$ is replaced by a smoothly homotopic map. Milnor proves it by picking, with Sard's theorem, a value regular for both maps at once and chaining the congruences.

He draws from it on p. 24 that the identity of a compact boundaryless manifold is not smoothly homotopic to a constant.

Verified edition V4.8

For $M$, $N$ as in V4.6 and $F : M \times \mathbb R \to N$ smooth, $\deg_2 F(\cdot, 0) = \deg_2 F(\cdot, 1)$. Proof as in the book: the two end maps have a common regular value (the regular values of the first form an open set by V1.4, nonempty by V3.3, and the regular values of the second are dense by V3.3), and V4.3 gives the parity. The case $N$ empty is handled separately (both degrees are $0$).

Milnor.degMod2_eq_of_homotopy

What differs (the comparison)

  • The homotopy is a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$ rather than $M \times [0,1]$, and the two maps compared are named as its ends $F(\cdot, 0)$ and $F(\cdot, 1)$.
  • $M$ is assumed Hausdorff and second countable and $N$ Hausdorff and preconnected; Milnor's manifolds have these properties by sitting in $\mathbb R^k$.
  • An empty $N$ is a separate case, forced by the convention of V4.7 that a map with no regular value has degree $0$.
23V4.9

maps of nonzero degree are onto

not in the book

to confirmA map of nonzero degree modulo 2 is surjective.

not stated in the bookvacuous regularity off the image
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §4, p. 20

The book states no such corollary. The nearest thing is the parenthetical aside made while the section is being set up, that nothing is lost by taking $N$ compact and boundaryless, because a degree into any other target is forced to vanish. That aside is this fact applied to a point missed by the compact image of $M$.

The examples he does draw on p. 24 concern the degree of a constant and of the identity, not surjectivity.

Verified edition V4.9

For $f$ as in V4.6, if $\deg_2 f \ne 0$ then $f$ is surjective: a point outside the range is a regular value with empty preimage.

Not formalized in Chapter 4: Milnor's example that a map homotopic to a constant has even counts and his remark on $\mathbb{RP}^n$ (both are immediate from V4.8 and are examples, not theorems).

Size. 894 lines in the five files of Chapter 4: 97 for V4.1, 215 for V4.2–V4.3, 137 for the bump diffeomorphism of the model space, 272 for the transport and the Homogeneity Lemma (including the isotopy through diffeomorphisms that V5.5 uses), 173 for V4.5–V4.9, that is, the Theorem and the degree. The strengthened V1.4 (about 80 lines) was written for this chapter but sits in Chapter 1's RegularValue.lean and is counted there. The estimate of 1,500 lines given in the previous edition of §8 was too high, mainly because the inverse function theorem in Mathlib already delivers the smoothness of the inverse of the bump shift.

Milnor.surjective_of_degMod2_ne_zerodegMod2_eq_zero_of_notMem_range

What differs (the comparison)

  • No such corollary appears in §4; the closest sentence is the parenthetical on p. 20 that a mod 2 degree into a noncompact $N$ must vanish.
  • The argument is that a point not in the image is a regular value with empty fibre, so V4.7 computes the degree there as $0$.

Chapter 5 (Milnor §5)

24V5.0

orientations

restated

to confirmAn orientation of a manifold is an orientation of each tangent space, carried to the

orientation as a ray of top formscompatibility read in the chartssign defined for every linear map
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, Definitions, pp. 26–27

An orientation of a finite-dimensional real vector space is an equivalence class of ordered bases, two of them equivalent when the change-of-basis determinant is positive; for the zero space an orientation is the symbol $+1$ or $-1$. An oriented manifold carries an orientation of each $T_xM$, and for $m \ge 1$ these must fit together: around every point there is a diffeomorphism onto an open subset of $\mathbb R^m$ or $\mathbb H^m$ whose derivative carries each chosen orientation to the standard orientation of $\mathbb R^m$. For $f : M \to N$ between oriented $n$-manifolds and $x$ a regular point, $\operatorname{sign} df_x$ is $+1$ or $-1$ according as $df_x$ preserves or reverses orientation.

He admits boundary from the start, so a chart may land in the half space $\mathbb H^m$, and he fixes the standard orientation of $\mathbb R^n$ to have something to compare with.

Verified edition V5.0

For finite-dimensional real spaces $E$, $F$ of the same dimension $n$ with orientations $a$, $b$ (Mathlib's Orientation, a ray of alternating $n$-forms) and a linear map $T : E \to F$, the sign $\operatorname{sgn}(a, b, T) \in \{1, -1, 0\}$ is defined by pulling a representative form of $b$ back along $T$ and comparing with a representative of $a$: the pullback is a multiple $c\,\omega$, and the sign is the sign of $c$. It is $0$ exactly when $T$ is singular, multiplicative under composition, and $1$ for the identity. An orientation of a manifold $M$ modelled on $E$ is a choice of orientation $o_x$ of $E = T_xM$ for each $x$ such that, for each $x$, the derivative at nearby points $y$ of the chart at $x$ carries $o_y$ to $o_x$ (sign $1$). The sign of $f : M \to N$ at $x$ is $\operatorname{sgn}(o_x, o_{f(x)}, df_x)$. The product $M \times \mathbb R$ is oriented by declaring a positive basis of $E$ followed by $(0,1)$ positive. Deviation (definition): Milnor defines an orientation as a locally consistent choice of orientations of tangent spaces; the formal definition makes "locally consistent" precise through the derivatives of the preferred charts. The reader should confirm that this is the intended notion; nothing in the chapter depends on the choice of charts, since every statement is chart-independent, but the definition is phrased through them.

Milnor.orSignMilnor.OrientMilnor.signAtMilnor.orProdOrient.prodReal

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's orientation of a space is a class of ordered bases; the formal one is a ray of nonzero alternating $n$-forms, the class of the determinant form of a positive basis.
  • Milnor asks for charts onto $\mathbb R^m$ or $\mathbb H^m$ carrying the chosen orientations to the standard one; the formal condition compares $o_y$ with $o_x$ through the chart at $x$ alone.
  • Milnor defines the sign only at a regular point, where it is $\pm 1$; the formal sign is defined for every linear map and is $0$ exactly when the map is singular.
  • The dimension $n$ is a parameter of the formal definition, so each theorem carries $\dim E = n$ as a hypothesis; there is no oriented atlas and no standard orientation of a model space.
25V5.1

local constancy of the sign

not in the book

to confirmThe sign of the derivative is locally constant near a regular point.

stated only in the formalizationvariant for f smooth on an open set
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, p. 27

The book states nothing here. On p. 27 Milnor asserts that $\deg(f;y)$ is a locally constant function of $y$, and in that assertion the local constancy of $\operatorname{sign} df_x$ in $x$ near a regular point is used without comment.

Verified edition V5.1

If $x_0$ is a regular point of a smooth map $f : M \to N$ between oriented manifolds of the same dimension, then $\operatorname{sign} df_x = \operatorname{sign} df_{x_0}$ for $x$ near $x_0$. This is the continuity statement Milnor uses without comment on p. 27; its proof is the chain rule in charts together with the continuity of a determinant.

Milnor.eventually_signAt_eq

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor never states this; it is the step behind his remark that the signed count is locally constant, and its proof is the chain rule in charts with the continuity of a determinant.
  • The open-set variant, where $f$ is smooth only near $x_0$, is what Lemma 1 needs for the pair maps built from a local coordinate along an arc.
26V5.3

Lemma 1, ambient form

restated

to confirmFor $X = \{g \ge 0\}$ compact in a boundaryless oriented $M'$ and $y$ regular for $f$

boundary presented as {g ≥ 0}signs of the ambient pair map (f, g)y regular for f on all of M′
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, Lemma 1

Let $X$ be a compact oriented manifold with boundary, $M = \partial X$ oriented as the boundary of $X$, and $N$ an oriented $n$-manifold. If $f : M \to N$ extends to a smooth $F : X \to N$, then $\deg(f;y) = 0$ for every regular value $y$ of $f$.

He proves it first for a $y$ regular for $F$ as well, then reaches a general regular value of $f$ by the local constancy of the count; the arc $A \subseteq F^{-1}(y)$ is oriented by a rule on positive bases of $TX_x$, after which the two end signs "follow immediately".

Verified edition V5.3

Let $M'$ be an oriented $(n{+}1)$-manifold without boundary, $N$ an oriented $n$-manifold without boundary, $g : M' \to \mathbb R$ smooth with $X = \{g \ge 0\}$ (more precisely $f^{-1}(y) \cap X$) compact, $f : M' \to N$ smooth, $y$ a regular value of $f$ such that at each point of $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}$ some vector in the kernel of $df$ is not in the kernel of $dg$ (this is "$y$ is a regular value of $f|\partial X$"). Then $$\sum_{x \in f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}} \operatorname{sign}\, d(f, g)_x = 0,$$ the signs of the pair map $(f, g) : M' \to N \times \mathbb R$ being taken for the orientation of $M'$ and the product orientation of $N \times \mathbb R$. Deviation (form): Milnor's Lemma 1 is stated for an abstract compact oriented manifold $X$ with boundary $M = \partial X$ and says $\deg(f|M; y) = 0$ for the boundary orientation of $M$. Here $X$ is presented inside a boundaryless $M'$ as $\{g \ge 0\}$, and the sign at a boundary point is expressed through the ambient pair map $(f, g)$ instead of through the boundary orientation of $\{g = 0\}$; with Milnor's convention "outward normal first" the two agree up to the global sign $(-1)^{n+1}$ (Milnor.signAt_levelOrient_eq', proved in Chapter 6 and used in V6.1), which does not affect the statement. The manifolds with boundary that occur in the book (the cylinder, regions in $\mathbb R^m$) are all of this form. Deviation (division of labour): the formal statement asks that $y$ be a regular value of $f$ on all of $M'$, which is Milnor's first case; his last paragraph, which reduces a regular value of $f|\partial X$ to that case by moving $y$ slightly, is carried out inside V5.4 instead, where a nearby regular value of the homotopy is chosen. Proof as in the book, with the sentence "we leave the details to the reader" carried out: $f^{-1}(y) \cap X$ is a compact 1-manifold with boundary (V2.6); the arc lemmas of the Appendix (OneManifold.exists_closed_arc, OneManifold.finite_boundary, §10) pair its boundary points by arcs; along an arc, for any local coordinate $c$ of the arc, the quantity $\operatorname{sign} d(f,c) \cdot (\text{direction of } c \text{ along the arc})$ is independent of $c$ (a two-by-two block computation), locally constant, hence constant; at the two ends the coordinate $c = g$ points into the arc at one end and out of it at the other, so the two signs are opposite (Milnor.bdrySign_end_eq_neg). Hidden hypotheses: $M'$ and $N$ Hausdorff (the formal statement carries both; the proof uses only the first).

Milnor.lemmaOne_ambient

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $X$ is an abstract compact oriented manifold with boundary; the formal $X$ is the region $\{g \ge 0\}$ inside a boundaryless $M'$, and only $f^{-1}(y) \cap X$ is assumed compact.
  • The boundary sign is that of the ambient pair map $(f,g)$; it differs from the sign for Milnor's boundary orientation by $(-1)^{n+1}$, which leaves the vanishing intact.
  • The formal $y$ must be a regular value of $f$ on all of $M'$, so this is Milnor's first case; his passage to a $y$ that is critical for $F$ is carried out inside V5.4 instead.
  • $0$ need not be a regular value of $g$: only the transversality at the points of $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g = 0\}$ is required, which is Milnor's "$y$ is a regular value of $f|\partial X$".
27V5.4

Lemma 2 of §5, homotopy invariance at a common regular value

hypotheses explicit

to confirmSmoothly homotopic maps have the same signed count at a common regular value.

homotopy defined on all of M × ℝM second countable, N HausdorffN need not be connected
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, Lemma 2

Let $F : [0,1] \times M \to N$ be a smooth homotopy between $f = F(0,\cdot)$ and $g = F(1,\cdot)$, with $M$ compact and $M$, $N$ oriented $n$-manifolds without boundary. Then $\deg(g;y) = \deg(f;y)$ for any common regular value $y$.

He deduces it from Lemma 1 for the oriented product $[0,1] \times M$, whose boundary is $1 \times M$ with the right orientation and $0 \times M$ with the wrong one, so that the boundary degree is the difference of the two counts.

Verified edition V5.4

Let $M$ be a compact Hausdorff second countable oriented manifold without boundary, $N$ a Hausdorff oriented manifold without boundary of the same dimension, $F : M \times \mathbb R \to N$ smooth, $f = F(\cdot,0)$, $g = F(\cdot,1)$, $y$ a regular value of both. Then $\deg(f; y) = \deg(g; y)$. Proof as in the book: V5.3 for the cylinder $X = \{t(1-t) \ge 0\} = M \times [0,1]$ in $M' = M \times \mathbb R$, the sign of $(F, t(1-t))$ at $(x, 0)$ being $\operatorname{sign} df_x$ and at $(x, 1)$ being $-\operatorname{sign} dg_x$ (the block formula of V5.0); then the reduction to a regular value of $F$ by V5.2 and Brown, as in V4.3. This is Milnor's Lemma 2 of §5; his Theorem B follows from it once the degree is defined (V5.7).

Milnor.degAt_eq_of_homotopy

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal homotopy is smooth on $M \times \mathbb R$, not on $[0,1] \times M$; under Milnor's convention his $F$ already extends smoothly to a neighbourhood of the cylinder.
  • Second countability of $M$ is named because the reduction from a common regular value of $f$ and $g$ to a regular value of the homotopy goes through Sard and Brown.
  • Hausdorffness of $M$ and $N$ is named; connectedness of $N$, which Milnor assumes when he introduces the degree, is nowhere used at a fixed regular value.
  • The cylinder is realized as $\{t(1-t) \ge 0\}$ in $M \times \mathbb R$, the sign of $(F, t(1-t))$ being $\operatorname{sign} df_x$ at $t = 0$ and $-\operatorname{sign} dg_x$ at $t = 1$.
28V5.5

isotopies preserve orientation

restated

to confirmA diffeomorphism reached from the identity by an isotopy through diffeomorphisms

asserted without proof in the bookisotopy data repeated in the conclusionN Hausdorff assumed but unused
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, p. 29

In the proof of Theorems A and B he chooses a diffeomorphism $h : N \to N$ carrying the regular value $y$ to the regular value $z$ and isotopic to the identity, and says that $h$ will then preserve orientation.

The claim occupies half a sentence and is given no argument; it rests on his notion of isotopy as a homotopy through diffeomorphisms.

Verified edition V5.5

The isotopy $H$ of V4.4 has every stage $H(\cdot, t)$ a diffeomorphism (this is Milnor's notion of isotopy, and the bump-function construction gives it for free). For such an isotopy from the identity to $h$, $\operatorname{sign} dh = 1$ everywhere: the sign of $H(\cdot, t)$ at $x$ is nonzero for all $t$, locally constant in $(x, t)$ by V5.1 applied to $(x, t) \mapsto (H(x,t), t)$, hence constant in $t$, hence equal to its value $1$ at $t = 0$.

Milnor.exists_isotopy_signAt_eq_one

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's sentence is an aside inside a proof; here it is a theorem, proved by the local constancy in $t$ of the sign of $(x,t) \mapsto (H(x,t),t)$ and its value $1$ at $t = 0$.
  • The formal statement hands back the whole isotopy together with the sign, because the proof of Theorem A reuses $H$ as the homotopy from $f$ to $h \circ f$.
  • That every stage is a diffeomorphism, implicit in Milnor's word "isotopic", is carried explicitly and is what makes the sign nonzero for all $t$.
  • The formal statement assumes $N$ Hausdorff, a hypothesis the proof never uses.
29V5.6

Theorem A of §5

hypotheses explicit

to confirmThe signed count is the same at all regular values ($N$ connected).

M Hausdorff and second countableN preconnected, so possibly empty
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, Theorem A

For $M$ compact and $N$ connected, oriented $n$-manifolds without boundary, and $f : M \to N$ smooth, the integer $\deg(f;y)$ does not depend on the choice of regular value $y$.

He proves it as in §4: move $y$ to $z$ by a diffeomorphism isotopic to the identity, note $\deg(f;y) = \deg(h \circ f; h(y))$ "by inspection", then apply Lemma 2.

Verified edition V5.6

For $M$ compact Hausdorff second countable, $N$ connected Hausdorff, both oriented without boundary, of the same dimension, and $f : M \to N$ smooth, $\deg(f; y) = \deg(f; z)$ for any two regular values. Proof as in the book: V4.4 and V5.5 give an orientation-preserving $h$ isotopic to the identity with $h(y) = z$; $\deg(h \circ f; z) = \deg(f; y)$ because the signs multiply (Milnor.signAt_comp); and $\deg(h \circ f; z) = \deg(f; z)$ by V5.4 along the homotopy $(x, t) \mapsto H(f(x), t)$.

Milnor.degAt_eq_of_isRegularValue

What differs (the comparison)

  • Hausdorffness and second countability of $M$ are named: the first for the finiteness of the fibres, the second for Brown's theorem inside Lemma 2.
  • Connectedness of $N$ is weakened to preconnectedness, so an empty $N$ is allowed and the statement is then vacuous.
  • Milnor's step $\deg(f;y) = \deg(h \circ f; h(y))$, which he calls inspection, is here the multiplicativity of the sign under composition together with V5.5.
30V5.7

the Brouwer degree and Theorem B of §5

hypotheses explicit

to confirmThe Brouwer degree is well defined, homotopy invariant, and nonzero only for

degree total, 0 when N is emptyregular value picked by choicesurjectivity added, not in the book
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, Theorem B

The common value of $\deg(f;y)$ over the regular values is the degree of $f$, written $\deg f$. Theorem B: if $f$ is smoothly homotopic to $g$, then $\deg f = \deg g$.

He illustrates with $z \mapsto z^k$ on the circle, with degree $0$ for a constant map and $\pm 1$ for a diffeomorphism, and concludes that an orientation reversing diffeomorphism is not homotopic to the identity.

Verified edition V5.7

For $M$, $N$ as in V5.6, $\deg f \in \mathbb Z$ is defined as $\deg(f; y)$ for a regular value $y$ chosen by the axiom of choice ($0$ if there is none, which happens only for $N$ empty); deg_eq says $\deg f = \deg(f; y)$ for every regular value $y$. Smoothly homotopic maps have the same degree (deg_eq_of_homotopy, Milnor's Theorem B, from V5.4 at a common regular value); a map of nonzero degree is surjective; the identity has degree $1$.

Milnor.degdeg_eqdeg_eq_of_homotopysurjective_of_deg_ne_zerodeg_id

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal $\deg f$ is a total function, defined with no hypotheses by choosing a regular value; the theorems then add compactness, Hausdorffness, second countability and preconnectedness.
  • Its value is $0$ when $f$ has no regular value, which under those hypotheses happens only for $N$ empty, the one case Milnor's connectedness excludes.
  • Homotopy invariance needs no common regular value in the formal statement: one is produced by Sard applied to the two ends of the homotopy.
  • That a map of nonzero degree is surjective has no counterpart in §5; Milnor there only remarks that a constant map has degree $0$.
31V5.8

the induced orientation of a level set

narrower

to confirmThe level set $g^{-1}(0)$ in an oriented vector space is oriented by "outward vector

only level sets in a vector spaceoutward vector given by a formulacontinuity proved, not assumed
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, p. 26

An orientation of $X$ induces one on $\partial X$: at $x \in \partial X$ take a positively oriented basis $(v_1,\dots,v_m)$ of $TX_x$ with $v_2,\dots,v_m$ tangent to the boundary and $v_1$ an outward vector; then $(v_2,\dots,v_m)$ is a positive basis of $T(\partial X)_x$. For $m = 1$ a boundary point gets $-1$ or $+1$ according as a positive vector there points inward or outward.

He leaves unmentioned that the rule is independent of the chosen basis and varies continuously, and gives $\mathbb S^{m-1}$ oriented as $\partial \mathbb D^m$ as his example.

Verified edition V5.8

Let $E$ be an oriented space of dimension $n + 1$, $g : E \to \mathbb R$ smooth with $0$ a regular value, $S = g^{-1}(0)$ (a submanifold by V2.2). Orient $T_qS$ by declaring a basis $z$ positive when $(\text{outward}(q), z)$ is a positive basis of $E$, where $\text{outward}(q) = -\sum_i dg_q(e_i)\,e_i$ for a basis $e$ of $E$ fixed once by the axiom of choice (finBasisOfFinrankEq), a vector with $dg_q(\text{outward}(q)) < 0$. Any other vector $w$ with $dg_q(w) < 0$ gives the same orientation, since $w$ is a positive multiple of $\text{outward}(q)$ plus a vector tangent to $S$ (Milnor.contractForm_eq_smul, proved in Chapter 6); and the choice of the top form representing the orientation of $E$ does not matter either (rayOfNeZero_contractForm). This is an orientation of $S$ in the sense of V5.0. This is Milnor's boundary orientation of $\partial X$ for $X = \{g \ge 0\} \subseteq E$ ("outward normal first"). Deviation (generality): only for level sets in a vector space $E$, not in an arbitrary oriented manifold; this covers the spheres and the regions of $\mathbb R^m$ used in the book. The proof is the continuity of the contracted form in the chart, a determinant computation.

Milnor.levelOrientcontractOr

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor orients $\partial X$ for any oriented manifold with boundary; the formal construction covers only $S = g^{-1}(0)$ inside an oriented vector space, that is, the boundary of $\{g \ge 0\}$.
  • Milnor's $v_1$ is any outward vector; the formal one is the fixed $\nu(q) = -\sum_i dg_q(e_i)e_i$, and separate lemmas show that any $w$ with $dg_q(w) < 0$ gives the same orientation.
  • Milnor takes for granted that his rule defines an orientation; the formal item includes the proof that it varies continuously in the sense of V5.0.
  • The regions of $\mathbb R^m$ and the spheres that the book actually orients this way are all level sets in a vector space, so the restriction costs nothing in §§5–8.
32V5.9

the antipodal map

stronger

to confirmThe antipodal map of $S^n$ has sign $(-1)^{n+1}$ at every point, and, for $n \ge 1$,

sign computed at every pointsphere oriented from an arbitrary basisoutward vector points into the ball
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, p. 30

The antipodal map of $\mathbb S^n$ has degree $(-1)^{n+1}$, since it is the composition of the $n+1$ coordinate reflections $r_i$, each of degree $-1$. Hence for $n$ even the antipodal map is not smoothly homotopic to the identity, a fact the degree modulo $2$ does not see.

The sphere carries the orientation fixed on the preceding page, as the boundary of the disk $\mathbb D^{n+1}$.

Verified edition V5.9

On the unit sphere $S^n \subseteq \mathbb R^{n+1}$, presented as the level set of $\langle x, x\rangle - 1$ with the orientation of V5.8 (called sphOr), the antipodal map $x \mapsto -x$ has sign $(-1)^{n+1}$ at every point, for every $n \ge 0$, and degree $(-1)^{n+1}$ for $n \ge 1$, the degree of §5 needing a connected target. Milnor computes this as a composite of $n + 1$ reflections; here it is read off directly from the contracted form, since $-1$ reverses the outward vector and each of the $n$ tangent vectors. Flag (the orientation of the sphere): sphOr is the level-set orientation of V5.8 for the orientation of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ given by the basis chosen by the axiom of choice, not by the standard basis. Moreover the region $\{\langle x,x\rangle \ge 1\}$ is the exterior of the ball, so the "outward" vector of V5.8 points into the ball and sphOr is the boundary orientation of the exterior: for the standard orientation of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ this rule would give the opposite of Milnor's orientation of $S^n$ as the boundary of the disk. Since the chosen basis may have either orientation, sphOr is Milnor's orientation or its opposite, and the formal text does not decide which. The degree of the antipodal map does not depend on this (the same orientation is used on both sides), and neither do the other uses of sphOr in Chapters 5–7 (the introduction to Chapter 6 explains the one place where the convention is visible); where Milnor's orientation matters (linking numbers and the Hopf invariant), Chapter 8 uses it explicitly (V8.0 (iii)).

Milnor.Sphere.deg_antipsignAt_antip

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal statement first gives the sign of $d\alpha$ at every point and for every $n \ge 0$; the degree, for $n \ge 1$, then follows because $\alpha^{-1}(y) = \{-y\}$.
  • Milnor factors $\alpha$ into $n+1$ reflections; the formal proof reads the sign off the contracted form, using $\nu(-q) = -\nu(q)$ and $d\iota_{-q} \circ d\alpha_q = -d\iota_q$.
  • The orientation is that of V5.8 for a basis of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ chosen by the axiom of choice, and $\{\langle x,x \rangle \ge 1\}$ is the exterior of the ball, so its "outward" vector points inward.
  • It is therefore Milnor's orientation of $\mathbb S^n = \partial \mathbb D^{n+1}$ or its opposite, and the text does not decide which; the degree is unaffected, being taken between two copies of it.
33V5.10

the hairy ball theorem

narrower

to confirmFor even $n \ge 2$, every smooth tangent vector field on $S^n$ has a zero.

n > 0 assumed in the even halffield splits the coordinates in halves
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, p. 30

Following Brouwer, $\mathbb S^n$ admits a smooth field of nonzero tangent vectors if and only if $n$ is odd; a tangent field on $\mathbb S^n$ is a smooth $v : \mathbb S^n \to \mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $v(x) \cdot x = 0$. For $n$ even a nowhere-zero $v$, normalized to unit length, would give the homotopy $F(x,\theta) = x \cos\theta + v(x) \sin\theta$ from the identity to the antipodal map, which the degree forbids; for $n = 2k-1$ an explicit field pairing the coordinates in twos has no zero.

He notes in passing that for $n$ odd the antipodal map is therefore homotopic to the identity, and points to Hopf's theorem, proved in §7.

Verified edition V5.10

For $n$ even and positive, every smooth tangent vector field on $S^n$ (a smooth $v : S^n \to \mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $\langle x, v(x)\rangle = 0$) vanishes somewhere. Proof as in the book: otherwise $(x, t) \mapsto \cos(\pi t)\,x + \sin(\pi t)\,v(x)/|v(x)|$ is a smooth homotopy in $S^n$ from the identity to the antipodal map, so by V5.7 their degrees $1$ and $(-1)^{n+1} = -1$ agree. Hidden hypotheses: none beyond smoothness of $v$ (Milnor's statement is for smooth fields; the continuous case follows by approximation and is not formalized). The converse (Milnor.Sphere.exists_nonvanishing_field): for $n$ odd, write $n + 1 = 2(k + 1)$ and $x = (a, b)$ with $a, b \in \mathbb R^{k+1}$ the first and second halves of the coordinates; the field $(a, b) \mapsto (b, -a)$ is a smooth tangent field on $S^n$ without zeros; verified, so Milnor's "if and only if" is complete. (Milnor's field $(x_2, -x_1, x_4, -x_3, \dots)$ pairs adjacent coordinates instead; the two differ by a permutation of coordinates.)

Not formalized in Chapter 5: Lemma 1 in Milnor's boundary-orientation form for an abstract compact manifold with boundary (see V5.3; the boundary-orientation form for regions $\{g \ge 0\}$ is V6.1); Milnor's remarks on the degree of a constant map and on $\mathbb{RP}^n$.

Size. 3,096 lines in the thirteen files of Chapter 5: 508 for the sign of a linear map and the product orientation, 248 for oriented manifolds and V5.1, 46 for V5.2, 1,089 for V5.3 (chart functions 231, the real-analysis of the monotonicity sign 204, the transition between chart functions 254, the sign along an arc 256, the pairing 144), 177 for V5.4, 204 for V5.5–V5.7, 354 for V5.8, 394 for V5.9–V5.10, and 76 for algebraic properties of the sign added later for Chapters 7 and 8 (OrSignAlg.lean). The signed strengthening of V1.4 (about 90 lines, in Chapter 1's RegularValue.lean) and the isotopy through diffeomorphisms (in Chapter 4's Homogeneity.lean) were written for this chapter and are counted with their files. The estimate of 3,000 lines in the previous edition was accurate. Lemma 1 is the expensive item, as predicted: Milnor's "details left to the reader" are a third of the chapter.

---

Milnor.Sphere.hairy_ball

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor states the equivalence for every $n$; the formal even half assumes $n > 0$ as well, so $\mathbb S^0$, where every tangent field vanishes for trivial reasons, is not covered.
  • Milnor's field for odd $n$ pairs adjacent coordinates; the formal one splits the coordinates into two halves, $(a,b) \mapsto (b,-a)$, the two differing by a permutation of coordinates.
  • The rotation is parametrized by $t \in \mathbb R$ with angle $\pi t$, so that the homotopy is defined on $\mathbb S^n \times \mathbb R$ rather than on $\mathbb S^n \times [0,\pi]$.
  • Milnor's theorem, like the formal one, is about smooth fields; the continuous case, which follows by approximation, is not formalized.

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V5.10, converse

For $n$ even and positive, every smooth tangent vector field on $S^n$ (a smooth $v : S^n \to \mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $\langle x, v(x)\rangle = 0$) vanishes somewhere. Proof as in the book: otherwise $(x, t) \mapsto \cos(\pi t)\,x + \sin(\pi t)\,v(x)/|v(x)|$ is a smooth homotopy in $S^n$ from the identity to the antipodal map, so by V5.7 their degrees $1$ and $(-1)^{n+1} = -1$ agree. Hidden hypotheses: none beyond smoothness of $v$ (Milnor's statement is for smooth fields; the continuous case follows by approximation and is not formalized). The converse (Milnor.Sphere.exists_nonvanishing_field): for $n$ odd, write $n + 1 = 2(k + 1)$ and $x = (a, b)$ with $a, b \in \mathbb R^{k+1}$ the first and second halves of the coordinates; the field $(a, b) \mapsto (b, -a)$ is a smooth tangent field on $S^n$ without zeros; verified, so Milnor's "if and only if" is complete. (Milnor's field $(x_2, -x_1, x_4, -x_3, \dots)$ pairs adjacent coordinates instead; the two differ by a permutation of coordinates.)

Not formalized in Chapter 5: Lemma 1 in Milnor's boundary-orientation form for an abstract compact manifold with boundary (see V5.3; the boundary-orientation form for regions $\{g \ge 0\}$ is V6.1); Milnor's remarks on the degree of a constant map and on $\mathbb{RP}^n$.

Size. 3,096 lines in the thirteen files of Chapter 5: 508 for the sign of a linear map and the product orientation, 248 for oriented manifolds and V5.1, 46 for V5.2, 1,089 for V5.3 (chart functions 231, the real-analysis of the monotonicity sign 204, the transition between chart functions 254, the sign along an arc 256, the pairing 144), 177 for V5.4, 204 for V5.5–V5.7, 354 for V5.8, 394 for V5.9–V5.10, and 76 for algebraic properties of the sign added later for Chapters 7 and 8 (OrSignAlg.lean). The signed strengthening of V1.4 (about 90 lines, in Chapter 1's RegularValue.lean) and the isotopy through diffeomorphisms (in Chapter 4's Homogeneity.lean) were written for this chapter and are counted with their files. The estimate of 3,000 lines in the previous edition was accurate. Lemma 1 is the expensive item, as predicted: Milnor's "details left to the reader" are a third of the chapter.
