Milnor.Sphere.hairy_ball

Chapter 6 (Milnor §6)

35V6.0

the index

restated

to confirmThe index of an isolated zero $z$ of a smooth field on $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ is the degree

field smooth on the whole spacedegree taken on the unit sphereindex 0 at a non-isolated zero
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, p. 32

For an open $U \subseteq \mathbb R^m$ and a smooth $v : U \to \mathbb R^m$ with an isolated zero at $z$, the normalized field $\bar v = v/\|v\|$ carries a small sphere about $z$ into the unit sphere, and the degree of that map is the index $\iota$ of $v$ at $z$. Each sphere is oriented as the boundary of the corresponding disk. Which small radius is meant is not said; the independence is left to homotopy invariance of the degree.

He introduces the index through pictures (Figure 12) and through the plane fields $z^k$ and $\bar z^k$, and never states the independence of the radius, or the invariance under a homotopy without zeros on the sphere, as a lemma.

Verified edition V6.0

For a smooth vector field $v : \mathbb R^{n+1} \to \mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $v(z) = 0$ and no other zero in the closed ball of radius $r$ about $z$, and any $0 < \varepsilon \le r$, the map $u \mapsto v(z + \varepsilon u)/|v(z + \varepsilon u)|$ of $S^n$ to itself is smooth, and its degree is the index of $v$ at $z$. It does not depend on $\varepsilon$ (indexAt_eq_of_radii), it is unchanged by a smooth homotopy of $v$ with no zeros on the $\varepsilon$-sphere (indexAt_eq_of_homotopy, Milnor p. 33 bottom), and it depends only on the germ of $v$ at $z$ (index_eq_of_eventuallyEq). Deviation (form of the statement): $v$ is a field on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$. A field on an open set agrees near any compact subset with a global field (Milnor.exists_extension, a cut-off), and the index depends only on the germ, so nothing is lost; V6.5 and V6.9 use this.

Milnor.Sphere.indexAtMilnor.Sphere.indexMilnor.Sphere.IsolatedZero

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $v$ lives on an open $U \subseteq \mathbb R^m$; the formal $v$ is smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$, which loses nothing since a local field agrees with a global one near a compact set.
  • The source of the degree is the unit sphere reparametrized by $u \mapsto z + \varepsilon u$, not the sphere of radius $\varepsilon$ about $z$; the affine identification carries the orientation across.
  • Milnor orients each sphere as the boundary of its disk; the formal spheres carry the opposite convention, but the same one at source and at target, so the integer is his.
  • Independence of the radius is proved and needs $n \ge 1$; the index is defined to be $0$ when $z$ is not an isolated zero, a case Milnor excludes by hypothesis.
36V6.1

the level-set orientation in a manifold

restated

to confirmA regular level set $g^{-1}(0)$ in an oriented manifold with Euclidean charts is

$X$ presented as $\{g \ge 0\}$$f$ smooth only on an open $U$transversality assumed, not derived
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §5, Lemma 1

Let $M$ be the boundary of a compact oriented manifold $X$, oriented as the boundary of $X$. If $f : M \to N$ extends to a smooth $F : X \to N$, then $\deg(f; y) = 0$ for every regular value $y$.

This is the lemma on which every degree computation of §6 rests; there it is applied to a region with small balls removed and to $f = v/|v|$, which is defined only where $v \ne 0$.

Verified edition V6.1

V5.8 is extended from vector spaces to any oriented manifold $M$ with charts in $E$ (in particular to open subsets of $E$): for $g : M \to \mathbb R$ smooth with $0$ a regular value, $g^{-1}(0)$ is oriented by "an outward vector $w$ with $dg(w) < 0$, then a positive basis of $T g^{-1}(0)$", and this is chart-continuous. The bridge lemma between the two ways of counting signs holds in this setting (signAt_levelOrientM_eq'; for level sets in a vector space it is Milnor.signAt_levelOrient_eq'): at a point of $g^{-1}(0)$, the sign of $f|g^{-1}(0)$ for the level-set orientation is $(-1)^{n+1}$ times the sign of the pair map $(f, g)$ of V5.3, where $\dim M = n + 1$. So does Lemma 1 of §5 in boundary form: let $N$ be an oriented $n$-manifold without boundary, $M$ and $N$ Hausdorff, $f : M \to N$ smooth on an open set $U \supseteq g^{-1}(0)$, $y$ a regular value of $f|U$ satisfying the transversality condition of V2.6 at the points of $f^{-1}(y) \cap g^{-1}(0)$, and $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g \ge 0\} \cap U$ compact; then $f^{-1}(y) \cap g^{-1}(0)$ is finite and $\deg(f|g^{-1}(0); y) = 0$. Given that $y$ is regular for $f|U$, the transversality condition is equivalent to $y$ being a regular value of $f|g^{-1}(0)$; the formal statement assumes the transversality condition, and the implication from regularity of $f|g^{-1}(0)$ to it is proved separately (Milnor.exists_mfd_eq_zero_of_isRegularPoint_level), which is the direction the applications need. Why the open set: in V6.3 the map is $v/|v|$, which is smooth only where $v \ne 0$.

Milnor.levelOrientMMilnor.signAt_levelOrientM_eq'Milnor.degAt_level_eq_zero_of_opens

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $X$ is an abstract compact oriented manifold with boundary; here $X = \{g \ge 0\}$ sits inside a boundaryless $M$ and $\partial X$ is the level set $g^{-1}(0)$.
  • Compactness is required of $f^{-1}(y) \cap \{g \ge 0\} \cap U$ rather than of $X$, because the application has $f = v/|v|$, smooth only on the open set where $v \ne 0$.
  • The hypothesis at the points of $f^{-1}(y) \cap g^{-1}(0)$ is the transversality condition of V2.6; that regularity of $y$ for $f|g^{-1}(0)$ implies it is proved as a separate lemma.
  • The boundary signs are read through the ambient pair map $(f, g)$, which differs from the level-set sign by the factor $(-1)^{n+1}$.
37V6.2

the sign of a map between level sets

not in the book

to confirmIf $\Phi$ carries $G^{-1}(0)$ into $G'^{-1}(0)$ and $dG' \circ d\Phi = c\, dG$ at $q$

tool for the sign computationsreplaces Milnor's isotopies
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

The book has no such lemma. Where a degree has to be moved from one hypersurface to another, Milnor deforms the map instead: in §6 he compares the index before and after a diffeomorphism by an isotopy (Lemma 2), and inside the proof of Lemma 3 he homotopes $\bar v|\partial X$ to the Gauss map.

Verified edition V6.2

Let $\Phi : M \to M'$ be smooth along $G^{-1}(0)$ and carry $G^{-1}(0)$ into $G'^{-1}(0)$, and suppose $dG'_{\Phi(q)} \circ d\Phi_q = c \cdot dG_q$ with $c \ne 0$. Then the sign at $q$ of the restricted map $G^{-1}(0) \to G'^{-1}(0)$, for the two level-set orientations, is $\operatorname{sgn}(c)$ times the sign of $\Phi$ at $q$ for the ambient orientations. This lemma has no counterpart in the book; it is the tool that lets every degree computation of the chapter be done by transporting along explicit maps (the inclusion of a component, the affine map $u \mapsto z + \varepsilon u$, a diffeomorphism $f$, the radial map of V6.5) instead of by an isotopy argument. Its proof is the contracted-form calculus of V5.8: the pullback of the outward vector decomposes as a positive multiple of the outward vector plus a tangent vector, and the tangent part contributes nothing to the top form.

Milnor.signAt_levelOrientM_map'

What differs (the comparison)

  • No counterpart in the book: it is what lets every degree of §6 be transported along an explicit map — the inclusion of a component, the affine map $u \mapsto z + \varepsilon u$, a diffeomorphism.
  • It is used three times in the proof of the region lemma and twice in the proof of Lemma 1 of §6, where it takes the place of Milnor's Lemma 2.
  • Its proof is the contracted-form calculus of V5.8: the pullback of the outward vector is a positive multiple of the outward vector plus a tangent vector, and the tangent part drops out.
38V6.3

the region lemma

restated

to confirmFor a compact region $X = \{G \ge 0\}$ in which every zero of $v$ is one of the centres

region presented as $\{G \ge 0\}$one common radius for all ballsthe $z_i$ need not be zeros
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, p. 36

In the proof of Lemma 3 Milnor removes an $\varepsilon$-ball around each zero of $v$, obtaining a manifold with boundary on which $\bar v = v/\|v\|$ is defined. The degrees of $\bar v$ over the boundary components then add to zero; since each small sphere gets the wrong orientation, the degree over $\partial X$ equals the index sum.

He states this only as a step inside a proof, and leaves implicit the choice of a common regular value of $\bar v$ on the several boundary components.

Verified edition V6.3

Let $X = \{G \ge 0\}$ be compact with $0$ a regular value of $G : \mathbb R^{n+1} \to \mathbb R$, let the closed $\varepsilon$-balls about $z_1, \dots, z_k$ be pairwise disjoint and contained in $\{G > 0\}$, and let every zero of $v$ in $X$ be one of the $z_i$ (an inclusion: the $z_i$ are not required to be zeros). Then $$\sum_i \operatorname{index}_{z_i}(v) = \deg\big(v/|v| : \partial X \to S^n\big),$$ $\partial X = G^{-1}(0)$ carrying the orientation of V6.1 reversed (see the convention above). Proof as in the book (p. 34): apply V6.1 on the open set $\{v \ne 0\}$ to the region $X' = X \setminus \bigcup \operatorname{int} B_\varepsilon(z_i)$, presented as $\{g \ge 0\}$ with $g = G \cdot \prod_i \big(|x - z_i|^2/\varepsilon^2 - 1\big)$; the degree over $\partial X'$ vanishes, splits by V6.2 (with $\Phi$ the identity, $c$ the product of the other factors) into the degree over $\partial X$ plus the degrees over the spheres, and each sphere term is the index by V6.2 with $\Phi(u) = z_i + \varepsilon u$ ($c = 1$). Hidden hypotheses: the common regular value of $v/|v|$ on $\{v \ne 0\}$, on $\partial X$ and on the $k$ spheres exists because regular values of a map with compact domain form an open dense set (Milnor.isOpen_regularValues, a variant for oriented manifolds of Milnor.isOpen_isRegularValue of Chapter 4, used in V4.8; density is V3.3) and $S^n$ is a Baire space.

Milnor.Sphere.sum_indexAt_eq_deg

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $X$ is any compact manifold with boundary in $\mathbb R^m$; the formal $X$ is $\{G \ge 0\}$ for a globally smooth $G$ with $0$ a regular value, and $\partial X$ is $G^{-1}(0)$.
  • All the balls share one radius $\varepsilon$, disjointness is the explicit inequality $2\varepsilon < |z_i - z_j|$, and the indices are all taken at that same radius.
  • The hypothesis is only that every zero of $v$ in $X$ is among the $z_i$, so the $z_i$ themselves need not be zeros; a point where $v \ne 0$ contributes $0$ to the sum.
  • The common regular value of $v/|v|$ on $\partial X$ and on the $k$ spheres exists because regular values of a map with compact domain are open and dense and $S^n$ is a Baire space.
39V6.4

Hopf's lemma

restated

to confirmIf moreover $v$ points outward along $\partial X$, the index sum is the degree of the

region presented as $\{G \ge 0\}$outward means $dG(v) < 0$isolated zeros replaced by $k$ balls
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, Lemma 3

Let $X \subseteq \mathbb R^m$ be a compact $m$-manifold with boundary and let the Gauss map $g : \partial X \to S^{m-1}$ send a boundary point to the outward unit normal there. If a smooth vector field $v$ on $X$ has isolated zeros and points out of $X$ along the boundary, the index sum $\sum \iota$ equals the degree of $g$; in particular it does not depend on the choice of $v$.

The proof is one paragraph: remove small balls, apply Lemma 1 of §5, and note that $\bar v|\partial X$ is homotopic to $g$.

Verified edition V6.4

Under the hypotheses of V6.3, if $v$ points outward along $\partial X$ (that is, $dG(v) < 0$ on $G^{-1}(0)$), then $\sum_i \operatorname{index}_{z_i}(v)$ equals the degree of the Gauss map $x \mapsto -\nabla G(x)/|\nabla G(x)|$ of $\partial X$, a number depending only on $X$. Proof as in the book (Lemma 3, p. 35): the straight-line homotopy from $v$ to $-\nabla G$ stays away from zero on $\partial X$, reparametrized by a smooth transition function so that it is defined for all times; V5.7.

Milnor.Sphere.hopf_lemma

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $X$ is an abstract compact manifold with boundary in $\mathbb R^m$; here it is $\{G \ge 0\}$, and the outward unit normal is the explicit $-\nabla G/|\nabla G|$.
  • "Points out of $X$" becomes the inequality $dG(v) < 0$ on $G^{-1}(0)$, and "isolated zeros" becomes finitely many disjoint closed balls of one radius covering all zeros of $v$ in $X$.
  • The straight-line homotopy from $v$ to $-\nabla G$ over $\partial X$ is reparametrized by a smooth transition function so as to be defined for all times, as elsewhere in the formalization.
40V6.5

invariance of the index under diffeomorphisms

restated

to confirmThe index of an isolated zero is invariant under diffeomorphisms of open subsets of

Lemma 2 proved but not used (V6.12)fields global, matched on one ballno orientation hypothesis on $f$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, Lemma 1

Suppose the vector field $v$ on $U$ corresponds under a diffeomorphism $f : U \to U'$ to the field $v' = df \circ v \circ f^{-1}$ on $U'$. Then the index of $v$ at an isolated zero $z$ equals the index of $v'$ at $f(z)$.

He proves it from Lemma 2 (p. 33), that an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of $\mathbb R^m$ is smoothly isotopic to the identity, and settles the orientation-reversing case by conjugating with a reflection.

Verified edition V6.5

Let $f : U \to U'$ be a diffeomorphism between open subsets of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ (smooth $f$, $g$ with $g \circ f = \mathrm{id}$ on $U$ and $f \circ g = \mathrm{id}$ on $U'$), let $v$ be a smooth field with an isolated zero at $z$, the closed $r$-ball about $z$ lying in $U$, and let $w$ be a smooth field equal to $df \circ v \circ f^{-1}$ on $f(\bar B_r(z))$. Then $\operatorname{index}_{f(z)}(w) = \operatorname{index}_z(v)$. Deviation (route of proof): Milnor proves this by his Lemma 2, that every orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of $\mathbb R^m$ is smoothly isotopic to the identity (first to its derivative at a fixed point, by $F(x, t) = f(tx)/t$, then through $GL^+$ to the identity). Lemma 2 is now proved as well (V6.12), but it is not used here, and this proof does not go through it. Instead: (i) by V6.3 applied to the region $f(\bar B_r(z)) = \{G' \ge 0\}$ (with $G' = r^2 - |g(y) - z|^2$ near it, made global by a cut-off, Milnor.exists_region_fun), the index of $w$ at $f(z)$ is the degree of $w/|w|$ over $f(S_r(z))$; (ii) by V6.2 with $\Phi = f$ (here $c = -r^2$), that degree is $-\sigma$ times the degree of $u \mapsto df_u(v(u))/|\cdot|$ over $S_r(z)$, where $\sigma = \operatorname{sgn}\det df$ is constant on the ball by the intermediate value theorem (Milnor.sgn_det_fderiv_eq); (iii) the homotopy $df_{z + t(u - z)} \to df_z$ ($t$ from $1$ to $0$) and V5.7 reduce this to $u \mapsto A\,v(u)/|A\,v(u)|$ with $A = df_z$; (iv) this is $h \circ (v/|v|)$ with $h(u) = Au/|Au|$, and the sign of $h$ at every point of $S^n$ is $\operatorname{sgn}\det A = \sigma$ (Milnor.Sphere.signAt_nv0_linear); so the two factors $\sigma$ cancel. Step (iv) is where Lemma 2 is replaced: $h$ is the restriction to $S^n$ of $\Theta(x) = (|x|/|Ax|)\,Ax$, which preserves $|x|^2 - 1$, so by V6.2 the sign of $h$ is $\operatorname{sgn}\det d\Theta$, and $d\Theta_u = (\lambda\,\mathrm{id} + (d\lambda_u \circ A^{-1}) \otimes Au) \circ A$ with $\lambda = |x|/|Ax|$ homogeneous of degree $0$, whose determinant is $\lambda^{n+1}\det A$ by the matrix determinant lemma (Milnor.det_id_add_smulRight).

Milnor.Sphere.index_eq_of_diffeo

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's route is his Lemma 2, that an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of $\mathbb R^m$ is smoothly isotopic to the identity; the formal proof does not take it. Lemma 2 is itself proved, in V6.12, and nothing in the development depends on it.
  • In its place the index of $w$ at $f(z)$ is a boundary degree over $f(S_r(z))$, transported by V6.2 with $c = -r^2$, which produces the factor $-\operatorname{sgn}\det df$, constant on the ball.
  • A second factor $\operatorname{sgn}\det df_z$ is the sign of $u \mapsto Au/|Au|$ on $S^n$ for $A = df_z$, by the matrix determinant lemma; the two cancel, so $f$ need not preserve orientation.
  • The correspondence $w = df \circ v \circ f^{-1}$ is required only on $f(\bar B_r(z))$, and both fields are smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ rather than on $U$ and $U'$.
41V6.6

the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood theorem

restated

to confirmA compact nonempty submanifold $N$ of $\mathbb R^k$ has an $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood

an exercise in the book, proved hereno normal bundle in the statement$r$ defined on all of $\mathbb R^k$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problems 11–12

Problem 11 asks the reader to show that the normal bundle space of a compact boundaryless $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ is a smooth manifold and that $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ carries the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood of $M \times 0$ in it diffeomorphically onto the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood $N_\varepsilon$ of $M$ in $\mathbb R^k$. Problem 12 asks for the retraction $r : N_\varepsilon \to M$ with $r(x + v) = x$, and for the fact that $x + v$ is closer to $x$ than to any other point of $M$.

In §6 (p. 38) he uses the theorem without proof: for $x \in N_\varepsilon$ he takes $r(x)$ to be the closest point of $M$, notes that $x - r(x)$ must be perpendicular to the tangent space at $r(x)$, and asserts that $r$ is smooth and well defined for $\varepsilon$ small.

Verified edition V6.6

Let $N \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ be a compact submanifold (given by slice charts with model $\mathbb R^n$, as in V2.2). There are $\varepsilon > 0$ and a map $r : \mathbb R^k \to N$ with $|x - r(x)| = \operatorname{dist}(x, N)$ for every $x$ (a nearest point, chosen for every $x$), such that every $x$ with $\operatorname{dist}(x, N) < \varepsilon$ has $r(x)$ as its unique nearest point in $N$; the map $r$ is smooth on this neighbourhood, is the identity on $N$, satisfies $x - r(x) \perp T_{r(x)}N$ there, and $dr_x$ takes values in $T_{r(x)}N$; and for $x \in N$ and $v \perp T_xN$ one has $r(x + sv) = x$ for all sufficiently small $s$ (this last conclusion is used in Chapter 7, for the Pontryagin framing and the framed cobordism of a homotopy). This is the theorem Milnor sets as Problems 11 and 12 of §8 (p. 52) and quotes in the proof of Poincaré–Hopf; it is proved here directly. Route of proof (different from Milnor's normal-bundle argument): in a local parametrization $\varphi$ of $N$, a nearest point $\varphi(z)$ of $x$ satisfies the nearest-point equation $(d\varphi_z)^{}(x - \varphi(z)) = 0$ (Fermat's condition, Milnor.Tub.Fn_eq_zero_of_nearest); the map $(z, x) \mapsto ((d\varphi_z)^(x - \varphi(z)), x)$ has invertible derivative at $(z_0, \varphi(z_0))$, so by the inverse function theorem the equation has, for $x$ near $\varphi(z_0)$, a unique solution $z = U(x)$ near $z_0$, smooth in $x$ (Milnor.Tub.exists_contDiffOn_U, which also shows that invertibility of the derivative is an open condition); a compactness argument on sequences shows that all nearest points of all $x$ close enough to $N$ are captured by these local solutions, which gives uniqueness and smoothness of $r$. Hidden hypotheses: $N$ nonempty; the slice charts of $N$ have Euclidean model and complement, so that adjoints make sense.

Milnor.Tub.tubular

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor leaves this to the reader in §8 and quotes it in §6; it is proved here from the inverse function theorem applied to the nearest-point equation $(d\varphi_z)^*(x - \varphi(z)) = 0$.
  • The formal statement says nothing about the normal bundle space, about $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ being a diffeomorphism, or about $N_\varepsilon$ being a manifold with boundary, which Problem 11 asks.
  • It gives two things the book does not state: $r$ is defined on all of $\mathbb R^k$ and always realizes $\operatorname{dist}(x, N)$, and $dr_x$ maps into $T_{r(x)}N$ on the neighbourhood.
  • Hidden hypotheses: $N$ is nonempty, and its slice charts have Euclidean model and complement so that the adjoint $(d\varphi_z)^*$ is available.
42V6.7

the region $N_{\varepsilon/2}$ and the extended field

restated

to confirm$N_{\varepsilon/2}$ is a compact region with smooth boundary, and

region at radius $\varepsilon/2$$G$ made global by a cut-offtangency of $v$ an explicit hypothesis
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, p. 39

With $\varphi(x) = \|x - r(x)\|^2$, whose gradient is $2(x - r(x))$, the boundary $\partial N_\varepsilon = \varphi^{-1}(\varepsilon^2)$ has outward unit normal $g(x) = (x - r(x))/\varepsilon$. A vector field $v$ on $M$ is extended over $N_\varepsilon$ by $w(x) = (x - r(x)) + v(r(x))$; then $w \cdot g = \varepsilon > 0$ along the boundary, so $w$ points outward, and $w$ vanishes only at the zeros of $v$, the two summands being orthogonal.

The gradient computation is called easy and the statement about the zeros clear; both rest on the orthogonality supplied by the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood theorem.

Verified edition V6.7

With $r$ and $\varepsilon$ as in V6.6, $X = \{\operatorname{dist}(\cdot, N) \le \varepsilon/2\}$ is compact, equals $\{G \ge 0\}$ for a smooth $G$ with $G = (\varepsilon/2)^2 - |x - r(x)|^2$ near $X$, and $0$ is a regular value of $G$. For a smooth tangent field $v$ on $N$, the field $w(x) = v(r(x)) + (x - r(x))$ is smooth near $X$, its zeros in $X$ are exactly the zeros of $v$, and it points outward along $\partial X$ ($dG(w) = -2|x - r(x)|^2 < 0$ there). Proof as in the book (p. 38): the two summands of $w$ are orthogonal by V6.6.

Milnor.Tub.exists_GMilnor.Tub.isRegularValue_GMilnor.Tub.w₀_eq_zeroMilnor.Tub.fderiv_G₀_w₀

What differs (the comparison)

  • The region is taken at radius $\varepsilon/2$, so that it lies well inside the set where $r$ is smooth, and is presented as $\{G \ge 0\}$ rather than as $\varphi \le \varepsilon^2$.
  • $G$ is only locally the formula $(\varepsilon/2)^2 - |x - r(x)|^2$; a cut-off makes it smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$, which is what the region lemma requires of it.
  • Outwardness is recorded as $dG(w) = -2|x - r(x)|^2 < 0$ rather than as $w \cdot g = \varepsilon$; the two say the same thing up to a positive factor.
  • That $v$ takes its values in the tangent spaces of $N$ is an explicit hypothesis of the statements about the zeros and about outwardness; for Milnor it is part of being a vector field on $M$.
43V6.8

the index of a tangent field on a submanifold

restated

to confirmThe index of a tangent field on a compact $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ at a point is

definition and theorem exchangedchart definition is V6.14depends on the retraction $r$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, p. 33

Granting Lemma 1, Milnor defines the index of a vector field $w$ on an arbitrary manifold $M$ at a zero $z$: take a parametrization $h : U \to M$ of a neighbourhood of $z$ and let $\iota$ be the index of the corresponding field $dh^{-1} \circ w \circ h$ at $h^{-1}(z)$. Lemma 1 is exactly what makes this independent of the parametrization.

On p. 39 he observes that for $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ this index also equals the index of the extended field $(x - r(x)) + v(r(x))$ at $z$, by the derivative computation of Lemma 5 (p. 37).

Verified edition V6.8

For a compact $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ and a smooth tangent field $v$ on $N$, the index of $v$ at $q \in N$ is defined as the index at $q$ of (a global smooth extension of) the field $w$ of V6.7; by V6.0 it depends only on the germ of $w$ at $q$, hence only on $v$, $N$ and the nearest-point retraction, which is canonical. Deviation (definition): Milnor defines the index on an abstract manifold through a chart, uses his Lemma 1 for well-definedness, and then remarks on p. 38 that this index equals the index of $w$. Here the second quantity is the definition. The chart definition is formalized too, and the two are proved equal at a non-degenerate zero (V6.14); the agreement at an arbitrary isolated zero is not proved, and what it would need is recorded there and in §11. For the theorem below this makes no difference, since its content is the independence of the index sum from $v$.

Milnor.Tub.indexNMilnor.Tub.indexN_eq

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's definition goes through a parametrization and needs Lemma 1 of §6 to be well posed; the formal definition is his p. 39 identity instead, so no invariance lemma is used.
  • Consequently the chart definition is a development of its own, V6.14, where it is formalized together with Milnor's Lemma 5 (V6.13) and shown to agree with this one at a non-degenerate zero; the agreement at an arbitrary isolated zero is not proved.
  • The definition refers to the nearest-point retraction $r$, which is canonical once $N$ is given, and to an arbitrary global smooth extension, which the germ property makes harmless.
44V6.9

the Poincaré–Hopf theorem

restated

to confirmFor a compact nonempty submanifold $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, $K \ge 1$, there is an

degenerate zeros allowed$\chi(N)$ only asserted to existEuler characteristic not formalized
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, Theorem 1

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ be a compact boundaryless manifold and $N_\varepsilon$ its closed $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood, a smooth manifold with boundary for $\varepsilon$ small. For any vector field $v$ on $M$ with only nondegenerate zeros, the index sum $\sum \iota$ equals the degree of the Gauss map $g : \partial N_\varepsilon \to S^{k-1}$; in particular it does not depend on the choice of $v$.

The theorem announced on p. 35 is stronger: for a compact $M$ and a field with isolated zeros the sum is the Euler number $\chi(M) = \sum_i (-1)^i \operatorname{rank} H_i(M)$, and p. 40 sketches the three further steps that would establish it.

Verified edition V6.9

Let $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, $K \ge 1$, be a compact submanifold. There is an integer $\chi(N)$ such that, for every smooth tangent vector field $v$ on $N$ whose zeros are finitely many points $z_1, \dots, z_j$, $$\sum_i \operatorname{index}_{z_i}(v) = \chi(N).$$ In the proof $\chi(N)$ is the degree of the Gauss map of $\partial N_{\varepsilon/2}$. Deviation (stronger): Milnor's Theorem 1 (p. 38) is stated for fields with nondegenerate zeros only; the formal statement asks for finitely many zeros, of any kind. The price is the definition of the index used here (V6.8, the index of the extended field); Milnor's Lemma 5 (p. 37), which identifies that index with the intrinsic one at a nondegenerate zero, is V6.13 and V6.14. Proof as in the book (p. 38): V6.7 puts $w$ in the situation of V6.4, with small balls about the $z_i$; the right-hand side of V6.4 does not involve $v$. Since the index is defined through the extended field (V6.8), the proof does not use V6.5. Hidden hypotheses: $N$ nonempty (as in V6.6; for empty $N$ the statement is trivial); "isolated zeros" is taken as "finitely many zeros", which on a compact $N$ is the same thing; the ambient dimension is at least $2$; the zeros are indexed injectively. Not formalized: the identification of $\chi(N)$ with the Euler characteristic (Milnor refers to Hopf's theorem and to the literature; no Euler characteristic exists in Mathlib), and Poincaré–Hopf for a manifold not given as a submanifold of Euclidean space (Whitney's embedding theorem is not formalized; Milnor also works with $N \subseteq \mathbb R^k$).

Milnor.Tub.poincare_hopf

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's Theorem 1 assumes all zeros nondegenerate; the formal statement assumes only that there are finitely many, which on a compact $N$ is the same as isolated.
  • That gain is bought by defining the index through the extended field (V6.8). Milnor's Lemma 5 (p. 37), which identifies that index with the one computed in a chart at a non-degenerate zero, is proved separately, in V6.13 and V6.14, and is not used here.
  • The Gauss-map degree appears only inside the proof, over $\partial N_{\varepsilon/2}$ rather than $\partial N_\varepsilon$, and the statement quantifies the invariant existentially.
  • Not formalized: the identification of $\chi(N)$ with the Euler number of p. 35 (G.1), and the theorem for a manifold not presented as a submanifold of Euclidean space. The value of $\chi(N)$ for the sphere is computed in V6.15.
85V6.10

Lemma 4, the index of a non-degenerate zero

hypotheses explicit

to confirmFor $n \ge 1$, if a smooth field $v$ on $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ has $v(z) = 0$ with $dv_z$

$n \ge 1$, the index being a degreefield smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$isolatedness proved, not assumed
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, Lemma 4

A zero $z$ of $v$ is non-degenerate when $dv_z$ is non-singular, and such a zero is isolated, by the inverse function theorem. Lemma 4: the index of $v$ at a non-degenerate zero is $+1$ or $-1$ according as the determinant of $dv_z$ is positive or negative, that is $\operatorname{sign}\det dv_z$. Milnor shrinks $U$ to a ball on which $v$ is a diffeomorphism, applies the construction of Lemma 2 to $v$ itself to replace $v$ by $dv_z$, connects $dv_z$ to the identity inside $GL^+(m)$ when the determinant is positive, and conjugates with a reflection when it is negative.

This is what makes an index computable; with Lemma 5 it is how every index of the section is evaluated.

Verified edition V6.10

Let $n \ge 1$ and let $v$ be a smooth field on $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $v(z) = 0$ and $dv_z$ bijective. Then $$\operatorname{index}_z(v) = \operatorname{sgn}\det dv_z,$$ so the index is $+1$ or $-1$ according as $\det dv_z$ is positive or negative, which is Milnor's Lemma 4 of §6 (p. 36, index_nondegenerate in his own form). Hidden hypothesis: $n \ge 1$, as everywhere in this chapter, the index being a degree on $S^n$. No isolatedness of the zero is assumed: it is proved. Proof as in the book, with two steps Milnor leaves implicit. First, one uniform estimate does two jobs at once: writing $A = dv_z$, a bijective linear map is bounded below, $\|Ay\| \ge c\|y\|$ (exists_lower_bound_of_bijective), and $v(x) = A(x - z) + o(|x - z|)$, so there is a radius $r > 0$ on which no combination $A(x - z) + b\,(v(x) - A(x - z))$ with $0 \le b \le 1$ vanishes off $z$ (exists_radius_of_nondegenerate); the case $b = 1$ says that $z$ is an isolated zero, and the whole range says that the straight-line homotopy from $v$ to its linear part keeps the $r$-sphere free of zeros, so V6.0 replaces $v$ by $x \mapsto A(x - z)$. Secondly, the sphere map of the linear field is $u \mapsto Au/|Au|$, whose sign at every point is $\operatorname{sgn}\det A$ (V6.5's signAt_nv0_linear, which is what stands in this development for Milnor's appeal to Lemma 2); the passage from a constant pointwise sign to a degree is deg_nv0_linear, new here: the map is a bijection of $S^n$, so every point is a regular value with exactly one preimage.

Milnor.Sphere.index_eq_sgn_detMilnor.Sphere.index_nondegenerateMilnor.Sphere.deg_nv0_linear

What differs (the comparison)

  • The dimension condition $n \ge 1$ is named, as everywhere in §6: the index is a degree on $S^n$, so the ambient space has dimension at least two. Milnor's index needs $S^{m-1}$ connected for the same reason, and he does not say so.
  • As in V6.0 the field is smooth on all of $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ rather than on an open $U$; the index depends only on the germ at $z$, and a field on an open set agrees near a compact subset with a global one, so nothing is lost.
  • Milnor deduces the lemma from his Lemma 2 (V6.12), through the isotopy $v(tx)/t$ and the connectedness of $GL^+$; the formal proof is a straight-line homotopy to the linear part, and the single estimate above makes the zero isolated and the homotopy zero-free at once.
  • The step from a constant pointwise sign to a degree is a new lemma: the sphere map of a bijective linear field is a bijection of $S^n$, so its degree is that one sign. Milnor reads the degree off the identity map and a reflection instead.
  • His worked examples at the start of §6 — the source, the sink, the saddle, the centre — are linear fields and so are instances of this formula; the complex examples $v(z) = z^k$ of index $k$ and $v(z) = \bar z^{\,k}$ of index $-k$ have no formal counterpart.
86V6.11

Hadamard's lemma, and smoothness of a parametric integral

stronger

to confirmAn integral over a fixed interval depends smoothly on a parameter: for $h$ of class

the integral theorem is not in Mathlibcoordinate-free, any normed spacestarget complete, not $\mathbb R^m$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, p. 33

Inside the proof of Lemma 2 Milnor needs the decomposition $f(x) = \sum_i x_i\,g_i(x)$ of a smooth $f$ with $f(0) = 0$, with the $g_i$ smooth and $g_i(0) = \partial f/\partial x_i(0)$. It comes from $f(x) = \int_0^1 \frac{d}{dt} f(tx)\,dt$, and the smoothness of $g_i(x) = \int_0^1 \partial_i f(tx)\,dt$ is settled by the remark that one may differentiate under the integral sign. With it his isotopy $F(x, t) = \sum_i x_i\,g_i(tx)$, which is $f(tx)/t$ for $t > 0$ and $df_0$ at $t = 0$, is smooth up to $t = 0$.

The whole difficulty of his isotopy is that smoothness at $t = 0$, and the decomposition is what supplies it.

Verified edition V6.11

Milnor's isotopy of Lemma 2 of §6 is $F(x, t) = f(tx)/t$, and the whole difficulty is that it is smooth at $t = 0$. Let $E$ and $F$ be real normed spaces, $F$ complete. Verified: (i) if $h : E \times \mathbb R \to F$ is $C^n$ (resp. $C^\infty$) then so is $x \mapsto \int_0^1 h(x, t)\,dt$ — Mathlib has no such theorem, and it is proved here by induction on $n$, differentiating under the integral sign and dominating on a tube around the compact segment $\{x\} \times [0,1]$, so that neither finite dimension nor local compactness is needed; (ii) Hadamard's lemma: a smooth $f$ with $f(0) = 0$ factors as $f(x) = L(x)\,x$ with $L(x) = \int_0^1 df_{tx}\,dt$ smooth and $L(0) = df_0$; (iii) consequently $t^{-1} f(tx)$ extends to a smooth map of $E \times \mathbb R$ with value $df_0(x)$ at $t = 0$, both as an existence statement and for the explicit if t = 0 then … else … formula in which the isotopy is written. Hidden hypothesis: completeness of the target $F$, so that the Bochner integrals exist — the one hypothesis Milnor would not state, his spaces being $\mathbb R^m$. Deviation (stronger): stated for smooth maps between real normed spaces, not for $\mathbb R^m$.

Milnor.contDiff_intervalIntegralMilnor.contDiff_intervalIntegral_inftyMilnor.exists_hadamardMilnor.exists_contDiff_extensionMilnor.contDiff_ite_smul_inv

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor works in $\mathbb R^m$ and writes the decomposition in coordinates; the formal statement is coordinate-free, $f(x) = L(x)\,x$ with $L$ a smooth family of continuous linear maps between arbitrary real normed spaces.
  • The one hypothesis he would not state is completeness of the target, needed for the Bochner integrals to exist; his spaces being $\mathbb R^m$, it is automatic.
  • His "differentiate under the integral sign" is a theorem Mathlib does not have: that an integral over a fixed interval is $C^n$ in a parameter when the integrand is. It is proved here by induction on $n$, the domination coming from compactness of the segment, and it needs neither finite dimension nor local compactness.
  • The consequence is stated in the form the isotopy needs, including the explicit formula with a case split at $t = 0$, so that no smoothness has to be rechecked when the isotopy of V6.12 is assembled.
87V6.12

Lemma 2 of §6

stronger

to confirmEvery diffeomorphism of a finite-dimensional real normed space whose Jacobian

any finite-dimensional space, any $m$determinant positive at one pointevery real time slice a diffeomorphism
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, Lemma 2

Any orientation-preserving diffeomorphism $f$ of $\mathbb R^m$ is smoothly isotopic to the identity. Milnor composes with a translation so that $f(0) = 0$, uses the decomposition of p. 33 to see that $F(x, t) = f(tx)/t$, with $F(x, 0) = df_0$, is a smooth isotopy from $df_0$ to $f$, and then joins $df_0$ to the identity through linear isomorphisms, the matrices of positive determinant being path connected. He uses the lemma to prove Lemma 1, that the index is unchanged by a diffeomorphism.

The connectedness of $GL^+(m)$ is asserted rather than proved, here and again in §7 (V7.3).

Verified edition V6.12

Every orientation-preserving diffeomorphism $f$ of $\mathbb R^m$ — and more generally of a finite-dimensional real normed space $E$ — is smoothly isotopic to the identity (Milnor's Lemma 2 of §6, p. 33). A diffeomorphism is given unbundled, as a smooth map with a smooth two-sided inverse, as in V6.5; an isotopy is a smooth $H : E \times \mathbb R \to E$ with $H(\cdot, 0) = f$, $H(\cdot, 1) = \mathrm{id}$ and every time slice a diffeomorphism, the shape already used in V4.4; "orientation-preserving" is positivity of the Jacobian determinant at one point, equivalently at every point. Deviation (stronger): stated for any finite-dimensional real normed space, with positivity of the dimension not needed. Proof as in the book, in three steps: $f$ is isotopic to $x \mapsto f(x) - f(0)$ by translation; a diffeomorphism fixing the origin is isotopic to its derivative there by $F(x, t) = f(tx)/t$, which is smooth by V6.11; and a linear automorphism of positive determinant is isotopic to the identity along the $GL^+$ path of V7.3. Three things Milnor passes over: transitivity of isotopy, which needs the two isotopies made constant near the join before gluing; that the slices at negative times are diffeomorphisms too, since the definition asks for every real time; and invertibility of the derivative, which comes from the chain rule applied to the two-sided inverse. Placement: the Lean file is Milnor/Chapter7/Isotopy.lean, because its last step is the $GL^+$ path of Chapter 7 and Chapter 6 may not import Chapter 7. Nothing in the development depends on this lemma: §6 replaced Milnor's appeal to it by the sign computation of V6.5, and it is formalized only because it is a theorem of the book.

Milnor.lemma2_of_sec6Milnor.smoothIsotopicMap_idMilnor.SmoothIsotopicMapMilnor.IsDiffeoSelf

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $\mathbb R^m$ becomes any finite-dimensional real normed space, and positivity of the dimension is not needed; his own statement is recorded as the case $E = \mathbb R^m$.
  • "Orientation-preserving" is positivity of $\det df$ at a single point, the weakest of the equivalent forms, and that the sign is the same at every point is part of the development rather than an observation.
  • Isotopy is the project's notion (V4.4): every slice, at every real time, is a diffeomorphism, where Milnor's $F$ lives on $\mathbb R^m \times [0, 1]$. Three things he passes over: transitivity of isotopy, which needs the two isotopies made constant near the join before gluing; that the slices at negative times are diffeomorphisms too; and invertibility of $df_0$, which comes from the chain rule on the two-sided inverse.
  • The Lean file sits in Chapter 7, because its last step is the $GL^+$ path proved there (V7.3) and Chapter 6 may not import Chapter 7.
  • Nothing in the development depends on this lemma: §6 replaced Milnor's appeal to it in Lemma 1 by a direct computation of signs (V6.5), so it is formalized only because it is a theorem of the book.
88V6.13

Lemma 5, and the derivative of the retraction

hypotheses explicit

to confirmFor a compact nonempty submanifold $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, the derivative at a

$dr_q$ is the orthogonal projectiontwo readings of the derivative$N$ compact, for the retraction
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, Lemma 5

Regard a field $w$ on $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ as a map $M \to \mathbb R^k$, so that $dw_z : TM_z \to \mathbb R^k$ is defined. Lemma 5: at a zero $z$ of $w$ the map $dw_z$ carries $TM_z$ into $TM_z$, and if the resulting endomorphism has determinant $D \ne 0$ then $z$ is an isolated zero of index $\operatorname{sign} D$. He proves it with a parametrization $h$, $h(u_0) = z$: writing $w(h(u)) = \sum_j v_j(u)\,t_j(u)$ in the basis $t_i = \partial h/\partial u_i$ of the tangent spaces and differentiating in $u_i$, the terms with $v_j$ drop out at $u_0$, so the matrix of $dw_z$ in that basis is the Jacobian of $v$ at $u_0$. On p. 39 he applies the lemma to the extended field $w = v \circ r + (\mathrm{id} - r)$ on the tubular neighbourhood, whose derivative is block diagonal with blocks $dv_z|_{TM_z}$ and the identity.

It is the bridge between the index on the manifold and the index of the extended field, and the place where the derivative of the retraction is used without comment.

Verified edition V6.13

Let $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty submanifold of dimension $n$ and $r$ the nearest-point retraction of V6.6. (i) At $q \in N$ the derivative $dr_q$ is the orthogonal projection of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$ onto $T_qN$: it is the identity on $T_qN$, its image is $T_qN$, it is idempotent, and its kernel is exactly $(T_qN)^\perp$. (ii) Milnor's Lemma 5 (p. 37): if $v$ is a smooth tangent field on $N$ with $v(q) = 0$, then the derivative of $v$ at $q$ carries $T_qN$ into itself, both in the ambient reading (the derivative at $q$ of $x \mapsto v(r(x))$) and in the intrinsic one (the derivative of $v : N \to \mathbb R^{K+1}$ on the image of the derivative of the inclusion). (iii) Hence, at a zero of $v$, the derivative of the extended field $w = v \circ r + (\mathrm{id} - r)$ of V6.7 is $dv_q \circ dr_q + (\mathrm{id} - dr_q)$, and in the splitting $\mathbb R^{K+1} = T_qN \oplus (T_qN)^\perp$ it is $dv_q$ on the tangent part and the identity on the normal part, so $\det dw_q$ is the determinant of the restriction of $dv_q$ to $T_qN$, and $dw_q$ is invertible exactly when that restriction is. Hidden hypotheses: $N$ compact and nonempty, because the retraction of V6.6 is available only then; Milnor's Lemma 5 itself needs no compactness. The hypothesis $v(q) = 0$ is essential in (ii) and is Milnor's: without it the derivative of $v$ at $q$ has no invariant meaning as an endomorphism of $T_qN$. Deviation (route of proof): Milnor's own proof of Lemma 5 differentiates the field written in a coefficient basis, which presumes the smoothness of the coefficients; here the identity $(\mathrm{id} - dr)(v \circ r) = 0$ is differentiated instead, which avoids the point. What Milnor leaves implicit: what "the derivative of a field on a submanifold" means at all; the two readings above, and their agreement, are stated as theorems.

Milnor.Tub.dretMilnor.Tub.dret_apply_of_mem_tanNMilnor.Tub.ker_dretMilnor.Tub.dvr_mem_tanNMilnor.Tub.mfd_mem_tanNMilnor.Tub.det_fderiv_w₀Milnor.Tub.bijective_fderiv_w₀_iff

What differs (the comparison)

  • Compactness and nonemptiness of $N$ are assumed, because the tubular neighbourhood and the retraction $r$ of V6.6 exist here only for a compact submanifold; Milnor's Lemma 5 itself needs no compactness.
  • What "the derivative of a field on a submanifold" means is not left to the reader: the ambient reading, the derivative of $v \circ r$, and the intrinsic one, the derivative of $v$ as a map of manifolds, are both defined and shown to agree on the tangent space.
  • The derivative of the retraction, which Milnor uses without comment on p. 39, is proved to be the orthogonal projection onto the tangent space; the block-diagonal form of $dw_q$ and the determinant identity follow from that.
  • His proof differentiates the field written in a coefficient basis, which presumes the coefficients smooth; the formal proof differentiates the identity $(\mathrm{id} - dr)(v \circ r) = 0$ instead and avoids the point.
  • The hypothesis $v(q) = 0$ is Milnor's and is essential: without it the derivative of $v$ at $q$ has no invariant meaning as an endomorphism of $T_qN$. The index clause of Lemma 5 is V6.14.
89V6.14

the chart definition of the index, and its agreement with V6.8

narrower

to confirmFor a compact nonempty $N \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ of dimension $n \ge 2$, Milnor's

agreement only at non-degenerate zeros$K \ge 1$ ambient, $n \ge 2$ in a chart$N$ compact, for the retraction
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, p. 33

Milnor defines the index of a zero of a field on a manifold through a parametrization (p. 33): a local parametrization $\psi : \Omega \to M$, with $\Omega \subseteq \mathbb R^n$ open and $\psi(u_0) = z$, pulls the field back to a field on $\Omega$, and the index at $z$ is the index of the pull-back at $u_0$; Lemma 1 (V6.5) is what makes the value independent of the parametrization. On p. 39 he identifies that index with the index of the extended field $(x - r(x)) + v(r(x))$ at $z$, by the derivative computation of Lemma 5 (p. 37).

The identification is what lets him prove the Poincaré–Hopf theorem on the tubular neighbourhood and read the answer on the manifold.

Verified edition V6.14

Milnor defines the index of a zero of a field on a manifold through a parametrization (p. 33): a local parametrization $\psi : \Omega \to N$, $\Omega \subseteq \mathbb R^n$ open, $\psi(u_0) = q$, pulls $v$ back to a field on $\Omega$, and the index of $v$ at $q$ is the index of the pull-back at $u_0$. Verified: (i) the pull-back through a slice-chart parametrization is smooth, and its index at the parameter point is chartIndexN, Milnor's index; (ii) it depends neither on the parametrization nor on the extension — for any smooth local parametrization $\psi$ of $N$ at $q$ with a smooth ambient left inverse $\chi$ and with $d\psi_u$ spanning the tangent space, and any globally smooth field on $\mathbb R^n$ agreeing near $u_0$ with the pull-back and having an isolated zero there, the index is the same number (this is Milnor's Lemma 1, V6.5, applied to the transition $\chi_q \circ \psi$); (iii) at a non-degenerate zero both definitions are the sign of the same determinant — the index of the extended field is $\operatorname{sgn}\det(dv_q|T_qN)$ by V6.13(iii) and V6.10, and so is the chart index, because $d\varphi_q$ conjugates the derivative of the pulled-back field into that restriction — hence the chart definition agrees with V6.8 at every non-degenerate zero (Milnor, pp. 33 and 37). Hidden hypotheses: the dimension conditions, and they are two, because both indices are degrees on spheres of different dimensions: the ambient index needs $K \ge 1$ and the chart index needs $n \ge 2$ (the index of a field on $\mathbb R^n$ is a degree on $S^{n-1}$). Also $N$ compact and nonempty, as in V6.13. Not formalized: the agreement at an arbitrary isolated zero. It is not a matter of effort: the two numbers are degrees of maps of spheres of different dimensions, $S^K$ and $S^{n-1}$, so no invariance-under-diffeomorphism argument compares them, and one would first need a suspension formula — that the index of a field of the shape $(u, y) \mapsto (\tilde v(u), y)$ on $\mathbb R^n \times \mathbb R^{K+1-n}$ is the index of $\tilde v$ — which this development does not have and which Milnor does not prove either.

Milnor.Tub.chartFieldMilnor.Tub.chartIndexNMilnor.Tub.index_eq_chartIndexNMilnor.Tub.indexN_eq_sgn_detMilnor.Tub.chartIndexN_eq_sgn_detMilnor.Tub.chartIndexN_eq_indexN

What differs (the comparison)

  • The agreement of the two definitions is proved only at a non-degenerate zero, which is what Lemma 5 gives and what the book uses. At an arbitrary isolated zero the two are degrees of maps of spheres of different dimensions, $S^K$ and $S^{n-1}$, so no appeal to invariance under diffeomorphisms compares them: one would first need a suspension formula, that the index of a field of the shape $(u, y) \mapsto (\tilde v(u), y)$ is the index of $\tilde v$. The project does not have it, and Milnor does not prove it either.
  • Two dimension conditions are carried where Milnor states none, and they are not the same one: $K \ge 1$ for the ambient index, $n \ge 2$ for the chart index, each because a degree is taken on a sphere that has to be connected.
  • Compactness and nonemptiness of $N$ come from the tubular neighbourhood, as in V6.13, and the chart index is defined from the slice-chart parametrizations of Chapter 2 rather than from a parametrization chosen by hand.
  • Independence is proved in the strong form: any smooth local parametrization with a smooth ambient left inverse and spanning derivative, and any global smooth extension of the pull-back with an isolated zero, give the same number. Milnor fixes one parametrization and appeals to Lemma 1.
  • Definition and theorem are exchanged, as V6.8 records: the extended-field index is the definition here and the chart index the theorem, so the Poincaré–Hopf theorem of V6.9 does not rest on this row.
90V6.15

the Euler number of $S^n$

restated

to confirmFor $n \ge 1$, every smooth tangent field on $S^n$ with finitely many zeros has index

index sum, not the Euler characteristicevery field with finitely many zerosthe odd case computed twice
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §6, Example

On $S^n$ the field $v(x) = p - (p \cdot x)\,x$, with $p$ the north pole, is tangent and vanishes exactly at $\pm p$; near the south pole it is the identity in the chart given by projection to $p^\perp$, of index $+1$, and near the north pole minus the identity, of index $\operatorname{sign}\det(-I_n) = (-1)^n$. So the Euler number of the sphere is $\chi(S^n) = 1 + (-1)^n$, which is $2$ for $n$ even and $0$ for $n$ odd. He observes that this reproves the hairy ball theorem, a field without zeros having index sum $0 \ne 2$, and that for odd $n$ the value also follows from replacing $v$ by $-v$.

$\chi$ is his name (p. 35) for the common value of the index sum; that it is the Euler characteristic of topology is the step he refers to Hopf and to the literature for.

Verified edition V6.15

For $n \ge 1$ and every smooth tangent field $v$ on $S^n$ whose zeros are finitely many distinct points $z_1, \dots, z_j$, $$\sum_i \operatorname{index}_{z_i}(v) = 1 + (-1)^n,$$ which is $2$ for $n$ even and $0$ for $n$ odd. This is Milnor's example on p. 39 (with pp. 32–33), and the integer is pinned down, not merely bounded: any integer with the property V6.9 asserts of $\chi(N)$ equals $1 + (-1)^n$ (eq_of_indexSum_sph). Proof as in the book: the field is Milnor's, $v(x) = e - \langle e, x\rangle x$, the gradient on $S^n$ of the height function, whose zeros are exactly $e$ and $-e$; by V6.13 the derivative on the tangent space is minus the identity at $e$, of determinant $(-1)^n$, and the identity at $-e$, of determinant $1$, so V6.10 and V6.14 give the indices $(-1)^n$ and $1$, and V6.9 transfers their sum to every field. A second, independent computation of the odd case: for $n$ odd the sphere carries a nowhere-zero field (V5.10, converse), so the index sum is an empty sum and the integer is $0$; a third theorem derives $1 + (-1)^n = 0$ from both computations, as it must. Hidden hypotheses: $n \ge 1$, from V6.9; tangency is the condition $\langle q, v(q)\rangle = 0$, which for the sphere is what V6.9 asks; the zeros are indexed injectively, as in V6.9. What had to be added: $S^n$ presented again as a slice submanifold, because §6's tubular machinery is written for slice charts whose complement model is a Euclidean space $\mathbb R^m$, whereas §5 presents $S^n$ as the level set of a real-valued function and so with complement model the line itself; the two are isomorphic but not the same model, so the presentation had to be redone with complement model $\mathbb R^1$, together with the tangent space at $q$ as $q^\perp$ for that structure and the derivative through the retraction of a field coming from the ambient space. For the same reason Milnor's odd-dimensional field of §5 had to be rebuilt here as an ambient linear map, some forty duplicated lines: the project has no lemma comparing two slice-submanifold structures on the same set, and a general "two slice structures, same smooth maps" lemma would remove the duplication. Not formalized: that this integer is the Euler characteristic of $S^n$. This is not a gap in effort but in vocabulary: Mathlib has no Euler characteristic of a space, no Betti numbers and no cellular homology, so the sentence $\chi(S^n) = 1 + (-1)^n$ cannot be written down at all. Every statement here is about index sums of vector fields.

Not formalized in Chapter 6: the agreement of the two definitions of the index at an arbitrary isolated zero (V6.14), and the identification of the Poincaré–Hopf integer with the Euler characteristic (V6.9, V6.15). Both are described in §11.

Size. V6.0–V6.9 took 5,064 lines in 22 files: 208 for V6.0 (and 51 for the extension by a cut-off), 1,270 for V6.1 (the level-set orientation on manifolds 410, the bridge lemmas 441, the boundary form of Lemma 1 and the product rule 239, open-subset bookkeeping 90, regular values 90), 146 for general sign lemmas (among them the independence of the outward vector used in V5.8), 345 for V6.2 (with the bijection-degree lemmas, and 66 more in Compose.lean), 639 for V6.3, 128 for V6.4, 976 for V6.5 (the matrix determinant lemma and the radial map 230, the sign of the Jacobian 70, the helper lemmas 202, the main proof 474), 1,020 for V6.6 and V6.7 (local parametrizations 170, the nearest-point equation and the inverse function theorem 293, the nearest-point retraction 259, the region and the extended field 298), 215 for V6.8–V6.9 (with the germ statement of V6.0). The estimate of 2,000 lines in the previous edition was too low by a factor of two and a half; the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood theorem, which Milnor takes from §8, and the replacement of Lemma 2 account for most of the excess.

The items added since then bring Chapter 6's directory to 7,153 lines in 26 files: 262 for V6.10, 199 for V6.11 (of which about half is the parametric integral Mathlib lacks), 929 for V6.13 and V6.14 together, and 699 for V6.15. V6.12, Milnor's Lemma 2, is a further 474 lines and is counted with Chapter 7, where its file sits. The estimate of 800 lines in the previous edition for Lemma 2 and the chart definition together was again too low, by a factor of two: 1,602 lines for V6.11–V6.14. Two things account for the excess. The chart definition needed the derivative of the retraction identified as the orthogonal projection onto the tangent space, which Milnor assumes; and Lemma 2 needed a theorem Mathlib does not have, that an integral over a fixed interval depends smoothly on a parameter. The Euler number of the sphere was not estimated at all, and its 699 lines are mostly the re-presentation of $S^n$ as a level set with a one-dimensional complement model, so that §6's own tubular machinery applies to it.

Milnor.Sphere.indexSum_sphMilnor.Sphere.poincare_hopf_sphMilnor.Sphere.eq_of_indexSum_sphMilnor.Sphere.indexSum_sph_evenMilnor.Sphere.indexSum_sph_oddMilnor.Sphere.indexSum_sph_of_nonvanishingMilnor.Sphere.indexSum_sph_odd_agree

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor writes the value as $\chi(S^n)$; the formal statement is about the integer of V6.9, the common value of the index sum. That this integer is the Euler characteristic is not formalized and cannot even be stated — Mathlib has no Euler characteristic of a space, no Betti numbers and no cellular homology — so the sentence $\chi(S^n) = 1 + (-1)^n$ cannot be written down (G.1).
  • The hypothesis is that the zeros are finitely many, not that they are non-degenerate: Milnor computes with his own field, whose two zeros are non-degenerate, and V6.9 carries the value to every field.
  • The integer is pinned down, not merely computed: any integer with the property V6.9 asserts of $\chi(N)$ for $S^n$ is $1 + (-1)^n$, so the value belongs to the sphere and not to the proof.
  • The odd case is proved a second time with no derivative at all, from the nowhere-zero field of §5, and the two computations are shown to agree; Milnor gets the odd case from $v \mapsto -v$.
  • Three things had to be added for §6's machinery to reach the sphere: the sphere presented again as a level set with a one-dimensional complement model, its tangent space at $q$ identified as $q^\perp$ for that structure, and the derivative through the retraction of a field coming from the ambient space. For the same reason the odd-dimensional field of §5 had to be rebuilt as an ambient linear map, the project having no lemma comparing two slice-submanifold structures on the same set.

Chapter 7 (Milnor §7)

45V7.0

framed submanifolds and framed cobordism

restated

to confirmA framed submanifold of $M$ of codimension $p$ is a compact submanifold with $p$

cobordism without boundaryproduct ends built into the definitionindependence in place of a basis
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, pp. 41–42

Let $N$ and $N'$ be compact $n$-dimensional submanifolds of $M$ with $\partial N = \partial N' = \partial M = \emptyset$; the difference $m - n$ is the codimension. They are cobordant within $M$ if $N \times [0,\varepsilon) \cup N' \times (1-\varepsilon,1]$ extends to a compact manifold $X \subseteq M \times [0,1]$ with $\partial X = N \times 0 \cup N' \times 1$, meeting $M \times 0 \cup M \times 1$ only in $\partial X$. A framing of $N$ is a smooth $\mathfrak v$ assigning to each $x \in N$ a basis of the space $TN_x^\perp \subseteq TM_x$ of normal vectors, and $(N,\mathfrak v)$ is a framed submanifold. Two framed submanifolds are framed cobordant if such an $X$ carries a framing $\mathfrak u$ with $u^i(x,t) = (v^i(x),0)$ near $t = 0$ and $(w^i(x),0)$ near $t = 1$.

The product structure near the two ends is written into the definition, as the set that $X$ is required to extend.

Verified edition V7.0

A framed submanifold of codimension $p$ is a compact submanifold $N \subseteq M$ of dimension $n$ together with $p$ smooth vector fields $v_1, \dots, v_p$ along $N$, tangent to $M$, orthogonal to $N$ and linearly independent at every point (Milnor's framing of the normal bundle of $N$ in $M$, p. 41). Two framed submanifolds are framed cobordant if a framed cobordism in the sense above exists.

Milnor.Framed.FramedSubMilnor.Framed.FramedCobMilnor.Framed.FramedCobordant

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's cobordism is a compact $X \subseteq M \times [0,1]$ with boundary $N_0 \times 0 \cup N_1 \times 1$; the formal $S \subseteq M \times \mathbb R$ has no boundary and exists for all $t$.
  • Milnor's collar becomes an equality: $S$ is exactly $N_0 \times (-\infty,\varepsilon)$ and exactly $N_1 \times (1-\varepsilon,\infty)$ at the two ends; his $X$ is $S \cap (M \times [0,1])$.
  • The framing asks only that the $p$ vectors be linearly independent; that they form a basis of $TN_x^\perp$, which is Milnor's requirement, follows from $n + p = m$.
  • The ambient $M$ is a boundaryless slice submanifold of $\mathbb R^k$; its compactness, a standing hypothesis of the book, is not part of the formal definition.
46V7.1

the Pontryagin manifold

restated

to confirmThe Pontryagin manifold of $f$ at a regular value $y$ for a basis $b$ is $f^{-1}(y)$

framing written as a pseudo-inversebasis need not be positive$M$ compact and nonempty
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 42

For $f : M \to S^p$ smooth and $y$ a regular value, choose a positively oriented basis $\mathfrak v = (v_1,\dots,v_p)$ of $T(S^p)_y$. At $x \in f^{-1}(y)$ the map $df_x$ sends $Tf^{-1}(y)_x$ to zero and carries its orthogonal complement in $TM_x$ isomorphically onto $T(S^p)_y$, so there is a unique vector $w^i(x)$ in that complement with $df_x(w^i(x)) = v_i$. The framed manifold $(f^{-1}(y), f^*\mathfrak v)$ is by definition the Pontryagin manifold associated with $f$.

The framing is pinned down by a uniqueness property, and nothing is said about its smoothness in $x$.

Verified edition V7.1

For $f : M \to S^p$ smooth, $y$ a regular value and $b$ a basis of $\mathbb R^p$, the framed submanifold $(f^{-1}(y), f^* b)$ is built as follows. Let $\tilde F = c_y \circ f \circ r$ be the extension of $f$ to the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood of $M$ (V6.6), read in the chart $c_y$ at $y$, and $A_x = d\tilde F_x$. Then $v_i(x) = A_x^(A_x A_x^)^{-1} b_i$ is tangent to $M$, orthogonal to $f^{-1}(y)$, smooth in $x$, and $A_x v_i(x) = b_i$; the $v_i$ are independent. Deviation (form of the framing): Milnor takes $v_i(x)$ to be the unique normal vector with $df_x(v_i(x)) = b_i$; this is the same vector (V7.8 proves the characterisation), written with the pseudo-inverse so that its smoothness is a formula.

Milnor.Framed.pontryaginMilnor.Framed.At_pontV

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $w^i(x)$ is the unique normal vector with $df_x(w^i(x)) = v_i$; the formal framing is the explicit minimal-norm solution $A_x^(A_xA_x^)^{-1}b_i$, so its smoothness is a formula.
  • $A_x$ is the derivative of an ambient extension through the nearest-point retraction of the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood theorem, which is why $M$ must be compact and nonempty.
  • A basis of $T_yS^p$ is a basis of the model space $\mathbb R^p$, because derivatives into $S^p$ are read in the chart at $y$.
  • Positivity is not asked here: orientations enter with Lemma 1 and positivity with Theorem A, while the agreement with Milnor's vector is proved separately (V7.8).
47V7.2

the framed cobordism of a homotopy, the construction inside Milnor's proofs of Lemmas 2 and 3

restated

to confirmA homotopy constant near the ends, with a common regular value $z$ of the homotopy

isolated from two of Milnor's proofshomotopy defined for all real $t$ends assumed regular as well
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 44 (inside Lemmas 2 and 3)

The book states no such result. Inside the proofs of Lemmas 2 and 3 Milnor observes that for a homotopy $F : M \times [0,1] \to S^p$ with regular value $z$ the set $F^{-1}(z) \subseteq M \times [0,1]$ is a framed manifold and provides a framed cobordism between the framed manifolds of the two ends. In Lemma 3 he arranges beforehand that $F(x,t) = f(x)$ for $t \le \varepsilon$ and $F(x,t) = g(x)$ for $1 - \varepsilon < t \le 1$.

The same step is used twice: in Lemma 2 with the rotated homotopy $F(x,t) = r_tf(x)$, and in Lemma 3 with a homotopy already constant near the ends.

Verified edition V7.2

Let $0 < \varepsilon < \tfrac12$. If $F : M \times \mathbb R \to S^p$ is smooth, equal to $f$ for $t \le \varepsilon$ and to $g$ for $t \ge 1 - \varepsilon$, and $z$ is a regular value of $F$, $f$ and $g$, then the Pontryagin manifolds of $f$ and $g$ at $z$ (same basis) are framed cobordant; the cobordism is $F^{-1}(z)$ with the framing pulled back through the extension of $F$ to $\mathbb R^{K+1} \times \mathbb R$. Any smooth homotopy can be reparametrized to be constant near the ends without losing a regular value (exists_reparam, using a smooth transition function $\rho$ with $\rho = 0$ for $t \le \varepsilon$, $\rho = 1$ for $t \ge 1 - \varepsilon$ and $\rho' > 0$ in between, Milnor.exists_timeTransition, built from Mathlib's smooth transition function).

Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_homotopyMilnor.Framed.exists_reparam

What differs (the comparison)

  • The book never states this step separately; it carries it out once inside the proof of Lemma 2 and again inside the proof of Lemma 3.
  • The homotopy is a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$, constant for $t \le \varepsilon$ and for $t \ge 1 - \varepsilon$, where Milnor's $F$ is defined on $M \times [0,1]$.
  • Regularity of $z$ for $f$ and for $g$ is assumed although it follows from regularity for $F$, because the two Pontryagin manifolds must exist before the statement can be made.
  • Milnor reparametrizes and then chooses the regular value; the formal lemma keeps a value chosen beforehand, so it needs $\rho' > 0$ in the middle and regular end maps.
48V7.3

Lemma 1, the basis does not matter

stronger

to confirmPontryagin manifolds of the same map at the same regular value for two bases of the

two maps allowed, not two basessame orientation, not positivityconnectedness of $GL^+$ proved
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Lemma 1

If $\mathfrak v$ and $\mathfrak v'$ are two different positively oriented bases at $y$, then the Pontryagin manifold $(f^{-1}(y), f^\mathfrak v)$ is framed cobordant to $(f^{-1}(y), f^\mathfrak v')$. The proof chooses a smooth path from $\mathfrak v$ to $\mathfrak v'$ in the space of positively oriented bases of $T(S^p)_y$, identified with $GL^+(p,\mathbb R)$ and therefore connected, and uses the path to frame the cobordism $f^{-1}(y) \times [0,1]$.

The connectedness of $GL^+(p,\mathbb R)$ is asserted without proof, here and again at the end of his proof of Lemma 2 of §6.

Verified edition V7.3

If $b$ and $b'$ determine the same orientation of $\mathbb R^p$, the Pontryagin manifolds of $f$ at $y$ for $b$ and for $b'$ are framed cobordant. The cobordism is $f^{-1}(y) \times \mathbb R$ with a framing that interpolates between the two, along a smooth path in $GL^+(p, \mathbb R)$ from the change-of-basis matrix to the identity. That such a path exists is the connectedness of $GL^+(p, \mathbb R)$, which Milnor asserts without proof in his proof of Lemma 1 (and uses again at the end of the proof of Lemma 2 of §6); it is proved here: every matrix of positive determinant is joined to the identity by a smooth path of matrices of positive determinant, built from transvections (Gaussian elimination), planar rotations by $\pi$ that flip pairs of negative diagonal entries, and a scaling of the diagonal. The general form of Lemma 1 proved here (framedCobordant_of_det_pos') allows the two maps to differ, as long as the level sets and the framings agree; this is what the proof of V7.5 uses, through the composition rule Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_comp, for a map and its composite with a rotation.

Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_det_pos'Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_orientation_eqMilnor.GLp.exists_glPath

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor assumes both bases positively oriented at $y$; the formal statement assumes only that they determine the same orientation of $\mathbb R^p$, which is all the proof uses.
  • The general form lets the two maps differ, provided the level sets coincide and $g^c = f^(A\cdot b)$ with $\det A > 0$; the proof of Theorem A needs exactly this, for $f$ and $r \circ f$.
  • The path in $GL^+(p,\mathbb R)$ is built from transvections, planar rotations by $\pi$ pairing the negative diagonal entries, and a scaling of the diagonal.
  • The cobordism is $f^{-1}(y) \times \mathbb R$, realized as a level set of $(x,t) \mapsto f(x)$, and the path of bases is made constant for $t \le \tfrac14$ and $t \ge \tfrac34$.
49V7.4

framed cobordism is an equivalence relation

restated

to confirmFramed cobordism is symmetric and transitive; it is reflexive for framed

asserted in the book, proved herereflexivity goes through Theorem C$M$ need not be compact
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 42

After defining cobordism Milnor remarks that it is clearly an equivalence relation, and after defining framed cobordism that this again is an equivalence relation. Neither claim is proved; a figure shows two cobordisms pasted together end to end within $M$.

Transitivity is the pasting drawn in the figure, and the collars of the definition are what make the pasting smooth.

Verified edition V7.4

Chapter 7 proves symmetry and transitivity in general. Symmetry reverses time, $(x, t) \mapsto (x, 1 - t)$, and negates the $t$-component of the framing; transitivity shifts the second cobordism by $1$, glues along the common product end $N_1 \times (1 - \varepsilon_1, 1 + \varepsilon_2)$, and rescales time by $\tfrac12$, scaling the $t$-component of the framing by $2$. Reflexivity is proved in Chapter 7 only for framed $0$-manifolds (FramedCobordant.refl'), which is all the Hopf theorem needs, and in every codimension in Chapter 8 (framedCobordant_refl), where the classes $\Pi^p(M)$ are formed; both proofs realise $F$ as the Pontryagin manifold of a map (Theorem C, V7.9) and apply V7.2 to the constant homotopy. That framed cobordism is an equivalence relation is asserted without proof on p. 43. The proof of symmetry and transitivity needs three facts about submanifolds of $M \times \mathbb R$ that are not in Mathlib: the preimage of a slice submanifold under a diffeomorphism is a slice submanifold, the tangent space at a point depends only on the submanifold near that point, and two submanifolds that agree on an open strip glue to a submanifold (Milnor/Chapter7/Transport.lean).

Milnor.Framed.FramedCobordant.symmMilnor.Framed.FramedCobordant.transMilnor.Framed.FramedCobordant.refl'Milnor.Problems.framedCobordant_refl

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor asserts both equivalence relations without proof; the formal text proves symmetry and transitivity and reaches reflexivity only by a detour.
  • Reflexivity is obtained by realizing a framed submanifold as a Pontryagin manifold (Theorem C) and applying the constant homotopy; in Chapter 7 this is done only for framed $0$-manifolds.
  • Symmetry and transitivity hold for any slice submanifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, compact or not; reflexivity needs $M$ compact and nonempty, through the tubular neighbourhood.
  • Three facts about submanifolds of $M \times \mathbb R$ carry the proof: preimages under $(x,t) \mapsto (x, at+c)$, locality of tangent spaces, and gluing along an open strip.
50V7.5

Theorem A of §7, the regular value does not matter

hypotheses explicit

to confirmFor $p \ge 1$, Pontryagin manifolds of the same map at two regular values, with

$p \ge 1$ is a hypothesis$M$ compact and nonemptypositive basis read in the chart
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Theorem A

If $y'$ is another regular value of $f$ and $\mathfrak v'$ is a positively oriented basis for $T(S^p)_{y'}$, then the framed manifold $(f^{-1}(y'), f^\mathfrak v')$ is framed cobordant to $(f^{-1}(y), f^\mathfrak v)$. The proof takes rotations $r_t$ of $S^p$ with $r_0$ the identity and $r_1(y) = y'$, so that $f$ is homotopic to $r_1 \circ f$; Lemma 3 then gives the cobordism, since $(r_1 \circ f)^{-1}(y') = f^{-1} \circ r_1^{-1}(y') = f^{-1}(y)$.

The half-page proof on p. 45 does not say where Lemma 1 enters, although the two bases are different; the local case, $y'$ near $y$, is Lemma 2 on p. 44.

Verified edition V7.5

For $p \ge 1$, if $y$ and $z$ are regular values of $f$ with positive bases $b$ at $y$ and $b'$ at $z$, the Pontryagin manifolds $(f^{-1}(y), f^b)$ and $(f^{-1}(z), f^*b')$ are framed cobordant. (The hypothesis $p \ge 1$ is part of the formal statement; it serves the proof, which rotates one regular value to the other.) Milnor's proof is followed: a planar rotation $r_\theta$ of $S^p$ carries $y$ to $z$; if every point of the arc $r_\theta(y)$, $0 \le \theta \le \theta_0$, is a regular value, then $z$ is a regular value of the homotopy $r_{\theta \rho(t)} \circ f$ and V7.2 gives $f^{-1}(z) \sim (r \circ f)^{-1}(z)$, while $(r \circ f)^{-1}(z) = f^{-1}(y)$ with the framing pulled back through $dr_y$, which preserves orientation, so Lemma 1 finishes (this is Milnor's Lemma 2). For general $y, z$ one chooses (Sard, and the fact that *the regular values of a map from a compact manifold form an open set, Milnor.Framed.isOpen_regularValues_sph) a value $z'$ near $z$ which is at once a regular value of the homotopy, of $f$ and of $r \circ f$, with $y' = r^{-1}(z')$ near $y$, and chains four cobordisms using V7.4. A step the book omits: Milnor's proof of Theorem A (p. 45) compares the two Pontryagin manifolds without invoking Lemma 1, although the positive bases $b$ at $y$ and $b'$ at $z$ are different and the rotated framing is $dr^{-1}b'$, not $b$. The formal proof closes this with the composition rule (Milnor.Framed.pontV_comp) and the general form of Lemma 1. Three tools were built for this: rotations of the sphere as smooth families with $\operatorname{sign}(dr_\theta) = 1$ everywhere (Milnor.Sphere.rotS, signAt_rotS, via V6.5's sign formula for linear maps), the composition rule for Pontryagin manifolds (Milnor.Framed.pontV_comp: $(r \circ f)^b = f^(dr_y^{-1} b)$), and the fact that a map with sign $1$ carries orientations (Milnor.orientation_map_of_orSign_eq_one).

Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_regularValues

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal statement carries $p \ge 1$, which Milnor does not write; the proof rotates one regular value to the other, and for $p = 0$ there is nothing to rotate.
  • $M$ is assumed compact, nonempty and of dimension $n + p$; nonemptiness is needed for the tubular neighbourhood on which every framing is built.
  • Positivity is stated as: the orientation of the basis equals the orientation of $S^p$ at $y$ read in the chart at $y$, since derivatives into the sphere are taken in charts.
  • Milnor moves $y$ to $z$ in one rotation; the formal proof inserts a $z'$ near $z$ regular for $f$, for $r \circ f$ and for the homotopy, and chains four cobordisms, regular values being open.
51V7.6

Theorem B of §7, first half, with Lemma 3

hypotheses explicit

to confirmFor $p \ge 1$, smoothly homotopic maps have framed cobordant Pontryagin manifolds

$p \ge 1$ is a hypothesishomotopy given on $M \times \mathbb R$ends assumed smooth as well
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Theorem B, first half

Two mappings from $M$ to $S^p$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if the associated Pontryagin manifolds are framed cobordant; the first half is the implication from homotopy to framed cobordism. It rests on Lemma 3: if $f$ and $g$ are smoothly homotopic and $y$ is a regular value for both, then $f^{-1}(y)$ is framed cobordant to $g^{-1}(y)$. There Milnor takes a homotopy $F$ equal to $f$ for $0 \le t \le \varepsilon$ and to $g$ for $1 - \varepsilon < t \le 1$, and a regular value $z$ of $F$ close enough to $y$ that Lemma 2 applies at both ends.

Lemma 3 (p. 44) is the special case of a common regular value; different regular values are reconciled by Theorem A.

Verified edition V7.6

For $p \ge 1$, if $f$ and $g$ are smoothly homotopic, $y$ is a regular value of $f$ and $z$ one of $g$ (positive bases), then their Pontryagin manifolds are framed cobordant. For $y = z$ this is Milnor's Lemma 3. (Choose a common regular value of the homotopy and of both ends, reparametrize, apply V7.2, then V7.5 twice.)

Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_homotopic

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal statement carries $p \ge 1$, inherited from Theorem A, and assumes $M$ compact, nonempty and of dimension $n + p$.
  • A smooth homotopy is a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$ rather than on $M \times [0,1]$, and it is given as data rather than quantified away.
  • Smoothness of the two ends is a hypothesis although it follows from smoothness of the homotopy.
  • Milnor reparametrizes and then picks the common regular value; the formal proof picks $z'$ regular for $F$, $f$ and $g$ first, so it needs the openness of regular values to make three dense sets meet.
52V7.7

the Product Neighborhood Theorem

restated

to confirmA framed submanifold has a product neighbourhood: $G(x, s) = r(x + \sum s_i v_i(x))$

tube over a ball, not over $\mathbb R^p$retraction in place of geodesicsabstract form allows noncompact $N$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Product Neighborhood Theorem

Let $N \subseteq M$ be a compact framed submanifold of codimension $p$ with framing $\mathfrak v$ and $\partial N = \partial M = \emptyset$. Then some neighborhood of $N$ in $M$ is diffeomorphic to the product $N \times \mathbb R^p$, by a diffeomorphism under which each $x \in N$ corresponds to $(x,0)$ and each normal frame $\mathfrak v(x)$ to the standard basis of $\mathbb R^p$. For $M = \mathbb R^{n+p}$ he takes $g(x;t_1,\dots,t_p) = x + t_1v^1(x) + \cdots + t_pv^p(x)$ and proves it one-one on $N \times U_\varepsilon$ by a compactness argument with sequences; in general he replaces straight lines by geodesics and leaves the verification to the reader.

He remarks that product neighborhoods do not exist for arbitrary submanifolds, with a figure of an unframable one.

Verified edition V7.7

For a framed submanifold $(N, v)$ of $M$ there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $G(x, s) = r\bigl(x + \sum_i s_i v_i(x)\bigr)$, $r$ the nearest-point retraction of V6.6, is a smooth map $N \times B_\varepsilon \to M$, injective, with open image and smooth inverse, with $G(x, 0) = x$ and $dG_{(x,0)}(a, s) = a + \sum_i s_i v_i(x)$. The abstract part is proved in a general setting and reused for the cobordism $S \subseteq M \times \mathbb R$ in V7.10: let $N$ be a first countable manifold modelled on $\mathbb R^n$, $X$ a Hausdorff manifold without boundary modelled on a finite-dimensional space, $G : N \times \mathbb R^p \to X$ smooth and injective on $N \times \{0\}$, and $K \subseteq N$ compact such that $dG$ is bijective along $K \times \{0\}$ and $G$ is already known to be injective on the pairs of points of $N \times B_{\varepsilon_0}$ with first coordinate outside $K$; then $G$ is injective on some $N \times B_\varepsilon$ (the inverse function theorem in charts and a compactness argument over $K$; $N$ itself need not be compact, which is what the cobordism case requires). If $dG$ is bijective on $N \times B_\varepsilon$, the image is open, and for $N$ nonempty the inverse is smooth on it. Deviation (form): Milnor's diffeomorphism $N \times \mathbb R^p \to V$ is here the map $G$ on a ball, with a smooth clamp $\lambda$ of $\mathbb R^p$ applied to $s$ so that $G$ is defined and smooth everywhere; the inverse is a function on $M$ that is smooth on $V = G(N \times B_\varepsilon)$.

Milnor.PN.exists_injOnMilnor.PN.isOpen_image_tubeMilnor.PN.contMDiffAt_GinvMilnor.Framed.exists_tubeData

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's diffeomorphism is defined on all of $N \times \mathbb R^p$; the formal $G$ is smooth everywhere but a diffeomorphism only from the ball $N \times B_\varepsilon$ onto the open set $V$.
  • Geodesics are replaced by the nearest-point retraction of the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood theorem, so the passage Milnor leaves to the reader is never needed.
  • The clamp $\lambda$, the identity near $0$, keeps $x + \sum_i \lambda(t)_iv_i(x)$ inside the domain of $r$, so that $G$ is defined and smooth on all of $N \times \mathbb R^p$.
  • The abstract injectivity radius allows $N$ noncompact with injectivity known outside a compact $K$, which the cobordism of V7.10 requires; smoothness of the inverse asks $N$ nonempty.
53V7.8

identification of the framing

not in the book

to confirmAt $x \in f^{-1}(y)$, $\ker A_x = T_x N \oplus (T_x M)^\perp$, so a tangent vector of

stated for the ambient derivativekernel found by a dimension countlinks the formula to Milnor's vector
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 42

The book has no such statement. On p. 42 Milnor defines the framing by the property that $df_x$ carries $Tf^{-1}(y)_x^\perp \subseteq TM_x$ isomorphically onto $T(S^p)_y$, so that $w^i(x)$ is the unique normal vector with $df_x(w^i(x)) = v_i$. The formal item fills the gap between that characterisation and the explicit pseudo-inverse formula of V7.1, which the book never writes.

Verified edition V7.8

At $x \in f^{-1}(y)$ the kernel of $A_x = d\tilde F_x$ is $T_x N \oplus (T_x M)^\perp$ (a dimension count), so a vector tangent to $M$, orthogonal to $N$, with $A_x v = b_i$ is the framing vector $f^*b_i(x)$.
