Consequently, if $k : M \to S^p$ has $k^{-1}(\mathrm{north}) = N$ and along each framing line $k(r(x + s v_i(x))) = \pi(c\,A(s e_i))$ for $|s| < \varepsilon'$ (some $\varepsilon' > 0$ and $c > 0$), where $\pi$ is the fixed map of V7.9 and $A = (d\pi_0)^{-1}B$ with $B e_i = b_i$ (the collapse map adapted to $b$), then the north pole is a regular value of $k$ and the Pontryagin manifold of $k$ at the north pole with basis $c\,b$ is exactly $(N, v)$.

Milnor.Framed.ker_At_eqMilnor.Framed.pontV_eq_ofMilnor.Framed.pontryagin_eq_of_collapse

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor needs only that $df_x$ is an isomorphism on the normal space; the formal statement computes the whole kernel of the ambient derivative $A_x$, an object the book does not have.
  • The count: $A_x$ is onto $\mathbb R^p$, so its kernel has dimension $K + 1 - p$, while $T_xN$ and $(T_xM)^\perp$ meet only in $0$ and have dimensions $n$ and $K + 1 - m$.
  • The framing is read off the behaviour of the map along the lines $s \mapsto r(x + sv_i(x))$, which is all that the construction of Theorem C controls.
  • The corollary identifies the Pontryagin manifold of the map built for Theorem C, and is reused unchanged at both ends of a framed cobordism.
54V7.9

Theorem C of §7

restated

to confirmEvery framed submanifold is the Pontryagin manifold of some map at the north pole,

basis realised up to a factor $c > 0$collapse map need not be injectiveregular value is the north pole
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Theorem C

Any compact framed submanifold $(N,\mathfrak w)$ of codimension $p$ in $M$ occurs as the Pontryagin manifold of some smooth mapping $f : M \to S^p$. The proof takes a product representation $g : N \times \mathbb R^p \to V \subseteq M$ and the projection $\pi : V \to \mathbb R^p$ with $\pi(g(x,y)) = y$, for which $0$ is a regular value and $\pi^{-1}(0)$ is precisely $N$ with its given framing. It then chooses a smooth $\varphi : \mathbb R^p \to S^p$ carrying every $x$ with $\|x\| \ge 1$ to a base point $s_0$ and the open unit ball diffeomorphically onto $S^p - s_0$, and sets $f = \varphi \circ \pi$ on $V$ and $f = s_0$ off $V$, with regular value $\varphi(0)$.

That this $f$ is smooth, and that $f^{-1}(\varphi(0)) = \pi^{-1}(0)$ is the framed manifold $N$, is asserted in a line.

Verified edition V7.9

Every framed submanifold $(N, v)$ of $M$ is the Pontryagin manifold of some smooth $f : M \to S^p$ at the north pole: for any basis $b$ of $\mathbb R^p$ there are $c > 0$ and $f$ with $(f^{-1}(\mathrm{north}), f^(c\,b)) = (N, v)$. The map is $f(G(x, s)) = \pi(c A s)$ on the product neighbourhood and the south pole elsewhere, where $\pi : \mathbb R^p \to S^p$ is a fixed smooth map with $\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{north}) = \{0\}$, $d\pi_0$ invertible and $\pi = \mathrm{south}$ outside the ball of radius $\tfrac12$, and $A$ is chosen so that $d(c_{\mathrm{north}} \circ \pi \circ A)_0 = b$ up to the scalar $c$. *Deviation (the scalar $c$): with a fixed collapse map the derivative along the framing comes out as $c\,b$, not $b$; since the framing is what matters and $c$ only rescales the basis, this is harmless (V7.10 and V7.11 carry it along; Lemma 1 absorbs it).

Milnor.Framed.exists_map_of_framedSubMilnor.Sphere.stdMapMilnor.Sphere.collapse

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $\varphi$ maps the open unit ball diffeomorphically onto the punctured sphere; the formal $\pi$ is one fixed explicit map which is never required to be injective.
  • The formal conclusion names the basis: the framing is realized for $c\,b$, not for $b$, because the fixed $\pi$ must be rescaled into the ball $B_{\varepsilon/2}$ of the tube.
  • Milnor parametrizes his product neighbourhood by all of $\mathbb R^p$, which removes the factor $c$; since $c\,b$ and $b$ give the same orientation, Lemma 1 absorbs it.
  • Smoothness off the tube is proved by a closed-core argument, $f$ being the south pole outside a compact subset of $V$, where Milnor writes one line.
55V7.10

Lemma 4 and the map of a cobordism

stronger

to confirmTwo maps with the same Pontryagin manifold at $y$ (bases $b$ and $c\,b$, $c > 0$) are

framings proportional, not equalfirst-order estimate, not Taylorcobordism map stated separately
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Lemma 4

Let $f, g : M \to S^p$ be smooth maps with a common regular value $y$. If the framed manifold $(f^{-1}(y), f^*v)$ is equal to $(g^{-1}(y), g^*v)$, then $f$ is smoothly homotopic to $g$. Milnor first disposes of the case where $f$ and $g$ already agree on a neighbourhood of $N = f^{-1}(y)$, by a straight-line homotopy in stereographic coordinates from the antipode of $y$; then he deforms $f$ until it agrees with $g$ near $N$, using a second-order Taylor bound to keep $F(x,u)$ and $G(x,u)$ in one open half-space so that no new point is carried to $y$.

The homotopy that realizes a framed cobordism is never stated as a lemma: in the proof of Theorem B (p. 49) Milnor says only that an argument analogous to his proof of Theorem C constructs it.

Verified edition V7.10

(a) Let $N$ be nonempty and let the product neighbourhood $G$ of $(N, v)$ (V7.7) be given. If $k : M \to S^p$ has $k^{-1}(y) = N$ and derivative $c\,b_i$, with $c > 0$, on the framing vectors of $(N, v)$ (read in the chart $c_y$ at $y$), then $k$ is smoothly homotopic to a map $k'$ which near $N$ is the standard map $G(x, s) \mapsto c_y^{-1}\bigl(c_y(y) + \sum s_i b_i\bigr)$ and does not take the value $y$ off $N$. The homotopy is the bump-function interpolation, in the chart at $y$, between the chart representative $\Phi$ of $k \circ G$ and its linearisation $L s = \sum s_i b_i$; that $k'$ takes the value $y$ only on $N$ follows from $|\Phi(x, s) - c L s| \le \kappa |s|$ uniformly (a uniform first-order estimate on a tube, Milnor.exists_uniform_taylor, from joint continuity of the fibre derivative and the mean value inequality). The formal statement records this for the end map $k'$ only; Milnor's remark that the deformation creates no new preimages of $y$ at intermediate times is not part of it, and is not needed. (b) Two maps with the same Pontryagin manifold at $y$, for bases $b$ and $c\,b$ with $c > 0$ (Milnor: the same basis), are smoothly homotopic: deform both to the standard form, then join them by the straight-line homotopy in stereographic coordinates from $y$ (Milnor.Sphere.exists_homotopy_of_eqOn: maps agreeing on an open set and avoiding $y$ outside it are smoothly homotopic relative to that set). This is Milnor's Lemma 4 (p. 47). (c) For a framed cobordism $S$ between $F_0$ and $F_1$ there is a smooth $H : M \times \mathbb R \to S^p$ whose restrictions to $t = 0$ and $t = 1$ have Pontryagin manifolds $F_0$ and $F_1$ at the north pole (with a common basis $c\,b$). The construction is V7.9 applied to $S \cap (M \times (-3, 4))$, using the product structure of the ends to obtain the injectivity radius outside a compact piece. The resulting map $H_0$ is defined on all of $M \times \mathbb R$ but is smooth only on $M \times (-2, 3)$; so the time variable is clamped, $H(x, t) = H_0(x, \rho(t))$ with $\rho$ smooth, equal to the identity near $[0, 1]$ and with values in $(-2, 3)$, which makes $H$ smooth on all of $M \times \mathbb R$.

Milnor.Framed.exists_deformMilnor.Framed.smoothHomotopic_of_pontryagin_eqMilnor.Framed.exists_cobMap

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's Lemma 4 asks that the two framed manifolds be equal; the formal statement asks only that $f^b$ and $g^(c\,b)$ agree for some $c > 0$, so it contains his case $c = 1$.
  • Milnor deforms $f$ onto $g$ with a second-order Taylor bound; the formal proof deforms each map separately onto the standard map $G(x,s) \mapsto \varphi_y^{-1}(\varphi_y(y) + \sum_i s_i b_i)$.
  • The deformation lemma controls the preimage of $y$ only at time $1$; Milnor's remark that no new point reaches $y$ during the deformation is not in the formal statement and is not used.
  • The map of a cobordism is a separate statement: it is built on $S \cap (M \times (-3,4))$, and time is clamped so that the result is smooth on all of $M \times \mathbb R$.
56V7.11

Theorem B of §7, second half

narrower

to confirmFor $p \ge 1$, if the Pontryagin manifolds of $f$ and $g$ (positive bases, any

$p \ge 1$ assumedhomotopy smooth on $M \times \mathbb R$regular values may differ
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Theorem B

Two mappings $M \to S^p$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if their associated Pontryagin manifolds are framed cobordant. The half proved on p. 49 is the converse: given a framed cobordism $(X, w)$ between $f^{-1}(y)$ and $g^{-1}(y)$, an argument like the proof of Theorem C produces $F : M \times [0,1] \to S^p$ whose Pontryagin manifold is exactly $(X, w)$; its two ends then have the same Pontryagin manifolds as $f$ and $g$, and Lemma 4 finishes.

Milnor works with a single regular value $y$ common to $f$ and $g$, which Theorem A lets him arrange, and with a positively oriented basis $v$ of $T(S^p)_y$ fixed once and for all.

Verified edition V7.11

For $p \ge 1$, if the Pontryagin manifolds of $f$ at $y$ and of $g$ at $y'$ (positive bases) are framed cobordant, then $f$ and $g$ are smoothly homotopic. Smooth homotopy, defined by a smooth map $M \times \mathbb R \to S^p$ restricting to the two maps at $t = 0, 1$, is an equivalence relation (SmoothHomotopic.refl/symm/trans; transitivity glues two homotopies made constant near their ends). The proof rotates both regular values to the north pole (a homotopy through rotations that changes the Pontryagin manifold only by the rotated basis, V7.5), uses V7.3 to make the two bases equal, builds the map $H$ of V7.10(c) for the cobordism, and applies V7.10(b) at both ends.

Milnor.Framed.smoothHomotopic_of_framedCobordantMilnor.SmoothHomotopic

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal statement assumes $p \ge 1$, used only to rotate a regular value to the north pole, where a unit vector orthogonal to $y$ is needed; the case $p = 0$ is not covered.
  • A smooth homotopy is a smooth map on $M \times \mathbb R$ with the two maps at $t = 0$ and $t = 1$, not a map on $M \times [0,1]$; the relation is proved to be an equivalence relation.
  • The two maps are allowed different regular values $y$ and $y'$, each with its own positive basis; Milnor first arranges a common $y$ by Theorem A.
  • Positivity of the two bases is a named hypothesis, as in Milnor's definition of the Pontryagin manifold, and $M$ is a compact submanifold of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$ given by slice charts.
57V7.12

framed $0$-manifolds and the signed count

hypotheses explicit

to confirmA framed $0$-manifold is a finite set of points with a basis of $T_x M$ at each;

finiteness of the point set provedsign read in the chart at $x$$m \ge 1$ assumed
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

Let $M$ be connected and oriented of dimension $m = p$. A framed submanifold of codimension $p$ is then just a finite set of points with a preferred basis at each, and $\operatorname{sgn}(x)$ is $+1$ or $-1$ according as that basis gives the right or the wrong orientation. Milnor says it is clear that $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$ equals the degree of the associated map $M \to S^m$.

He states this in one sentence, with no proof, and does not say that finiteness of the point set needs an argument.

Verified edition V7.12

For $n = 0$ and $p = m$ a framed submanifold is a finite set of points (finiteness is proved: a compact $0$-dimensional slice submanifold is finite), each carrying $m$ tangent vectors forming a basis of $T_x M$. Relative to an orientation of $M$ (V5.0) each point has a sign $\pm 1$, the sign of the basis of chart coordinates of the frame against $o_x$, and the signed count is the sum of the signs. For $m \ge 1$ and the Pontryagin manifold of $f : M \to S^m$ at a regular value $y$ with a positive basis, the sign of $x \in f^{-1}(y)$ is the sign of $df_x$, so the signed count is the degree of $f$ (V5.7). This is, in formal form, Milnor's statement (p. 50) that the sum of the signs is the degree of the associated map; his next statement, that the framed cobordism class of the $0$-manifold is determined by this integer, is V7.16.

Milnor.Framed.frameSignMilnor.Framed.countMilnor.Framed.count_pontryagin

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor calls the identity clear; the formal proof shows that $df_x$ carries the Pontryagin frame at $x$ to the positive basis $e$, so the two signs multiply to $+1$, and sums over $f^{-1}(y)$.
  • That a compact framed $0$-manifold is finite is proved, from a slice chart at each point whose domain meets it only there; Milnor takes it for granted.
  • The sign of a frame at $x$ is defined through the chart coordinates of its vectors, compared with $o_x$; Milnor speaks of the basis giving the right or the wrong orientation.
  • $m \ge 1$ is assumed, so that $S^m$ is connected and the degree may be computed at any regular value; Milnor's next clause, on the framed cobordism class, is V7.16.
58V7.13

moving a framed point

not in the book

to confirmFor $m \ge 1$, let $U$ be a chart ball whose closed ball lies in the chart image, on

no statement in the bookclosed $R$-ball inside the chart imagechart signs compared, no orientation
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

The book has no statement of this kind. Moving a framed point is the first of the steps hidden in Milnor's single clause that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is determined by $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$; the picture he gives, in the non-orientable remark just below, is of a point slid "around $M$ in a closed loop" carrying its basis with it.

Connectedness of $M$ is the only property he invokes for such motions.

Verified edition V7.13

Let $m \ge 1$, let $\varphi$ be the chart at a point $x_c$ of $M$, let $R > 0$ be such that the closed ball of radius $R$ about $\varphi(x_c)$ lies in the chart image and $\varphi$ preserves orientation on the corresponding chart ball $U$, and suppose that no point of $N$ other than $x_0$ lies in the closure of $U$. If $x_0 \in N$ and $x_1$ lie in the chart ball of radius $R/4$ about $\varphi(x_c)$, and $x_1$ carries a frame $v_1$ of the same sign as $v_0$, then $(N, v)$ is framed cobordant to $(N, v)$ with $(x_0, v_0)$ replaced by $(x_1, v_1)$. The cobordism is the Pontryagin manifold of a homotopy: the Theorem-C map of $(N, v)$ (V7.9, with its support kept away from the frontier of $U$) is replaced on $U$ by a collapse map whose centre moves from $\varphi(x_0)$ to $\varphi(x_1)$ and whose linear part moves along a path in $GL^+$ or $GL^-$ (V7.3) from the matrix producing $v_0$ to the one producing $v_1$; V7.2 gives the cobordism, and the framings at both ends are identified as in V7.8. Consequently ($m \ge 1$, $M$ oriented) every point of the connected component of $x_0$ in $M \setminus (N \setminus \{x_0\})$ can be reached, with any frame of the right sign (the set of reachable points is open and closed in the component). Deviation (proof): Milnor moves points along paths; here the moves are homotopies of maps inside chart balls, and paths are replaced by the open-closed argument.

Milnor.Framed.exists_moveMilnor.Framed.exists_reach

What differs (the comparison)

  • The book states no move: one clause at the end of §7 covers the whole induction, and the picture it suggests is a point sliding along a path in $M$ carrying its basis.
  • The formal statement needs the chart image at $x_c$ to contain the closed ball of radius $R$ about $\varphi(x_c)$, so that $\overline{U_R}$ stays inside the chart and the glued map is smooth.
  • It no longer needs $\varphi$ to preserve orientation on $U_R$, nor an orientation of $M$ at all, so neither is a difference from the book any more: the two frames are compared by their chart signs at the hub $x_c$, the determinant signs of the frames read in that one chart. All the old positivity hypothesis was ever used for is that $d\varphi_z$ is invertible, and a chart derivative is invertible at every point of its domain.
  • With it went two hypotheses from each of six theorems in the glued-homotopy files, the orientation of $M$ and that positivity, so that those files now mention no orientation at all; and the lemma asserting that the derivative of a glued slice is bijective, whose hypothesis that the chart preserves orientation was spurious, was deleted in favour of the general fact.
  • Both $x_0$ and $x_1$ must lie in $U_{R/4}$, and no point of $N$ other than $x_0$ in $\overline{U_R}$; paths are replaced by an open-and-closed argument on the reachable points.
59V7.14

cancelling a pair

not in the book

to confirmFor $m \ge 1$, in such a chart ball $U$ of radius $R$, two points at

no statement in the bookstandard frames of chart signs $\pm 1$no orientation, no positivity
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

The book has no statement of this kind either. Cancelling a pair of points of opposite sign is the second step hidden in Milnor's clause that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is determined by $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$; the geometry behind it is the U-shaped arc of a cobordism whose two ends are preimages of opposite sign, the picture already used for the degree in §5.

Verified edition V7.14

Let $m \ge 1$, and let $\varphi$, $x_c$, $R$ and $U$ be as in V7.13 (closed $R$-ball in the chart image, $\varphi$ orientation-preserving on $U$), with no point of $N$ other than $x_1, x_2$ in the closure of $U$. If $x_1, x_2 \in N$ are the points $\varphi^{-1}(c_0 \pm a w)$ with $w$ a unit vector, $|c_0 - \varphi(x_c)| < R/8$ and $0 < 8a \le R$, then $(N, v)$, with the frames at $x_1, x_2$ replaced by the standard ones, is framed cobordant to $(N, v)$ with $x_1, x_2$ removed; the standard frames have signs $\varepsilon$ and $-\varepsilon$, where $\varepsilon = \pm 1$ depends only on the chart (it is the sign of the identity of $\mathbb R^m$ from $o_{x_c}$ to the standard orientation), so a pair of arbitrary frames of opposite signs is cancelled after two frame changes (V7.13, with $w$ or $-w$ according to which point has sign $\varepsilon$). The homotopy is the collapse map composed with $s \mapsto (\langle s, w\rangle^2 - a^2\rho(t))\, w + (s - \langle s, w\rangle w)$, whose zero set at time $t$ is $\{\lambda w : \lambda^2 = a^2 \rho(t)\}$ with $\rho$ going from $1$ to $-1$; at the moment the two zeros merge the time derivative makes the north pole a regular value of the homotopy, as V7.2 requires. Combined with the neighbour lemma (V7.15): if $x_0$ has a neighbour $z$ of the opposite sign, $x_0$ is first moved next to $z$ (V7.13) and the pair cancelled (cancel_step, which finds a suitable chart ball about $z$).

Milnor.Framed.exists_cancelMilnor.Framed.cancel_step

What differs (the comparison)

  • The book states nothing here; cancelling a pair of opposite signs is one step of the induction left to the reader at the end of §7.
  • The cobordism is between $(N, v)$ with the standard frames installed at $x_1, x_2$ and $N \setminus \{x_1, x_2\}$, not between $(N, v)$ itself and the two points removed, so two frame changes come first.
  • Those frames no longer have signs $\pm\varepsilon$ for a factor $\varepsilon$ read off an orientation: their chart signs are $1$ and $-1$ on the nose, and the statement carrying the proof needs neither an orientation of $M$ nor the hypothesis that $\varphi$ preserve one on $U_R$, so neither is a difference from the book any more. The factor $\varepsilon$ survives only in the oriented corollary, where it is the orientation factor at the centre of the ball, and the positivity is the one hypothesis that corollary still carries.
  • The positivity was doing no work: regularity of the north pole for the cancelling homotopy and for its slices needs only that a chart derivative be invertible, which holds at every point of the chart's domain. So the lemma whose hypothesis was that the chart preserves orientation was spurious and is gone, and six theorems of the two glued-homotopy files lost two hypotheses each (V7.13).
  • The points must sit at $\varphi^{-1}(c_0 \pm a w)$ with $|c_0 - \varphi(x_c)| < R/8$ and $8a \le R$; the zeros at time $t$ are $\{\lambda w : \lambda^2 = a^2 \rho(t)\}$, with $\rho$ from $1$ to $-1$.
60V7.15

the neighbour lemma and chains

not in the book

to confirmIn a connected, locally connected $T_1$ space with the segment property, for a finite

no statement in the bookno sign, no orientation, no compactnesschains needed when $m = 1$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

There is no such lemma in the book. On p. 50 Milnor lets $M$ be connected and, having identified $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$ with the degree, remarks in a single clause that the framed cobordism class of the $0$-manifold is determined by that integer; connectedness is all he invokes for moving points about.

For $m \ge 2$ the complement of a finite set is still connected and any point can be moved onto any other, so nothing more is needed; the difficulty is invisible until $m = 1$.

Verified edition V7.15

In a connected, locally connected $T_1$ space, let $N$ be finite and, for $x \in N$, let $C(x)$ be the connected component of $x$ in the complement of $N \setminus \{x\}$; assume every $x \in N$ has a neighbourhood meeting $N$ only in $x$ and contained in $C(x) \cup \{x\}$ (true for chart balls, by joining points to the centre along segments). Then for every partition of $N$ into two nonempty parts some point of one part lies in the closure of $C(x)$ for a point $x$ of the other part ("$x$ has a neighbour in the other part"). Proof: otherwise one part together with its components is open, closed, nonempty and not everything. The same argument gives chains of consecutive neighbours from one part to the other. This is the formal content of the connectedness of $M$ in Milnor's "it is not difficult to see" (the Hopf theorem, end of §7). It is needed in this form because for $m = 1$ points cannot pass each other, so that a point of $F$ may have to be moved onto a point of $G$ along a chain through points that are already in place, which are moved one step each.

Milnor.exists_neighbourMilnor.Framed.exists_neighbour_MMilnor.exists_chainRel

What differs (the comparison)

  • This is the formal content of Milnor's appeal to the connectedness of $M$; the book states no lemma of the kind.
  • It is proved for an abstract connected, locally connected $T_1$ space with a segment property at each point of $N$; for $M$ that property comes from a chart ball meeting $N$ only in its centre, and asks nothing of the chart but that the closed ball of the radius lie in its image.
  • Where the two moves of V7.13 and V7.14 had to be restated with the chart sign, this lemma needed no restating, and the row lists no orientation among its differences from the book: it carries no frame, no sign, no orientation, no compactness and no dimension hypothesis. What the sign-free endgame deletes is the sorting of the points by sign that precedes the application (V7.24), not anything in the lemma.
  • Chains are needed because for $m = 1$ points cannot pass each other: a point may reach its target only through points already in place, which are then moved one step each.
  • For $m \ge 2$ every point of $N$ is a neighbour of every other and every chain has length one, the component $C(x)$ being then the whole of $M \setminus (N \setminus \{x\})$ (V7.22); the formal proof is uniform in the dimension and never uses this.
61V7.16

framed cobordism classes of framed $0$-manifolds

hypotheses explicit

to confirmOn a compact connected oriented $M$ ($m \ge 1$), framed $0$-manifolds with the

$m \ge 1$ assumedproof left to the reader in the bookconnectedness used via neighbours
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

With $M$ connected, oriented and of dimension $m = p$, Milnor writes that it is "not difficult to see" that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is uniquely determined by the integer $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$, and passes at once to the Hopf theorem.

This is the one assertion of §7 for which the book gives no argument at all.

Verified edition V7.16

Two framed $0$-manifolds of a compact connected oriented $M$ ($m \ge 1$) with the same signed count are framed cobordant. Induction on the total number of points: if one of them has points of both signs, V7.15 gives a neighbouring pair of opposite signs and V7.14 removes it without changing the count; if all points of $F$ have one sign and all points of $G$ have one sign, equal counts force the same sign and the same number of points; the frames at common points are matched by frame changes, and then the points of $F$ are moved onto the points of $G$ one at a time along shortest chains of neighbours (gather).

Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_count_eq

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor asserts this in a single clause and gives no argument; the formal proof is an induction on the total number of points of the two framed $0$-manifolds.
  • The formal statement names $m \ge 1$ and $M$ compact, nonempty, connected and oriented; all but $m \ge 1$ are already in force in Milnor's paragraph.
  • Connectedness enters only through the neighbour lemma and the chain lemma of V7.15, never as a way of joining two points by a path in $M$.
  • Cancelling a neighbouring pair of opposite signs leaves the count unchanged and drops the point count by two; the points left over are gathered along shortest chains.
62V7.17

the Hopf theorem

restated

to confirmOn such $M$, smooth maps $M \to S^m$ of the same degree are smoothly homotopic,

converse is Theorem B of §5homotopy means smooth homotopydegrees realized without connectedness
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Theorem of Hopf

If $M$ is connected, oriented and boundaryless, then two maps $M \to S^m$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if they have the same degree. Milnor obtains it by putting together the identity $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x) = \deg$, the assertion that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is determined by that integer, and Theorem B. He adds that for non-orientable $M$ an easy argument classifies the maps by the degree modulo $2$.

He states only the classification; that every integer occurs as a degree is not part of it.

Verified edition V7.17

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact connected oriented submanifold of dimension $m \ge 1$. Two smooth maps $M \to S^m$ with the same degree are smoothly homotopic (the converse is Theorem B of §5, V5.7), and every integer is the degree of a smooth map $M \to S^m$ (for this second statement $M$ need not be connected). Proof of the first: choose regular values and positive bases; the signed counts of the two Pontryagin manifolds are the two degrees (V7.12), so the Pontryagin manifolds are framed cobordant (V7.16) and the maps are homotopic (V7.11). Proof of the second: starting from the empty framed $0$-manifold, add $|d|$ points of the required sign one at a time (a point outside a finite set exists because chart balls are infinite; the new point is added by gluing a stationary collapse map onto the Theorem-C map, exists_add_point), then apply Theorem C (V7.9) and V7.12. Deviations: homotopy means smooth homotopy (V4, convention), and $M$ is a submanifold of Euclidean space, as in all of §6–§7. Milnor's remark that for non-orientable $M$ the maps are classified by the degree modulo $2$ is V7.26, at the end of this section.

The eleven items that follow prove that remark. Milnor gives it a single clause of justification — a framed point carried around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with its frame reversed, so only the parity of the number of points of a framed $0$-manifold survives — and the clause presupposes what an orientation-reversing loop is and that a non-orientable manifold has one. An orientation of a manifold is V5.0, Milnor's own; orientability as a property, and its characterization by reversing loops, existed neither in Mathlib nor anywhere in this project, and the book does not prove it either. So the work is in two halves: orientability and the loop (V7.18, V7.19, V7.22), and the removal of the signs from the framed machinery (V7.20, V7.21, V7.23–V7.25), ending in the theorem itself (V7.26); two further items (V7.27, V7.28), written after the Appendix was finished, then remove the one hypothesis that route left behind. The survey behind them found that the framed theory is almost entirely orientation-free already: V7.0, V7.1 and V7.9 take no orientation of $M$ at all and V7.11 needs one only on the sphere, so what had to be redone is one definition, the sign of a frame (V7.12), and the endgame that cancels a plus against a minus (V7.14, V7.16).

Milnor.Framed.hopf_homotopicMilnor.Framed.exists_map_of_deg

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor states one equivalence; the formal statement is the half saying that equal degrees give a homotopy, the other half being Theorem B of §5.
  • Homotopy means smooth homotopy throughout: a smooth map $M \times \mathbb R \to S^m$ restricting to the two maps at $t = 0$ and $t = 1$.
  • $m \ge 1$ is assumed, and $M$ is a compact nonempty oriented submanifold of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$, as everywhere in §§6–7 of the formalization.
  • That every integer is a degree is a second statement, proved without assuming $M$ connected; Milnor's mod 2 classification for non-orientable $M$ is V7.26.
91V7.18

orientability, transport of an orientation, and the chart-level dichotomy

not in the book

to confirmA manifold is orientable if it carries an orientation in the sense of item 24. The

no orientability in the booka hop records which chart it usesexactly two orientations if connected
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

The book defines an oriented manifold (§5, p. 26, V5.0) but never asks whether an orientation exists, and orientability itself is nowhere defined: the word appears in the remark at the end of §7, that for a connected non-orientable $M$ the maps to $S^m$ are classified by the degree modulo $2$. What Milnor relies on instead is the picture of a frame carried around a closed loop in $M$ and coming back reversed, and the fact that his manifolds are subsets of Euclidean space, where every tangent space is a subspace of one fixed $\mathbb R^k$.

Nothing in the book compares the orientations at two points along a chain of charts; that comparison is the content of this row.

Verified edition V7.18

Let $M$ be a manifold modelled on a finite-dimensional $E$ with $\dim E = m$. (i) $M$ is orientable if it carries an orientation in the sense of V5.0. (ii) The derivative at $y$ of the chart centred at $x$ is a linear isomorphism, so exactly one orientation of the model space at $y$ is carried by it to a given orientation at $x$: this is the transport $o \mapsto \operatorname{tr}_{x \to y} o$ through that chart, an involution which anticommutes with reversal. An orientation of $M$ is exactly a family of orientations which near every point is the transport of its value at that point. (iii) A hop from $x$ to $y$ is a single chart whose source contains both; a chain is a finite sequence of hops; chains compose and reverse, transport along a composite is the composite of the transports, and transport along the reverse undoes the chain. In a connected manifold any two points are joined by a chain (the reachable set is open and closed). (iv) A chain-loop reverses if its transport carries some orientation at the base point to the opposite one; which of the two is tested is immaterial. (v) Half of the dichotomy: if no chain-loop at a point of a connected $M$ reverses, then $M$ is orientable — transport a fixed orientation from the base point, the answer being unambiguous because two chains differ by a loop. Contrapositively, a connected non-orientable manifold carries at every point a chain-loop that reverses every orientation there. (vi) A connected orientable manifold has exactly two orientations, a given one and its pointwise reverse. Deviation, and a warning: the converse of (v) is false for an orientation in the sense of V5.0, whose compatibility is a condition only near each point. It holds for the stronger notion of an orientation coherent with every chart on the whole of that chart's source, and for connected $M$ the absence of reversing chain-loops is equivalent to the existence of such a chart-coherent orientation. The counterexample is recorded in the Lean file: on $\mathbb R$ take the atlas consisting of the identity together with the chart that is $x$ on $(-1, 0)$ and $3 - x$ on $(1, 2)$; this is a smooth structure on $\mathbb R$, which is orientable, and yet the orientation that is standard except reversed on $(1, 2)$ satisfies the condition of V5.0 while the loop out through the second chart and back through the identity chart reverses. The culprit is that a chart source may be disconnected, and V7.19 removes the defect. A step the design did not foresee: a hop must record which chart it travels through. The naive relation "some chart contains both points" is not symmetric in the transport it induces, and the obvious symmetric repair is wrong, precisely because two charts can disagree on a disconnected overlap. Hidden hypotheses: none — no boundarylessness, no Hausdorffness, no second countability, and $M$ may be empty; connectedness only where it is stated.

Milnor.IsOrientableMilnor.orTransportMilnor.ChartHopMilnor.ChartChainMilnor.Orient.isTransportSectionMilnor.Orient.ofTransportSectionMilnor.ChartChain.ReversesMilnor.isOrientable_of_forall_not_reversesMilnor.IsChartCoherentMilnor.exists_isChartCoherent_iff_forall_not_reversesMilnor.exists_reverses_of_not_isOrientableMilnor.Orient.or_eq_or_eq_neg

What differs (the comparison)

  • No counterpart in the book: Milnor's manifolds are subsets of Euclidean space, orientability is never defined for them, and the transport of an orientation along a chain of charts has to be built before the remark of p. 50 can be stated at all.
  • A hop has to record which chart it travels through. The naive relation "some chart contains both points" does not induce a symmetric transport, and the obvious symmetric repair is wrong, precisely because two charts can disagree on a disconnected overlap.
  • Only half of the dichotomy is unconditional at this level. The converse is false for an orientation in the sense of V5.0, whose compatibility is a condition only near each point; it holds for the stronger notion of an orientation coherent with each chart on the whole of that chart's source, and the counterexample is recorded in the source: on $\mathbb R$ with the atlas made of the identity chart and the chart that is $x$ on $(-1, 0)$ and $3 - x$ on $(1, 2)$, the orientation that is standard except reversed on $(1, 2)$ satisfies V5.0's condition, and the loop out through the second chart and back through the first reverses, although $\mathbb R$ is orientable. V7.19 removes the defect.
  • Nothing is assumed of $M$ beyond what each statement says: no boundarylessness, no Hausdorffness, no second countability, no positive dimension, and $M$ may be empty; connectedness appears as preconnectedness and only where it is stated.
92V7.19

chart balls and the exact dichotomy

not in the book

to confirmTransport along a hop through a round chart ball carries any orientation of the

round balls, where chart sources failevery orientation is ball-coherentthe converse holds, with no extra notion
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

For the remark at the end of §7 Milnor needs one alternative of a dichotomy he does not state: a connected manifold either carries a coherent choice of orientations, or admits a closed loop that brings an orientation back reversed, in which case a framed point slid around the loop returns with the sign of its frame changed. He takes the second alternative as read for a non-orientable $M$, and the loops he has in mind are the paths along which points are slid in the proof of the Hopf theorem.

The reversing loop is the one geometric input of the mod 2 remark; the rest of §7 is orientation-free.

Verified edition V7.19

A chart ball is the preimage under the chart centred at $c$ of a round ball about the chart image of $c$; when that ball lies in the target of the chart, the chart ball is connected, which is the one property a chart source lacks. A ball hop is a hop through such a ball containing both its endpoints, a ball chain a chain all of whose hops are of that kind; the entire transport algebra of V7.18 applies verbatim, only the predicate having to be shown closed under the constructions. The key lemma: every orientation of $M$ is ball-coherent — transport along a ball hop carries the orientation at one end to the orientation at the other. (On a chart ball about $c$ the function $z \mapsto \operatorname{sgn}(o_z \text{ against } o_c \text{ through } d\varphi_c)$ takes only the values $\pm 1$, is locally constant by the continuity field of V5.0, equals $1$ at $c$, and a locally constant function on a connected set is constant. For a chart source the conclusion is false, which is exactly the counterexample of V7.18.) Hence the exact dichotomy: for connected $M$ and any base point, $M$ is orientable if and only if no ball-chain-loop at that point reverses; and a connected non-orientable $M$ carries at every point a ball-chain-loop reversing every orientation there. Why this matters: unlike V7.18(v) the converse now holds with no auxiliary notion, so a reversing ball loop is a genuine obstruction to orientability rather than a symptom of a badly chosen atlas — and the loops run through round balls, which is what the moving machinery of V7.13 can follow. Hidden hypothesis: the model boundaryless, on 24 of the 61 declarations and for two independent reasons, both of which are that a chart target must be open: a ball inside the chart target has to exist at all (hence for every statement that produces a ball hop, including the converse half), and the sign of a chart derivative has to be locally constant (hence for the key lemma and the forward half). Everything about a chart ball that is given needs it not. Hausdorffness, second countability and positivity of the dimension are nowhere needed.

Milnor.chartBallMilnor.isPreconnected_chartBallMilnor.BallHopMilnor.ChartChain.IsBallMilnor.BallHop.transport_orMilnor.ballReachable_of_preconnectedSpaceMilnor.isOrientable_iff_forall_not_reverses_isBallMilnor.exists_isBall_reverses_of_not_isOrientable

What differs (the comparison)

  • No statement of this kind in the book: it is what the remark of p. 50 presupposes, and the mod 2 endgame consumes it in the form "at every point outside a finite set there is a reversing loop of balls" (V7.26).
  • Round balls make the dichotomy exact, where chains of charts give only one half (V7.18): for a chart source the key lemma is false, and that is exactly the counterexample recorded there.
  • The key lemma is the one step with no chart-level counterpart, and it is where the continuity field of an orientation (V5.0) earns its keep; it needs a connected chart domain, which a round ball is and a chart source need not be.
  • Ball chains are chart chains together with a predicate, not a second inductive type, so composition, reversal, transport and the notion of a reversing loop are literally the chart-level ones and no algebra is duplicated.
  • Boundarylessness of the model enters twice, in both cases because a chart target must be open: a ball inside the target has to exist at all, which every statement producing a ball hop needs, and the sign of a chart derivative has to be locally constant, which the key lemma needs. Everything about a chart ball that is given rather than produced needs it not, and Hausdorffness, second countability and positivity of the dimension are needed nowhere.
93V7.20

the chart sign of a frame

not in the book

to confirmThe chart sign of a frame at a point — the determinant sign of the frame read in a

a sign with no orientation in itMilnor's sign, times a point factorframes of both signs at every point
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

The remark at the end of §7 is justified in a single clause: a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged. This row carries out those five words. The sign Milnor means is the one he defines a few lines earlier (V7.12): $\operatorname{sgn}(x)$ is $+1$ or $-1$ according as the frame at $x$ gives the right or the wrong orientation of $M$. On the manifolds of the remark there is no right orientation, so that sign is not defined, and the book offers no substitute: the clause says the sign changes without saying what it is measured against.

A sign of a frame that mentions no orientation, and the identity relating it to Milnor's sign where an orientation exists.

Verified edition V7.20

The sign of V7.12 is defined against an orientation of $M$, which a non-orientable $M$ does not have, so the local lemmas must be restated with a sign that mentions none. The chart sign of a frame $v$ at $x$ relative to a centre $x_c$ is the determinant sign of $v$ read in the chart at $x_c$; the self sign is the case $x_c = x$; the Jacobian sign is the determinant sign of the derivative at $x$ of the chart at $x_c$. All three are $\pm 1$ for a tangent independent frame at a point of the chart's source, none mentions an orientation, and $\text{chart sign} = \text{Jacobian sign} \cdot \text{self sign}$, with Jacobian sign $1$ at the centre. The bridge to the oriented theory: the sign of V7.12 satisfies $$\operatorname{frameSign}(v, x) = \varepsilon(x)\cdot \text{self sign}(v, x),$$ where $\varepsilon(x)$ depends only on the point and not on the frame. The sign of a frame against an orientation of the manifold is thus the determinant sign of the frame read in one chart, times a factor depending only on the point — and that is what lets the local machinery run without an orientation, since every local proof compares a product of two such signs, in which the factor cancels. At the level of a chart ball the same holds with the hub's factor, so equality of frame signs at two points of a ball is exactly equality of their chart signs relative to the hub. Also: frames of both chart signs exist at every point, with no case split on a global reference sign, and a frame at $x$ may be transported to any other point of the same chart's source as the same frame read in that chart, hence with the same chart sign. Consequences for the earlier items: V7.13–V7.15 are restated in these terms (MoveH and CancelH no longer mention an orientation at all; six theorems lost two hypotheses each, and a lemma whose hypothesis "the chart preserves orientation" was spurious is deleted in favour of the general one), and the oriented statements are short corollaries beside the sign-free ones, so that no proof is duplicated. The sign of the frame at a zero of the cancelling map is now flatly the sign of the signed distance, where before it carried a factor depending on the orientation. A better primitive than expected: the self sign needs no chart-membership hypothesis at all, which is what makes it usable in the clopen arguments of V7.23.

Milnor.Framed.chartSignMilnor.Framed.selfSignMilnor.Framed.jacSignMilnor.Framed.chartSign_eq_jacSign_mul_selfSignMilnor.Framed.frameSign_eq_orEps_mul_selfSignMilnor.Framed.exists_frame_of_selfSignMilnor.Framed.exists_frame_transport

What differs (the comparison)

  • No counterpart in the book: the sign of a frame is defined there only against an orientation of $M$ (V7.12), which is exactly what a non-orientable $M$ lacks, so the sign the clause speaks of has to be built before the clause can be stated.
  • The bridge is what makes the rest of the chapter reusable rather than rewritten. Every local proof of §7 compares a product of two signs, and the point-dependent factor cancels from such a product; so V7.13 to V7.15 were restated in chart-sign form, six theorems losing two hypotheses each, and the oriented statements sit beside the sign-free ones as short corollaries, no proof being duplicated. A lemma whose hypothesis "the chart preserves orientation" turned out to be spurious was deleted in favour of the general one, and the sign of the frame at a zero of the cancelling map is now flatly the sign of the signed distance, where before it carried a factor depending on the orientation.
  • The self sign is a better primitive than the plan expected: it needs no chart-membership hypothesis at all, which is what makes it usable in the clopen arguments that subdivide a hop (V7.23).
  • Frames of both signs exist at every point with no case split on a global reference sign, where the oriented version had to compare against the orientation; the proof of that statement collapses from sixty-five lines to one.
94V7.21

the sign dictionary, the transition cocycle, and chains avoiding a set

not in the book

to confirmTransport along a chain of charts is multiplication by a sign, the product over the

reversing means chain sign $-1$jointly locally constant, singly notavoidance via the closed chart ball
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

Milnor's single clause of justification turns on an orientation-reversing loop: a framed point carried around one comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged. This row carries out the loop. What reversing is, Milnor leaves to the picture of a frame taken around a closed loop in $M$ and coming back the other way; and the loop must be one the point can be slid along without ever running into the other points of the framed set, which the clause does not mention, those points not being in the picture.

Reversal becomes a sign attached to the loop, and the loops can be confined to a region chosen in advance.

Verified edition V7.21

(i) The dictionary. Transport across a chart is multiplication by the Jacobian sign; the sign of a hop is the product of the two Jacobian signs of its chart at the two endpoints; the sign of a chain is the product of the signs of its hops. The sign of a composite is the product, a chain and its reverse have the same sign, transport along a chain is multiplication by its sign, and a loop reverses exactly when its sign is $-1$, so the dichotomy of V7.19 becomes arithmetic. All of this holds for arbitrary chart chains, not only ball ones, at no extra cost. (ii) The transition cocycle lemma. For two chart centres $p$, $q$ and a point $y$ in both their sources, the product of the two Jacobian signs at $z$ is locally constant in $z$ near $y$. Neither factor is, because each reads a fixed chart through the moving chart at $z$; but in the product the moving chart cancels and what is left is the determinant sign of the fixed smooth transition $\varphi_p \circ \varphi_q^{-1}$, which is continuous and invertible, hence of locally constant sign. This is the technical key, used in V7.22 and again in V7.23. (iii) Avoidance. A hop runs inside a set $C$ if the closed chart ball of its radius lies in $C$; for $C$ open and preconnected any two of its points are joined by a chain of hops running inside $C$, and such a chain automatically avoids every set disjoint from $C$ — indeed each of its closed chart balls does. Two hypotheses weaker than expected: the avoided set need not be closed, only disjoint from the region, and the region need only be preconnected. One hypothesis that had to be added: avoidance is phrased through the closed chart ball rather than the topological closure of the ball, because on a non-Hausdorff manifold the closure of a chart ball need not lie in its chart — on the line with two origins the closure of an interval about one origin contains the other. The closure form is given separately under Hausdorffness, which is free in this chapter, where the manifolds are subsets of a Euclidean space.

Milnor.jacSignMilnor.hopSignMilnor.chainSignMilnor.chainSign_compMilnor.chainSign_reverseMilnor.ChartChain.transport_eq_signActMilnor.ChartChain.reverses_iff_chainSignMilnor.eventually_jacSign_mul_jacSign_eqMilnor.ChartChain.IsBallInMilnor.exists_isBallInMilnor.exists_isBallIn_avoiding

What differs (the comparison)

  • No counterpart in the book: reversal is a picture there, and the dictionary is what makes it a quantity — after it, "this loop reverses" is the arithmetic statement that a product of $\pm 1$'s is $-1$, and the dichotomy of V7.19 can be used by calculation rather than by transporting orientations by hand.
  • The dictionary costs nothing extra in generality: a hop already carries the two membership facts that make its two Jacobian signs $\pm 1$, so everything is proved for arbitrary chart chains and nothing has to be repeated for ball chains, which are chart chains satisfying a predicate.
  • The cocycle lemma is the one genuinely analytic step in the whole orientation theory, and it has no counterpart at any point of the book. It is used twice: to extend a family of orientations across a puncture (V7.22), and to see that the sign of a chain does not notice a hop being subdivided (V7.23).
  • Two hypotheses came out weaker than the plan asked: the avoided set need not be closed, only disjoint from the region, and the region need only be preconnected, not connected. No local connectedness of $M$ and no hypothesis on the dimension is used, and $M$ may be empty.
  • One hypothesis had to be added, and the later rows must supply it: avoidance is phrased through the closed chart ball rather than the topological closure of the ball, because on a non-Hausdorff manifold the closure of a chart ball need not even lie in the source of its chart — on the line with two origins the closure of an interval about one origin contains the other. The closure form is given separately under Hausdorffness, which is free in this chapter, where the manifolds are subsets of a Euclidean space.
  • Boundarylessness of the model is assumed exactly where a chart target has to be open: to produce a ball inside that target at all, and to make the transition map smooth on an open set. The sign dictionary is free of it.
95V7.22

punctures, and the punctured dichotomy

not in the book

to confirmFor $m \ge 2$: a punctured chart ball is connected, and so is the complement of a

$2 \le m$, and the hypothesis is sharporientations extend over puncturesno separation hypothesis: $T_1$ is free
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

Milnor's single clause of justification — a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives — carries a side condition it never states, and that is this row. The point being slid is one of finitely many, and the loop must miss the others or the sliding would collide with them; Milnor's loop simply goes around $M$. Nothing in the book says that a non-orientable manifold still has a reversing loop once finitely many points have been deleted, and nothing says that the complement of a finite set is connected.

The dichotomy of V7.19 rerun on a punctured manifold, which is what makes the reversing loop available where the framed set leaves room for it.

Verified edition V7.22

The loops of V7.19 must avoid the other points of a framed $0$-manifold, so the dichotomy has to be run on the complement of a finite set. Verified, for $2 \le m$: a punctured chart ball is path connected; the complement of a finite set in a connected $M$ is connected, and nonempty as soon as $0 < m$ (a nonempty open subset of a manifold of positive dimension is infinite); a family of orientations of the tangent spaces that is ball-coherent on the complement of a finite set $P$ agrees off $P$ with an orientation of the whole of $M$; and hence the punctured dichotomy: a connected non-orientable manifold of dimension at least two has, at every point outside a given finite set, a loop of chart balls at that point missing the set and of chain sign $-1$. The extension lemma is where the cocycle lemma of V7.21 earns its keep: the value at a puncture $p$ is the value at a nearby point transported back to $p$, well defined because that assignment is locally constant on a punctured ball about $p$ — the two Jacobian signs being separately discontinuous and jointly locally constant — and because the punctured ball is connected. Hidden hypothesis: $2 \le m$, and it is sharp, not a convenience: for $m = 1$ a punctured ball is an interval minus an interior point, and $\mathbb R \setminus \{0\}$ is a disconnected complement of a finite set in a connected $1$-manifold; for $m = 0$ a punctured ball is empty. This item therefore keeps $2 \le m$, and keeps it sharply; but it is no longer a restriction on anything downstream, because it is now derivable where it is used. It was the sole source of the dimension restriction in V7.26, and V7.28 derives $2 \le m$ from non-orientability by way of the classification of one-manifolds (§10), so the theorems that appeal to this item supply the hypothesis instead of assuming it. Deviations from the expected route, both simplifications: well-definedness of the extension holds at every point, not only at the punctures, so the extension is one total function with no case split and the transport-section condition is checked at every point at once; and no separation hypothesis is needed anywhere, charts giving $T_1$ for free. What had to be added: that no point of a manifold of positive dimension is isolated, and that a locally constant function on a connected set is constant — Mathlib's version wants a discrete target, and the two orientations of a vector space carry no topology.

Milnor.isConnected_chartBall_diff_singletonMilnor.isConnected_compl_finiteMilnor.infinite_of_isOpenMilnor.IsBallCoherentOnMilnor.extend_ballCoherentMilnor.HasReversingBallLoopMilnor.hasReversingBallLoop_of_not_isOrientable

What differs (the comparison)

  • No counterpart in the book: avoiding the other points is not a matter of drawing the loop more carefully, but of rerunning the dichotomy of V7.19 on the complement of a finite set, which needs the point-set theorem that the complement is still connected and the orientation theorem that it is still non-orientable.
  • The hypothesis $2 \le m$ is sharp, not a convenience, and this row is the sole place in the development that needs it: for $m = 1$ a punctured chart ball is an interval minus an interior point, and $\mathbb R \setminus \{0\}$ is a disconnected complement of a finite set in a connected $1$-manifold; for $m = 0$ a punctured chart ball is empty. V7.26 no longer carries the hypothesis, however: since the classification of one-manifolds (V.A2) it is derived there from non-orientability, every connected one-manifold being orientable (V.A4). The statements about a chart ball that is given, with its ball inside the chart's target, need no boundarylessness; producing one does.
  • The extension theorem is where the cocycle lemma of V7.21 earns its keep. The value read at a nearby point involves two Jacobian signs which are separately discontinuous in that point and jointly locally constant, and it is the joint constancy, together with the connectedness of the punctured ball, that makes the value independent of the point it is read at.
  • Where the plan turned out wrong, twice and in the same direction. Well-definedness of the extension holds at every point, not only at the punctures, so the extension is one total function with no case split and the transport-section condition is checked at every point at once; and no separation hypothesis is needed anywhere, charts giving $T_1$ for free, which is all that the closedness of a finite set asks.
  • Two small facts had to be proved that Mathlib does not supply in usable form: that no point of a manifold of positive dimension is isolated, and that a locally constant function on a connected set is constant — Mathlib's version wants a discrete target, and the two orientations of a vector space carry no topology.
96V7.23

the slide and the flip

not in the book

to confirmFor $m \ge 1$, sliding a point of a framed $0$-manifold along a chain of chart balls

Milnor's clause, provedone hop is many movesthe loop is needed only where it starts
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

This row is Milnor's clause, and the only part of it he asserts outright: on p. 50, in the remark just after the Hopf theorem, a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged. He gives no construction. The sliding is the picture of §7, and its justification in the oriented case is the machinery of V7.13, whose every statement mentions an orientation of $M$.

The five other rows of this group are what it takes to state this one and to make its hypothesis available.

Verified edition V7.23

Let $m \ge 1$ and let $(N, v)$ be a framed $0$-manifold of a compact nonempty $M$. (i) The slide: given a chain of chart balls from a point $x_0$ of $N$ to $y$, none of whose balls meets any other point of $N$, $(N, v)$ is framed cobordant to a framed $0$-manifold with point set $(N \setminus \{x_0\}) \cup \{y\}$, the same frames elsewhere, and self sign at $y$ equal to the chain sign (V7.21) times the self sign at $x_0$. (ii) The flip: if the chain is a loop at $x_0$ of chain sign $-1$, the point set and all the other frames are literally unchanged and the self sign at $x_0$ is reversed. This is Milnor's clause. (iii) Consequently, with a reversing loop available, the frame at a point may be replaced by any tangent independent frame, and the point may be moved anywhere in its component of $M \setminus (N \setminus \{x_0\})$ with any frame: this is V7.13 with the orientation and its sign hypothesis simply deleted. Hidden hypotheses: $0 < m$, $M$ compact and nonempty, which the Theorem-B machinery behind V7.13 needs. No orientation, no connectedness, no $2 \le m$ and no Hausdorffness occur; the reversing loop is a hypothesis here, in exactly the form V7.22 produces it, so the two halves join with no glue. Where the plan was wrong: one ball hop cannot be performed by one application of V7.13, because a move needs its two points inside a quarter-radius ball whose closed ball fits in the chart, while a hop's endpoints can be almost a full diameter apart and the chart target need not contain a ball four times as large. So each hop is subdivided, and the sign must be shown not to notice the subdivision; that needs an open-set form of the cocycle lemma of V7.21 and a change-of-hub lemma, and the invariant carried across a hop turns out to be the chart sign at the hop's own hub. An improvement: the sign-free reach lemma needs the reversing loop only at the starting point, not at the target.

Milnor.Framed.exists_slideMilnor.Framed.exists_reach_anyMilnor.Framed.exists_flipMilnor.Framed.exists_frame_freeMilnor.Framed.exists_reach_free

What differs (the comparison)

  • This is the one row of the six with something in the book to compare, and the comparison is one clause against a file: what Milnor asserts is the flip, and the row is the slide of which the flip is the loop case, together with the bookkeeping that says what the sign of the frame does along the way.
  • Where the plan turned out wrong: a ball hop is too coarse to be performed by one move. A move (V7.13) needs its two points inside the ball of a quarter of a radius $R$ whose closed ball fits in the chart's target, while the endpoints of a ball hop need only lie in the ball itself and can be almost a full diameter apart, the target containing no ball four times as large. So each hop has to be subdivided, and the sign shown not to notice the subdivision: that needs an open-set form of the cocycle lemma of V7.21 and a change-of-hub lemma, and the invariant carried across a hop turns out to be the chart sign at the hop's own hub.
  • An improvement on the plan in the other direction: the sign-free reach lemma needs the reversing loop only at the point that moves, not at its destination. That is what lets the parity induction of V7.25 finish by carrying one point onto one point.
  • What the slide asserts about the frame is a self sign, not a sign against an orientation; there being no orientation to be had, the bookkeeping is the dictionary of V7.20 and V7.21 throughout, and the hypothesis relating the two frames that V7.13 carries has no analogue.
  • Hypotheses: $0 < m$, and compactness and nonemptiness of $M$, which the Theorem-B machinery behind the moves needs. No orientation, no connectedness, no $2 \le m$, no Hausdorffness — the closed chart ball of V7.21 is used in place of the closure of a ball, and each move gets the closure condition it needs from its own good ball.
97V7.24

sign-free cancellation

not in the book

to confirmFor $m \ge 1$ and with reversing loops available off every finite set: two framed

no condition on the two framesshorter than the signed versionthe case split disappears
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

Milnor's single clause of justification ends and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged, and this row is the place where it does not. The oriented endgame cancels a point of sign $+1$ against a point of sign $-1$ (V7.14), sorting the points by sign to find such a pair (V7.15), and that is what makes the signed count the complete invariant; a framed point slid around a reversing loop comes back with its sign changed, so with no signs to be had the step has to become the cancellation of any two neighbouring points. Milnor does not remark on the difference.

In the book this is the sentence that the framed cobordism class is determined by the count; the mod $2$ version needs a different cancellation, not merely a different count.

Verified edition V7.24

Let $(N, v)$ be a framed $0$-manifold of $M$, as in V7.23, and let reversing ball loops be available off every finite set. (i) Two framed $0$-manifolds with the same point set and the same frames away from two distinct points are framed cobordant, with no condition relating the frames at those two points; (ii) any two distinct points of $N$, one in the closure of the component of the other, can be cancelled — V7.14 with its opposite-sign hypothesis deleted outright; (iii) if $N$ has two distinct points and $M$ is connected, some two of them are neighbours in that sense, which is V7.15 with the signs deleted, the partition handed to the neighbour lemma being $\{x\} \sqcup (N \setminus \{x\})$ instead of a partition by sign. Hidden hypotheses: $0 < m$, $M$ compact and nonempty; no orientation and no $2 \le m$. Where the plan was wrong, and this is the point of the remark: it prescribed choosing an arrival frame of the opposite sign and replacing the old case split by a new one. Neither is needed. The cancellation lemma supplies its own two frames, of chart signs $+1$ and $-1$, and "frames are free at two points" connects any configuration to that one, so the arriving frame may be arbitrary and the case split disappears rather than being replaced. The sign-free cancel step is therefore shorter than the signed one.

Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_frames_freeMilnor.Framed.cancel_step_freeMilnor.Framed.exists_neighbour_pair

What differs (the comparison)

  • No counterpart in the book: "the rest applies unchanged" is the claim that this cancellation exists, and it is the one place where dropping the orientation is not a restatement but a different theorem — any two neighbours, rather than a plus and a minus.
  • Where the plan turned out wrong, and this is the point of Milnor's remark: it prescribed choosing, on arrival, a frame whose chart sign is the negative of the sign at the point to be cancelled against, and replacing the old case split by a new one. Neither is needed. The cancelling map supplies its own two frames, of chart signs $+1$ and $-1$, and "frames are free at two points" connects any configuration to that one, so the arriving frame may be arbitrary and the case split disappears rather than being replaced. The sign-free cancel step is therefore shorter than the signed one it replaces.
  • What is deleted is a hypothesis, not a case. V7.14 asks the two signs to be opposite, and the oriented endgame (V7.16) sorts the points of the framed set by sign before applying the neighbour lemma (V7.15), which carries no sign itself; here neither statement mentions a sign, and the neighbour lemma is applied to the partition $\{x\} \sqcup (N \setminus \{x\})$, which exists as soon as there are two points.
  • The reversing loop is carried in the uniform shape "at every point off every finite set", the finite set always being instantiated to the other points of the framed $0$-manifold, so that nothing here depends on how the loop is obtained and V7.26 can discharge the hypothesis once from V7.22.
98V7.25

the count modulo 2, and the parity classification

not in the book

to confirmFor $m \ge 1$ and $M$ compact connected, with reversing loops available off every

parity is a complete invariantno gathering step in the inductionthe bridge ignores the framing
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, p. 50

Milnor's single clause of justification concludes that, a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop coming back with the sign of its frame changed, only the number of points modulo $2$ survives. This row carries out that conclusion. For an oriented $M$ Milnor writes on p. 50 that it is "not difficult to see" that the framed cobordism class of a framed $0$-manifold is determined by the integer $\sum \operatorname{sgn}(x)$ (V7.16), and that it is clear that this integer is the degree (V7.12); the mod $2$ statements are those two sentences with the integer replaced by the parity of the number of points, and neither is written down.

The invariant, that it is complete, and the bridge to the degree modulo $2$ of §4 (V4.7).

Verified edition V7.25

The count modulo 2 of a framed $0$-manifold is the cardinality of its point set read in $\mathbb Z/2$; no orientation occurs in the definition, in contrast with V7.12. Verified: (i) two framed $0$-manifolds of a connected $M$ with the same count modulo $2$ are framed cobordant, given reversing ball loops off every finite set; (ii) the count modulo $2$ of the Pontryagin manifold of $f : M \to S^m$ at a regular value is the mod 2 degree of $f$ (V4.8) — both are the parity of $\#f^{-1}(y)$, and, unlike V7.12, this needs nothing of the framing, so the frame may be any independent tuple rather than a positive basis. This is Milnor's statement on p. 50 that the framed cobordism class is determined by the number of points modulo $2$, and it is V7.16 with the signed count replaced by the parity. Hidden hypotheses: $0 < m$, $M$ compact, nonempty and connected; $2 \le m$ does not appear, being needed only to produce the loops — and since V7.27 the loops are available on a connected non-orientable $M$ with no dimension hypothesis whatever, so the non-orientable form of (i), Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_card_parity, which is where the reversing loops are actually discharged and which is stated with V7.26, carries none either. Deviation (route of proof): the signed induction of V7.16 can cancel only while both signs occur and needs a separate gathering argument when they do not; here the induction runs on the cruder condition "two or more points", there is no gathering step at all, and it stops when each side has at most one point, which is then carried onto the other by V7.23.

Milnor.Framed.countMod2Milnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_countMod2_eqMilnor.Framed.countMod2_pontryagin

What differs (the comparison)

  • No counterpart in the book: Milnor states the signed versions and leaves these to "the rest applies unchanged". The count is a different definition, mentioning no orientation, and the classification is a different induction.
  • Where the plan turned out wrong: the signed induction of V7.16 can cancel only while both signs occur, and needs a separate gathering argument to bring the remaining points of one sign together when they do not. Here there is no gathering step at all. The induction runs on the cruder condition "two or more points", cancels any two neighbours by V7.24, and stops when each side has at most one point, equal counts then forcing equal cardinalities; one point is carried onto one point by the reach lemma of V7.23, which is exactly where that row's improvement — the loop needed only at the point that moves — is used.
  • The bridge asks less than its oriented counterpart. The point set of the Pontryagin manifold is $f^{-1}(y)$ and the mod $2$ degree is the parity of its cardinality at any regular value, so the frame may be an arbitrary independent tuple, where the signed identity of V7.12 needs a positively oriented basis of the tangent space of the sphere at $y$.
  • The reversing loop is a hypothesis here as in V7.24, discharged only in V7.26; and the two statements rest on different hypotheses, connectedness of $M$ being used by the classification and not by the bridge.
99V7.26

Milnor's theorem of p. 50

restated

to confirmLet $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a compact nonempty connected non-orientable

no dimension hypothesis$M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, compacthomotopy means smooth homotopy
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §7, Theorem, p. 50

If $M$ is connected but non-orientable, two maps $M \to S^m$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if they have the same degree modulo $2$. Milnor states it in a remark just after the Hopf theorem, and gives the reason in one clause: a framed point slid around an orientation-reversing loop comes back with the sign of its frame changed, so that only the number of points modulo $2$ survives, and the rest of the framed-cobordism machinery applies unchanged.

The degree modulo $2$ is the one of §4 (V4.7); with no orientation of $M$ there is no signed degree to be had.

Verified edition V7.26

Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ be a closed connected non-orientable submanifold of dimension $m$ (compact, nonempty, in the sense of V7.0 and with the manifold structure of Chapter 2, and non-orientable in the sense of V7.18). Then two smooth maps $M \to S^m$ are smoothly homotopic if and only if they have the same degree modulo $2$. This is Milnor's theorem at the end of §7, the counterpart for non-orientable $M$ of the Hopf theorem V7.17. The easy half is the homotopy invariance of the mod 2 degree (V4.8, the second assertion of the Theorem of §4) transcribed into the homotopy relation of this chapter: it needs no orientation of any kind, no connectedness and nothing of the dimension beyond $0 < m$, which is only what the mod 2 degree needs of its target. The substantial half is Milnor's remark, and runs through Sard (V3.3) for a regular value of each map, a positively oriented basis of the sphere at each — orientations of the target sphere, which the Pontryagin construction and the converse of Theorem B require and which are the only orientations in the whole argument; the source carries none and is assumed not to have one — the two Pontryagin manifolds (V7.1), the parity bridge and the parity classification (V7.25), and the converse half of Theorem B (V7.11), which is itself orientation-free. No hidden hypothesis of dimension: the theorem as stated has none at all. Each half wants $0 < m$, which is all the mod 2 degree needs of its target, and each half gets it from non-orientability along with everything else, so the dimension does not appear among the hypotheses of the two-way statement. Earlier editions of this statement assumed $2 \le m$, which is mathematically vacuous: a connected $1$-manifold is diffeomorphic to the circle or to an interval and all of those are orientable, so a connected non-orientable manifold has dimension at least two and non-orientability already implies it. It was carried only because the classification of one-manifolds was not formalized in this project. It now is (§10), the deduction is V7.28, and since the hypothesis enters at exactly one point, the appeal to V7.22, each of the three statements here that mentions non-orientability derives it there in one line instead of assuming it. Deviations: homotopy means smooth homotopy (V4, convention), and $M$ is a submanifold of Euclidean space, as in all of §6–§7.

Milnor.Framed.hopf_homotopic_mod2Milnor.Framed.smoothHomotopic_of_degMod2_eqMilnor.Framed.framedCobordant_of_card_parityMilnor.Framed.degMod2_eq_of_smoothHomotopic_sphMilnor.degMod2_eq_of_smoothHomotopic

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal statement carries no dimension hypothesis, and neither does Milnor's. It used to carry $2 \le m$, which the punctured stage needs (V7.22) and which is empty mathematics, a connected $1$-manifold being the circle or an interval and all of those orientable. The hypothesis has now been removed, not excused: the classification of one-manifolds (V.A2) and the orientability of every connected one-manifold (V.A4) make non-orientability of a connected $M$ imply $2 \le m$, so it is derived in one line at the single point where it is used. It is gone from the three statements that carried it — the equivalence, its substantial half and the parity classification. The easy half keeps an explicit $0 < m$, as it must: it assumes neither connectedness nor non-orientability, so it has nothing to derive a dimension bound from, and $0 < m$ is only what the mod 2 degree asks of its target.
  • $M$ is a compact nonempty submanifold of a Euclidean space, as in all of §§6–7, and homotopy means smooth homotopy, a smooth map $M \times \mathbb R \to S^m$; Milnor's manifolds are subsets of Euclidean space and his homotopies are smooth, so neither restricts the mathematics.
  • The clause Milnor offers as the reason is the whole of the sign-free machinery, and it is six items long: a sign of a frame that mentions no orientation (V7.20), the sign of a chain and the chains that avoid a finite set (V7.21), the punctured dichotomy that produces the reversing loop (V7.22), the slide and the flip, which are the clause itself (V7.23), the cancellation of any two neighbours (V7.24), and the parity count with its classification (V7.25). What the theorem itself adds is the discharge of the reversing-loop hypothesis and the two halves of the equivalence.
  • The easy half assumes neither connectedness nor non-orientability, and no orientation anywhere, while the classification by parity assumes both; the two directions of the equivalence rest on very different hypotheses.
109V7.27

orientations pulled back, the four models of the Appendix oriented, and the orientability of every connected one-manifold

to confirmAn invertible linear map between spaces of the same finite dimension carries exactly one

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V7.27

Five statements, whose only purpose is the next item. (i) Pullback. For an invertible continuous linear map $T : E \to F$ between spaces of dimension $n$ and an orientation $b$ of $F$, exactly one orientation of $E$ is carried by $T$ to $b$. (ii) Two cheap orientations. If all the charts of $M$ are the same partial homeomorphism, every transition is the identity and a constant family of orientations is an orientation in the sense of V5.0; and every $0$-dimensional manifold is orientable whatever its charts, because in dimension $0$ every linear map preserves every orientation — the only tuple to test is the empty one. (iii) Transport along a map with invertible derivative. Let $\Phi : M \to N$ be smooth between manifolds of the same dimension $n$, let $N$ carry an orientation, and let every $d\Phi_x$ be invertible. Then $x \mapsto \operatorname{orPull}(o_N(\Phi x), d\Phi_x)$ is an orientation of $M$; $\Phi$ need be neither injective nor surjective nor a local diffeomorphism, so in particular orientability travels backwards along a diffeomorphism. (iv) The four models of V.J are orientable. The line and the closed half-line by constant families, through (ii). The unit interval not by a constant family: Mathlib's manifold structure on $[0,1]$ has two charts, $z \mapsto z$ on $[0,1)$ and $z \mapsto 1 - z$ on $(0,1]$, whose transition reverses orientation, so the family has to flip at the right-hand endpoint, and that one transition derivative has to be computed. And the circle not through its own charts but through the unit circle $S^1 \subseteq \mathbb R^2$, which Chapter 8 already orients as the boundary of the disk (V8.0's sphOrStd): $S^1$ is a nonempty compact Hausdorff connected boundaryless smooth one-manifold, so V.H hands over a diffeomorphism $S^1 \to$ Circle and (iii) carries the orientation across. (v) Hence every connected Hausdorff $\sigma$-compact smooth one-manifold over a boundaryless model is orientable: the classification V.J makes it the circle if it is compact and $\mathbb R$ if it is not — the two interval models are excluded because the boundary of such an $M$ is empty — and orientability travels back along the diffeomorphism by (iii). Hidden hypothesis: every transport statement, hence (iii), (iv) and (v), assumes the model of the source boundaryless. It is used in exactly one way: a chart target of the source has to be open in the model space, so that the map read in charts is defined on an open set and has a genuine derivative there, continuous in the point. For a model with boundary the argument would have to be rerun with fderivWithin along the model range, and it is not. So (v) is available for boundaryless models only, even though all four models of (iv), the two with boundary included, are oriented; nothing in the project needs more, the manifolds of Chapters 7 and 8 being compact slice submanifolds of a Euclidean space, whose model is boundaryless. Deviation (route of proof): (iii) is V5.1's local constancy of the sign read backwards. Writing $\Phi$ in the charts at $x_0$ and $\Phi x_0$ as $g$, the transition of $N$ at $\Phi x$ composed with $d\Phi_x$ equals $dg$ composed with the transition of $M$ at $x$; the left side has sign $1$ by the definition of the pulled-back family and the compatibility of the orientation of $N$, and the right side splits as the sign of $dg$ — locally constant, and $1$ at $x_0$, where $dg = d\Phi_{x_0}$ — times the sign of the transition of $M$, which is therefore $1$.
