Verified edition V7.28

Let $M$ be a connected Hausdorff $\sigma$-compact smooth manifold of dimension $m$ over a boundaryless model. If $M$ is not orientable, then $2 \le m$. Dimension $0$ is excluded by the second clause of V7.27 and dimension $1$ by the last. The second form is the same statement in the vocabulary of Chapters 7 and 8 — $M$ a compact connected slice submanifold of a Euclidean space, charted on $\mathbb R^m$ — and there every side hypothesis comes free: $M$ is Hausdorff as a subspace of a metric space, $\sigma$-compact because it is compact, and its model $\mathbb R^m$ is boundaryless. This is the item that deletes $2 \le m$ from V7.26. The three statements there that assume non-orientability now derive the dimension bound from it, in one line each, at the single point where V7.22 wants it; V7.22 itself keeps the hypothesis, sharply, as a statement about punctured balls. Hidden hypotheses: the ones listed, which are exactly those of V7.27(v). Deviation: none from Milnor, who states his theorem of p. 50 with no dimension condition at all; this item is what makes the formal statement match his.

Chapter 8 (Milnor §8, the seventeen problems)

63V8.0

tools built for the chapter

not in the book

to confirmThe sphere is oriented as the boundary of the disk (outward normal first); the preimage

local zero sets of submersionsframe-first orientationsdegrees with compact preimages
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

The book has no counterpart here. Writing the problems "for the reader", Milnor uses without comment that a set cut out near each of its points by a submersion is a manifold with the expected tangent space, that a product of oriented manifolds is oriented by a positive basis of the first factor followed by one of the second, that $S^n$ is oriented as the boundary of the disk, and that the local degree of §5 behaves well as soon as the relevant preimages are compact.

§2 proves only the global case, that $f^{-1}(y)$ is a manifold for a regular value $y$ of a map defined on all of $M$.

Verified edition V8.0

Several general facts that Mathlib lacks were proved once and used throughout. (i) Local zero sets: let $M$ be a manifold without boundary modelled on a finite-dimensional space $E$. A subset of $M$ which near each of its points is the zero set of a map to a space $F$, smooth on an open neighbourhood and with surjective derivative at the points of the subset, is a submanifold modelled on any $Z$ with $\dim Z + \dim F = \dim E$ (Milnor.IsSliceSubmanifold.of_locally_preimage), and its tangent space at a point is the kernel of the derivative of any such local defining map (Milnor.IsSliceSubmanifold.range_mfderiv_val_eq_ker, Milnor.IsSliceSubmanifold.mem_range_mfderiv_val_iff_of_local). (ii) Product orientations: the orientation of $M \times N$ given by a positive basis of $TM$ followed by one of $TN$, the sign of a product of maps, and the sign $(-1)^{mn}$ of the swap (Milnor.Orient.prod, Milnor.signAt_prodMap, Milnor.signAt_swap). (iii) Frame orientations: a submanifold of Euclidean space with a continuous complementary frame is oriented by "frame first, then tangent vectors"; in particular the sphere $S^n$ is oriented as the boundary of the disk, outward normal first (Milnor.Sphere.sphOrStd), and preimages of regular values in spheres are oriented as in §5 (Milnor.Problems.preimOr; frame first differs from §5's tangent vectors first by $(-1)^{dp}$, which is $+1$ in the case $p = d + 1$ used below). The orientation sphOr of V5.9 was built from a basis chosen by the axiom of choice, and is the boundary orientation of the exterior of the ball relative to that basis; for $n \ge 1$ it agrees with Milnor's orientation sphOrStd up to a global sign (Milnor.Sphere.sphOrStd_eq_or_neg). Linking numbers and the Hopf invariant use Milnor's. (iv) Local degrees for maps with compact preimages: for $M$ and $N$ Hausdorff, if some neighbourhood $V$ of a regular value $y$ has compact preimage $f^{-1}(V)$, then nearby values are regular and the local degree is constant near $y$ (Milnor.IsRegularValue.eventually_degAt_eq_of_isCompact); if moreover $M$ is second countable, $F$ is a smooth homotopy, $y$ is a regular value of both end maps and $\{(x, t) : F(x, t) \in V,\ 0 \le t \le 1\}$ is compact for some neighbourhood $V$ of $y$, the two end maps have the same local degree at $y$ (Milnor.degAt_eq_of_homotopy_of_isCompact). (v) Genericity: the image of a second countable manifold of smaller dimension has dense complement, and is meagre; the complement of the diagonal in $S^p \times S^p$ is connected ($p \ge 1$); a function locally constant near every point on a dense subset of a connected set is constant (Milnor.Problems.dense_compl_range_of_finrank_lt, Milnor.Problems.isConnected_offDiag_sph, Milnor.Problems.eq_of_locallyConst_on_dense). As in Chapters 4 and 5, the statements of (iv), and those of (v) about images, require the model spaces of source and target to lie in a common universe (§1).

What differs (the comparison)

  • §2 makes a global level set a manifold; the local criterion asks only for a defining map near each point, which is what Problems 8 to 11 actually supply.
  • The sphere orientation of §5 was built from a basis chosen by the axiom of choice; for $n \ge 1$ it agrees with Milnor's only up to a global sign, so linking numbers needed a new one.
  • Putting the complementary frame first, rather than the tangent vectors as in §5, changes the orientation of a preimage by $(-1)^{dp}$, which is $+1$ in the case $p = d + 1$ used later.
  • The degree results replace compactness of the source by compactness of $f^{-1}(V)$, and add Hausdorff, second countability and a common universe for the two model spaces.
64V8.1

Problem 1, the degree of a composition

hypotheses explicit

to confirmFor smooth maps $f : M \to N$ and $g : N \to P$ between compact oriented boundaryless

$N$ compact, so $\deg g$ exists$P$ Hausdorff, not compactpreconnected, so possibly empty
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 1

The degree of a composition $g \circ f$ is the product $(\deg g)(\deg f)$.

One line, with no hypotheses named: the degrees are those of §5, so the manifolds are the compact oriented ones of that section.

Verified edition V8.1

Let $f : M \to N$ and $g : N \to P$ be smooth maps between oriented boundaryless manifolds of the same dimension $n$, with $M$ and $N$ compact (Hausdorff, second countable), $P$ Hausdorff, and $N$, $P$ connected (and the model spaces in a common universe, §1). Then $\deg(g \circ f) = \deg g \cdot \deg f$. Proof as in the solutions: a point $z$ which is a regular value of both $g$ and $g \circ f$ exists (regular values of $g$ are open and dense, those of $g \circ f$ dense); every $y \in g^{-1}(z)$ is then a regular value of $f$ (isRegularValue_of_isRegularValue_comp); the sum over $(g \circ f)^{-1}(z)$ splits over the fibres of $f$ and the signs multiply. Hidden hypothesis: $N$ compact (so that $\deg g$ is defined).

Milnor.Problems.deg_comp

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor names no hypotheses; the formal statement needs $N$ compact for $\deg g$ to exist, and uses that compactness again to make the regular values of $g$ open.
  • $P$ is only assumed Hausdorff and preconnected: it is neither compact nor second countable, and it may be empty, in which case both sides are $0$ by the definition of the degree.
  • Preconnected is weaker than Milnor's connected, so the empty target is covered rather than excluded.
  • The three model spaces must lie in one universe, a condition of the formal degree of §5 and not of the mathematics.
65V8.2

Problem 2, polynomials

restated

to confirmA complex polynomial of degree $n \ge 1$ induces a smooth map of the Riemann sphere of

Riemann sphere, not the Gauss sphereneeds $\deg P \ge 1$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 2

Every complex polynomial of degree $n$ gives rise to a smooth self-map of the Gauss sphere $S^2$ of degree $n$.

The sphere is $S^2 \subseteq \mathbb R^3$, identified with $\mathbb C \cup \{\infty\}$ by stereographic projection as in §1.

Verified edition V8.2

The Riemann sphere carries the orientation given by the standard orientation of $\mathbb C = \mathbb R^2$ in both charts $z$ and $1/z$ (well defined: the chart change has complex derivative $w \ne 0$, whose real determinant is $|w|^2 > 0$, orSign_complexMul). For a complex polynomial $P$ of degree $n \ge 1$, the induced smooth map of the Riemann sphere has degree $n$. Proof: at a regular value $y \in \mathbb C$ outside the finite set of critical values, the preimages are the $n$ distinct simple roots of $P - y$, each with derivative multiplication by $P'(z) \ne 0$, of sign $+1$. Deviation: the Riemann sphere replaces the Gauss sphere $S^2$, as in V1.6.

Milnor.Problems.riemannOrMilnor.Problems.deg_polyMap

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $S^2 \subseteq \mathbb R^3$ is replaced throughout by the Riemann sphere with the charts $z$ and $1/z$, as already in §1; no identification of the two is formalized.
  • The hypothesis $\deg P \ge 1$ is named and used: for a constant $P$ the extension by $P(\infty) = \infty$ is not even continuous at $\infty$.
  • The orientation of the Riemann sphere has to be constructed before the statement can be made, since the sphere is not presented as a level set.
66V8.3

Problem 3

stronger

to confirmMaps $f, g : X \to S^p$ with $\|f(x) - g(x)\| < 2$ for all $x$ are homotopic, and smoothly

$X$ any topological spacemodel space may be infinite-dimensionalhomotopy on $X \times \mathbb R$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 3

If two maps $f$ and $g$ from $X$ to $S^p$ satisfy $\|f(x) - g(x)\| < 2$ for all $x$, then $f$ is homotopic to $g$, the homotopy being smooth if $f$ and $g$ are.

He does not say what $X$ is; in the smooth half it must be a manifold.

Verified edition V8.3

If $f, g : X \to S^p$ satisfy $\|f(x) - g(x)\| < 2$ for all $x$, then $f$ and $g$ are homotopic ($X$ any topological space, continuous maps); if $X$ is a manifold and $f, g$ are smooth, they are smoothly homotopic. The homotopy is the normalized chord $((1-t)f + tg)/\|(1-t)f + tg\|$.

Milnor.Problems.homotopic_of_norm_sub_lt_twoMilnor.Problems.smoothHomotopic_of_norm_sub_lt_two

What differs (the comparison)

  • The continuous half assumes nothing about $X$ beyond a topology, where Milnor's $X$ is a manifold.
  • The smooth half allows any model space, finite-dimensional or not, with or without boundary, and asks neither that $X$ be Hausdorff nor that it be second countable.
  • Smooth homotopy here means a smooth map on $X \times \mathbb R$, so the chord is reparametrized by a transition function that is $0$ near $t = 0$ and $1$ near $t = 1$.

Problem 4

hypotheses explicit

to confirmEvery continuous map from a compact manifold $X \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ to $S^p$ is a uniform

$X$ compact in $\mathbb R^{K+1}$homotopy on $X \times \mathbb R$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 4

If $X$ is compact, every continuous map $X \to S^p$ can be uniformly approximated by a smooth map; and two smooth maps $X \to S^p$ that are continuously homotopic are smoothly homotopic.

Compactness of $X$ is the only hypothesis he names.

Verified edition V8.4

For a compact manifold $X \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$: every continuous map $X \to S^p$ is a uniform limit of smooth maps, and two smooth maps $X \to S^p$ which are continuously homotopic are smoothly homotopic. The approximation theorem underneath is proved for any compact subset of a finite-dimensional space (Stone–Weierstrass for restrictions of smooth functions).

Milnor.Problems.exists_contDiff_approx_of_isCompactMilnor.Problems.exists_smooth_approxMilnor.Problems.smoothHomotopic_of_homotopic

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's $X$ is any compact manifold; the formal $X$ is a compact subset of $\mathbb R^{K+1}$ given by slice charts with model $\mathbb R^m$, the setting of §6 and §7.
  • Smooth homotopy means a smooth map on $X \times \mathbb R$, so the approximated homotopy is composed with a transition function before its ends are compared with $f$ and $g$ by Problem 3.
  • The approximation theorem is stated for a compact subset of a finite-dimensional space, although the exposition records that finite dimensionality of the source is not used in its proof.

Problem 5

narrower

to confirmIf $m < p$, every smooth map from a boundaryless $m$-manifold to $S^p$ is smoothly

continuous case needs $M$ compactsecond countable sourcemodel in the lowest universe
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 5

If $m < p$, every map $M^m \to S^p$ is homotopic to a constant.

One line: he says neither that $M$ is compact nor whether the maps are smooth.

Verified edition V8.5

If $m < p$, every smooth map from a second countable boundaryless $m$-manifold to $S^p$ is smoothly homotopic to a constant, and every continuous map from a compact $m$-manifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ to $S^p$ is homotopic to a constant. Proof: by Brown's theorem a smooth map misses a point $y$ (a regular value cannot be attained when $m < p$); stereographic projection from $y$ contracts; the continuous case follows by V8.4 and V8.3. Formal note: because Brown's theorem is applied with the sphere as target, the model space of the $m$-manifold is required to lie in the lowest universe (§1).

Milnor.Problems.exists_smoothHomotopic_const_of_ltMilnor.Problems.exists_homotopic_const_of_lt

What differs (the comparison)

  • The continuous statement is proved only for a compact $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, because it passes through the approximation of Problem 4; Milnor asks for no compactness.
  • The smooth statement needs $X$ second countable, since Brown's theorem supplies the missed point, but it needs no compactness at all.
  • Because Brown's theorem is applied with $S^p$ as target, the model space of the $m$-manifold is required to lie in the lowest universe: a formal side condition, not a mathematical one.

Any map $S^n \to S^n$ whose degree is different from $(-1)^{n+1}$ must have a fixed point.

He labels the problem "Brouwer" and does not say the map is smooth.

Verified edition V8.6

A smooth map $f : S^n \to S^n$ ($n \ge 1$) with $\deg f \ne (-1)^{n+1}$ has a fixed point. Deviation: stated for smooth maps, for which the degree is defined in the formalization.

Milnor.Problems.exists_fixedPoint_of_deg_ne

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal degree is defined only for smooth maps, so the statement is about smooth $f$, whereas Milnor's "any map" covers continuous ones.
  • The hypothesis $n \ge 1$ is needed so that $S^n$ is connected, as the degree of §5 requires; $S^0$ is excluded.
  • The degree is taken for the orientation of §5 on both sides; reversing that orientation on both sides changes no degree, so the value $(-1)^{n+1}$ is unaffected.

Any map $S^n \to S^n$ of odd degree must carry some pair of antipodal points into a pair of antipodal points.

Verified edition V8.7

A smooth map $f : S^n \to S^n$ ($n \ge 1$) of odd degree satisfies $f(-x) = -f(x)$ for some $x$. The key lemma: a smooth map with $h(-x) = h(x)$ has even degree (preimages of a regular value come in antipodal pairs).

Milnor.Problems.exists_antipodal_of_odd_degMilnor.Problems.even_deg_of_even_map

What differs (the comparison)

  • The parity lemma counts the preimage modulo $2$; the signs at $x$ and at $-x$ are never compared with each other.

Problem 8, products

stronger

to confirmFor manifolds $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$, $N \subseteq \mathbb R^l$, the product $M \times N$ is a

ambient spaces any finite-dimensionalmanifold structure also proved
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 8

Given smooth manifolds $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ and $N \subseteq \mathbb R^l$, the tangent space $T(M \times N)_{(x,y)}$ is equal to $TM_x \times TN_y$.

He asks only for the tangent space; that $M \times N$ is a manifold is taken for granted.

Verified edition V8.8

If $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ and $N \subseteq \mathbb R^l$ are smooth manifolds, then $M \times N \subseteq \mathbb R^k \times \mathbb R^l$ is a smooth manifold of dimension $m + n$, and at every point its tangent space is $T(M \times N)_{(x,y)} = TM_x \times TN_y$. Proof: near $(x, y)$ the set $M \times N$ is the common zero set of the normal coordinates of slice charts of $M$ and of $N$, a map with surjective derivative; a set which is locally the zero set of such a map is a manifold whose tangent space is the kernel of the derivative (a general criterion proved once for the chapter, Milnor.IsSliceSubmanifold.of_locally_preimage). Formal setting: the ambient spaces may be any finite-dimensional real spaces.

Milnor.Problems.isSliceSubmanifold_prodMilnor.Problems.range_mfd_val_prodMilnor.Problems.IsSliceSubmanifold.finrank_add

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor asks only for the tangent space; the formal statement also proves that $M \times N$ is a manifold, which he takes as known.
  • The ambient spaces are arbitrary finite-dimensional real normed spaces, not only $\mathbb R^k$ and $\mathbb R^l$.
  • Near a point $M \times N$ is presented as the common zero set of the normal parts of two slice charts, and both conclusions are read off that one map; no parametrization is built.
  • The identity $\dim Z + \dim W = \dim E$ for a slice presentation needs the submanifold to be nonempty, and is stated with that hypothesis.

Problem 9, graphs

stronger

to confirmThe graph of a smooth $f : M \to N$ is a manifold of dimension $m$ whose tangent space at

ambient spaces any finite-dimensionalcomplement $W_1 \times E_2$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 9

The graph $\Gamma$ of a smooth map $f : M \to N$, the set of $(x, y) \in M \times N$ with $f(x) = y$, is a smooth manifold, and the tangent space $T\Gamma_{(x,y)} \subseteq TM_x \times TN_y$ is the graph of the linear map $df_x$.

Verified edition V8.9

For a smooth map $f : M \to N$, the graph $\Gamma = \{(x, f(x))\} \subseteq \mathbb R^k \times \mathbb R^l$ is a smooth manifold of the dimension of $M$, and its tangent space at $(x, f(x))$ is $\{(v, df_x v) : v \in TM_x\}$, the graph of $df_x$. Proof: near a point, $\Gamma$ is the zero set of $(a, b) \mapsto (\text{normal coordinates of } a,\ b - \tilde f(a))$, where $\tilde f$ is a smooth extension of $f$ to an open subset of $\mathbb R^k$; this map has surjective derivative, and its kernel is the graph of $df_x$.

Milnor.Problems.graphMilnor.Problems.isSliceSubmanifold_graphMilnor.Problems.range_mfd_val_graph

What differs (the comparison)

  • The ambient spaces are arbitrary finite-dimensional real normed spaces; the model and complement of $N$ are not even required to be finite-dimensional.
  • $\Gamma$ is presented as a subset of $E_1 \times E_2$ with complement $W_1 \times E_2$, rather than as a subset of the product manifold $M \times N$.
  • The tangent space is computed as the kernel of the derivative of one defining function; the formal proof never shows that $x \mapsto (x, f(x))$ is a diffeomorphism onto $\Gamma$.

Problem 10, the tangent bundle

stronger

to confirmThe tangent bundle space $TM$ is a manifold of dimension $2m$; a smooth $f$ induces a

ambient space any finite-dimensional$df$ reads $v$ in chart coordinates
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 10

Given $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$, the tangent bundle space $TM = \{(x, v) \in M \times \mathbb R^k : v \in TM_x\}$ is again a smooth manifold; any smooth $f : M \to N$ gives rise to a smooth $df : TM \to TN$, with $d(\mathrm{identity}) = \mathrm{identity}$ and $d(g \circ f) = (dg) \circ (df)$.

Verified edition V8.10

For a smooth manifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ of dimension $m$, the tangent bundle space $TM = \{(x, v) \in M \times \mathbb R^k : v \in TM_x\}$ is a smooth manifold of dimension $2m$. A smooth map $f : M \to N$ induces $df : TM \to TN$, $(x, v) \mapsto (f(x), df_x(v))$, which is smooth, with $d(\mathrm{identity}) = \mathrm{identity}$ and $d(g \circ f) = dg \circ df$. Proof: near a point, $TM$ is the zero set of $(a, w) \mapsto (\text{normal coordinates of } a,\ \text{normal part of } d\varphi_a(w))$ for a slice chart $\varphi$, a map with surjective derivative; $df$ is smooth because $f$ has a smooth local extension to an open subset of $\mathbb R^k$; the composition rule is the chain rule. Formal setting: the ambient spaces may be any finite-dimensional real spaces; the identity rule needs no smoothness hypothesis.

Milnor.Problems.tangentBundleSetMilnor.Problems.isSliceSubmanifold_tangentBundleMilnor.Problems.tangentMapMilnor.Problems.contMDiff_tangentMapMilnor.Problems.tangentMap_idMilnor.Problems.tangentMap_comp

What differs (the comparison)

  • The ambient space is an arbitrary finite-dimensional real normed space rather than $\mathbb R^k$, and $M$ comes with a named model $Z$ and complement $W$.
  • A tangent vector $v$ lies in $E$ while $df_x$ acts on the model space $Z$, so the formal $df$ first writes $v$ in chart coordinates; this is well defined because $d\iota_x$ is injective.
  • The identity rule carries no smoothness and no finite-dimensionality hypothesis; the chain rule needs both maps smooth.

Problem 11, the normal bundle

hypotheses explicit

to confirmThe normal bundle space $E$ of $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ is a manifold of dimension $k$; for

$M$ nonemptydimension identity $m + w = k$second part only for $k = K + 1$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 11

The normal bundle space $E = \{(x, v) \in M \times \mathbb R^k : v \perp TM_x\}$ is a smooth manifold; and if $M$ is compact and boundaryless, $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ carries the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood of $M \times 0$ in $E$ diffeomorphically onto the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood $N_\varepsilon$ of $M$ in $\mathbb R^k$.

He asks the reader to compare the product neighbourhood theorem of §7, and does not say how $\varepsilon$ is found.

Verified edition V8.11

For a smooth manifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ of dimension $m$, the normal bundle space $E = \{(x, v) \in M \times \mathbb R^k : v \perp TM_x\}$ is a smooth manifold of dimension $k$. If $M$ is compact and nonempty, there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ maps $\{(x, v) \in E : \|v\| < \varepsilon\}$ diffeomorphically onto the $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood $N_\varepsilon = \{y : \operatorname{dist}(y, M) < \varepsilon\}$. Proof: near a point, $E$ is the zero set of $(a, v) \mapsto (\text{normal coordinates of } a,\ \text{tangential components of } v)$, a map with surjective derivative. The sum map has invertible derivative along the zero section, it is injective on a uniformly thin tube because $M$ is compact (finitely many balls on which it is a local diffeomorphism), and its inverse is $y \mapsto (r(y), y - r(y))$ with $r$ the nearest-point retraction of V6.6. Hidden hypothesis: $M$ nonempty. Formal setting: the first part is proved for $M \subseteq \mathbb R^k$ given by slice charts with model $\mathbb R^m$ and complement $\mathbb R^w$, $m + w = k$; the second part is stated for $\mathbb R^{K+1}$, that is for $k \ge 1$, as in §6–§7.

Milnor.Problems.normalBundleSetMilnor.Problems.isSliceSubmanifold_normalBundleMilnor.Problems.normalεMilnor.Problems.exists_diffeomorph_normalεMilnor.Problems.exists_normalTube_diffeomorph

What differs (the comparison)

  • $M$ is assumed nonempty, because the tubular radius of §6 and the minimum over a finite cover of $M$ both need it; for empty $M$ the claim is trivial but is not treated.
  • Boundarylessness, which Milnor states, is automatic here: a submanifold given by slice charts has no boundary by construction.
  • The identity $m + w = k$ must be named, since model and complement are given separately, and the second part is stated for $k = K + 1$, as everywhere in §6 and §7.
  • The order of the argument is reversed: uniform injectivity of $(x, v) \mapsto x + v$ is proved first from compactness, and $r(x + v) = x$ is deduced from it.

Problem 12, the retraction

restated

to confirmFor such $\varepsilon$, $r(x + v) = x$ is strictly closer to $x + v$ than any other point of

$r$ is the nearest-point retraction$M$ nonemptystrict inequality for every $z \ne x$
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 12

Define $r : N_\varepsilon \to M$ by $r(x + v) = x$; then $r(x + v)$ is closer to $x + v$ than any other point of $M$, and using this retraction one proves the analogue of Problem 4 with the sphere $S^p$ replaced by a manifold $M$.

He defines $r$ by the formula and takes for granted that the formula determines a map.

Verified edition V8.12

With $\varepsilon$ as in V8.11 and $r : N_\varepsilon \to M$ the retraction: for $(x, v) \in E$ with $\|v\| < \varepsilon$ we have $r(x + v) = x$, and $x$ is strictly closer to $x + v$ than any other point of $M$. The analogue of Problem 4 with $S^p$ replaced by $M$: for compact manifolds $X \subseteq \mathbb R^{L+1}$ and $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$, $M$ nonempty, every continuous map $X \to M$ is a uniform limit of smooth maps, and two smooth maps $X \to M$ which are continuously homotopic are smoothly homotopic. Proof: approximate by a smooth map into $\mathbb R^{K+1}$ and compose with $r$; smooth maps $X \to M$ closer than the tubular radius of V6.6 (which is at least $\varepsilon$; for this step $X$ need not be compact) are joined by $r((1-s)f + sg)$ (Milnor.Problems.smoothHomotopic_of_dist_lt_mfld); a continuous homotopy is approximated on $X \times [0, 1]$ and retracted.

Milnor.Problems.normalRetraction_nearestMilnor.Problems.exists_smooth_approx_mfldMilnor.Problems.smoothHomotopic_of_homotopic_mfld

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor defines $r$ by $r(x + v) = x$; the formal $r$ is the nearest-point retraction of §6, and $r(x + v) = x$ is a theorem, got from the uniform injectivity of Problem 11.
  • $M$ is assumed nonempty, as in Problem 11, since the tubular radius of §6 requires it.
  • The nearest-point claim is made as a strict inequality against every $z \in M$ with $z \ne x$, not merely as a statement that $x$ is a nearest point.
  • Compactness of $X$ is used only to approximate on $X \times [0,1]$; the step joining two nearby smooth maps into $M$ is stated without it.

Problem 13, linking numbers

stronger

to confirmFor disjoint compact oriented $a$- and $b$-manifolds mapped into $\mathbb R^{k+1}$,

smooth maps, not embedded M and Ntotal dimension k > 0bounding in the ambient {g ≥ 0} form
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 13

For disjoint manifolds $M, N \subseteq \mathbb R^{k+1}$ the linking map $\lambda : M \times N \to S^k$ is $\lambda(x, y) = (x - y)/\|x - y\|$. When $M$ and $N$ are compact, oriented and boundaryless with $m + n = k$, the degree of $\lambda$ is the linking number $l(M, N)$. Milnor asks for the symmetry $l(N, M) = (-1)^{(m+1)(n+1)} l(M, N)$, and for $l(M, N) = 0$ whenever $M$ bounds an oriented manifold $X$ disjoint from $N$.

He leaves the orientations of $M \times N$ and of $S^k$ unsaid, does not exclude $k = 0$, and gives no meaning to "bounds" beyond the word.

Verified edition V8.13

Let $A^a$ and $B^b$ be compact oriented boundaryless manifolds and $\alpha : A \to \mathbb R^{k+1}$, $\beta : B \to \mathbb R^{k+1}$ smooth maps with disjoint images, $k = a + b$. The linking map $\lambda(x, y) = (\alpha(x) - \beta(y))/\|\alpha(x) - \beta(y)\|$ is smooth, and the linking number $l(\alpha, \beta)$ is its degree, for the product orientation of $A \times B$ and the orientation of $S^k$ as the boundary of the disk. (Milnor's disjoint $M, N \subseteq \mathbb R^{k+1}$ are the case where $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are inclusions.)

  • Symmetry. If $k > 0$, then $l(\beta, \alpha) = (-1)^{(a+1)(b+1)}\, l(\alpha, \beta)$. Proof: $\lambda_{\beta,\alpha}$ is the antipodal map composed with $\lambda_{\alpha,\beta}$ composed with the swap of factors; the swap has sign $(-1)^{ab}$ everywhere and the antipodal map has degree $(-1)^{k+1}$.
  • Bounding. Suppose $A$ is the boundary $\{g = 0\}$ of a compact region $X = \{g \ge 0\}$ in an oriented $(a+1)$-manifold $W$ ($0$ a regular value of $g$, $A$ with the boundary orientation), and $\alpha$ extends to a smooth $\bar\alpha : W \to \mathbb R^{k+1}$ with $\bar\alpha(X)$ disjoint from $\beta(B)$. Then $l(\alpha, \beta) = 0$. Proof: the linking map extends over $X \times B$, and the boundary orientation of $A \times B$ inside $W \times B$ is the product orientation; a map which extends over a compact region has degree $0$ on its boundary (V6.1).

Hidden hypotheses: $k > 0$ (for $k = 0$ the target $S^0$ is not connected and the degree is not defined); $A$ and $B$ Hausdorff and second countable for the symmetry, and $B$ and $W$ Hausdorff and second countable for the bounding statement. Formal setting: "$M$ bounds an oriented manifold $X$ disjoint from $N$" is taken in the ambient form above, as manifolds with boundary are throughout.

Milnor.Problems.linkMapMilnor.Problems.linkingNumberMilnor.Problems.linkingNumber_symmMilnor.Problems.linkingNumber_eq_zero_of_bounds

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor's disjoint $M, N \subseteq \mathbb R^{k+1}$ are the case in which $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are inclusions; the formal statement allows any two smooth maps with disjoint images.
  • Both the symmetry and the vanishing assume $k > 0$: the degree is independent of the regular value only for a connected target, and $S^0$ is two points.
  • "$A$ bounds" is taken ambiently, as manifolds with boundary are throughout: $A = \{g = 0\}$ bounds the compact region $X = \{g \ge 0\}$ in a boundaryless $W$, and $\alpha$ extends over $W$.
  • The orientations Milnor leaves unsaid are fixed: the product orientation on $A \times B$, and $S^k$ oriented as the boundary of the disk, outward normal first.
77V8.13′

Problem 13, linking numbers in the sphere

stronger

to confirmFor $a, b \ge 1$, the linking number in $S^{k+1}$, computed after any rotation that

the book only asks for a definitionindependence of the rotation provedneeds a, b ≥ 1
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 13

The last sentence of Problem 13 asks the reader to define the linking number for disjoint manifolds in the sphere $S^{m+n+1}$.

It asks for a definition and supplies neither a construction nor the hypotheses under which one is well posed.

Verified edition V8.13′

For smooth maps $\alpha : A \to S^{k+1}$ and $\beta : B \to S^{k+1}$ with disjoint images, $k = a + b$: choose a rotation $R$ of $\mathbb R^{k+2}$ (a linear isometry of positive determinant) such that the north pole avoids $R\alpha(A) \cup R\beta(B)$, project stereographically from the north pole to $\mathbb R^{k+1}$, and take the linking number of V8.13. Such rotations exist when $A$ and $B$ are second countable (exists_admissible_rotation: the images are meagre; the formal statement also assumes $a, b \ge 1$, which its proof does not use). If $a, b \ge 1$ and $A$, $B$ are Hausdorff and second countable, the result does not depend on the choice (linkingNumberSph_eq). Proof: a rotation fixing the north pole acts on $\mathbb R^{k+1}$ by a linear map of positive determinant, which does not change linking numbers; a smooth family of rotations that keeps the north pole off both images does not change it either (homotopy invariance of the degree); two admissible rotations are joined by such families along two great-circle arcs through a common point, chosen outside the "bad midpoints", which form a meagre set (images of manifolds of dimensions $a + 1$ and $b + 1$, both less than $k + 1$; Brown's theorem and Baire's theorem). Hidden hypothesis: $a, b \ge 1$ for the independence. Remark (informal, not a formal result): the hypothesis cannot simply be dropped; for a point and a great sphere ($a = 0$) the answer depends on the side of the sphere on which the projection point lies. This example is not formalized.

Milnor.Problems.linkingNumberSphMilnor.Problems.exists_admissible_rotationMilnor.Problems.linkingNumberSph_eq

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor asks only that the linking number be defined in $S^{m+n+1}$; the formal item fixes one construction and proves that it does not depend on the choices it makes.
  • The construction is: rotate both images off the north pole, project stereographically onto $\mathbb R^{k+1}$, and take the Euclidean linking number of the projections.
  • Independence of the rotation is proved only for $a, b \ge 1$; for $a = 0$, a point against a great sphere, the value depends on the side of that sphere the projection point lies on.
  • That last example is an informal remark and is not formalized; no counterexample is proved in Lean.
78V8.14 a, b

Problem 14, the Hopf invariant

to confirmFor $p \ge 2$ and smooth $f, g : S^{2p-1} \to S^p$ with regular values $y \ne z$:

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V8.14 a, b

Let $p \ge 2$ and let $f, g : S^{2p-1} \to S^p$ be smooth. For a regular value $y$ of $g$ and a regular value $z$ of $f$ with disjoint preimages, $l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ denotes the linking number in the sphere (V8.13′) of the preimages, oriented as in §5.

  • a) For $y \ne z$ regular values of $f$, $l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ is locally constant in $y$ (and in $z$).
  • b) If $y \ne z$ are regular values of $f$ and of $g$ and $\|f(x) - g(x)\| < \|y - z\|$ for all $x$, then $l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z)) = l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z)) = l(g^{-1}(y), g^{-1}(z))$.
  • c) $l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ does not depend on the choice of regular values $y \ne z$; this integer is the Hopf invariant $H(f)$; smoothly homotopic maps have the same Hopf invariant.

Proof. The engine is a description of the linking number as a local degree (Milnor.Problems.degAt_phiR_eq_linkingNumberRot): after a rotation $R$ that keeps the north pole off both preimages, $l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ is the local degree at $((y, z), e)$ of $\Phi(x, x') = ((g(x), f(x')), \lambda(x, x'))$ on the open set of pairs of distinct points of $S^{2p-1}$ away from $R^{-1}(\text{north})$, where $\lambda$ is the direction between the stereographic images and $e$ is a regular value of the linking map. (At a preimage point the sign of $\Phi$ is the sign of the linking map; the comparison is a block determinant in which the sign $(-1)^{(p-1)p} = 1$ appears.) Local degrees do not change under homotopies whose preimages stay in a compact part of the domain, so the linking number does not change under homotopies of $g$ and $f$ during which the preimages of $y$ and $z$ stay disjoint and away from $R^{-1}(\text{north})$, with $y$ and $z$ regular only at the two ends (Milnor.Problems.linkingNumberRot_eq_of_homotopy). Moving $y$ along a short arc by rotations of the target is such a homotopy (a); so is the normalized chord from $f$ to $g$, because a chord point is no farther from $f(x)$ than $g(x)$ is (b); so is a smooth homotopy from $f$ to $g$ once $y$ and $z$ are chosen off the curve traced by one base point (c). The independence of $(y, z)$ is not derived from a) but proved directly (Milnor.Problems.linkPre_eq_linkPre_of_ne): the pairs of distinct points of $S^p$ form a connected set in which pairs of regular values are dense, and near any pair $(y_0, z_0)$ two pairs of regular values are joined by moving both values at once along short rotation arcs (Milnor.Problems.linkPre_move) that avoid $f(q)$, for a base point $q$ with $f(q) \notin \{y_0, z_0\}$; for a constant map all these linking numbers vanish.

Remark on a natural generalization (informal, not a formal result). For two different maps one should not expect $l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ to be locally constant in $y$ under the sole assumption $y \ne z$: moving $y$ can push $g^{-1}(y)$ through $f^{-1}(z)$, and the linking number can then jump. No counterexample is formalized; the formal mixed statement (Milnor.Problems.linkPre_eventually_eq) simply assumes the preimages disjoint. Milnor's statement, with a single map $f$, needs nothing more than $y \ne z$. Hidden hypothesis: $p \ge 2$ (for $p = 1$ the preimages are finite sets in $S^1$ and V8.13′ does not apply). Deviation: smooth maps and smooth homotopies (V4 convention).

Milnor.Problems.linkPreMilnor.Problems.linkPre_self_eventually_eqMilnor.Problems.linkPre_eq_of_norm_ltMilnor.Problems.hopfInvariantMilnor.Problems.linkPre_eq_hopfInvariantMilnor.Problems.hopfInvariant_eq_of_smoothHomotopic
79V8.14 c

Problem 14, the Hopf invariant

to confirm$l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ does not depend on the regular values $y \ne z$, and smoothly

the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor

No comparison row for this item; the Book note on the right gives the page.

Verified edition V8.14 c

Let $p \ge 2$ and let $f, g : S^{2p-1} \to S^p$ be smooth. For a regular value $y$ of $g$ and a regular value $z$ of $f$ with disjoint preimages, $l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ denotes the linking number in the sphere (V8.13′) of the preimages, oriented as in §5.

  • a) For $y \ne z$ regular values of $f$, $l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ is locally constant in $y$ (and in $z$).
  • b) If $y \ne z$ are regular values of $f$ and of $g$ and $\|f(x) - g(x)\| < \|y - z\|$ for all $x$, then $l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z)) = l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z)) = l(g^{-1}(y), g^{-1}(z))$.
  • c) $l(f^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ does not depend on the choice of regular values $y \ne z$; this integer is the Hopf invariant $H(f)$; smoothly homotopic maps have the same Hopf invariant.

Proof. The engine is a description of the linking number as a local degree (Milnor.Problems.degAt_phiR_eq_linkingNumberRot): after a rotation $R$ that keeps the north pole off both preimages, $l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ is the local degree at $((y, z), e)$ of $\Phi(x, x') = ((g(x), f(x')), \lambda(x, x'))$ on the open set of pairs of distinct points of $S^{2p-1}$ away from $R^{-1}(\text{north})$, where $\lambda$ is the direction between the stereographic images and $e$ is a regular value of the linking map. (At a preimage point the sign of $\Phi$ is the sign of the linking map; the comparison is a block determinant in which the sign $(-1)^{(p-1)p} = 1$ appears.) Local degrees do not change under homotopies whose preimages stay in a compact part of the domain, so the linking number does not change under homotopies of $g$ and $f$ during which the preimages of $y$ and $z$ stay disjoint and away from $R^{-1}(\text{north})$, with $y$ and $z$ regular only at the two ends (Milnor.Problems.linkingNumberRot_eq_of_homotopy). Moving $y$ along a short arc by rotations of the target is such a homotopy (a); so is the normalized chord from $f$ to $g$, because a chord point is no farther from $f(x)$ than $g(x)$ is (b); so is a smooth homotopy from $f$ to $g$ once $y$ and $z$ are chosen off the curve traced by one base point (c). The independence of $(y, z)$ is not derived from a) but proved directly (Milnor.Problems.linkPre_eq_linkPre_of_ne): the pairs of distinct points of $S^p$ form a connected set in which pairs of regular values are dense, and near any pair $(y_0, z_0)$ two pairs of regular values are joined by moving both values at once along short rotation arcs (Milnor.Problems.linkPre_move) that avoid $f(q)$, for a base point $q$ with $f(q) \notin \{y_0, z_0\}$; for a constant map all these linking numbers vanish.

Remark on a natural generalization (informal, not a formal result). For two different maps one should not expect $l(g^{-1}(y), f^{-1}(z))$ to be locally constant in $y$ under the sole assumption $y \ne z$: moving $y$ can push $g^{-1}(y)$ through $f^{-1}(z)$, and the linking number can then jump. No counterexample is formalized; the formal mixed statement (Milnor.Problems.linkPre_eventually_eq) simply assumes the preimages disjoint. Milnor's statement, with a single map $f$, needs nothing more than $y \ne z$. Hidden hypothesis: $p \ge 2$ (for $p = 1$ the preimages are finite sets in $S^1$ and V8.13′ does not apply). Deviation: smooth maps and smooth homotopies (V4 convention).

Milnor.Problems.linkPreMilnor.Problems.linkPre_self_eventually_eqMilnor.Problems.linkPre_eq_of_norm_ltMilnor.Problems.hopfInvariantMilnor.Problems.linkPre_eq_hopfInvariantMilnor.Problems.hopfInvariant_eq_of_smoothHomotopic
80V8.15

Problem 15

value differs

to confirmFor odd $p$, $H(f) = 0$; $H(g \circ f) = (\deg g)^2 H(f)$; the Hopf fibration

H(π) = −1, not +1p ≥ 2, smooth mapsthree conventions flip the sign
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 15

If the dimension $p$ is odd then $H(f) = 0$. For a composition $S^{2p-1} \xrightarrow{f} S^p \xrightarrow{g} S^p$, $H(g \circ f)$ is $H(f)$ times the square of the degree of $g$. The Hopf fibration $\pi : S^3 \to S^2$ is given by $\pi(x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4) = h^{-1}\!\left((x_1 + i x_2)/(x_3 + i x_4)\right)$, where $h$ is the stereographic projection to the complex plane, and Milnor asks for $H(\pi) = 1$.

He specifies $\pi$ only through that formula, and fixes no orientation conventions for $h$, for $S^2$, or for the preimages.

Verified edition V8.15

Let $p \ge 2$.

  • If $p$ is odd, $H(f) = 0$ for every smooth $f : S^{2p-1} \to S^p$.
  • For smooth $f : S^{2p-1} \to S^p$ and $g : S^p \to S^p$, $H(g \circ f) = (\deg g)^2 H(f)$.
  • The Hopf fibration. With $z_1 = x_1 + i x_2$ and $z_2 = x_3 + i x_4$, the map $\pi(z_1, z_2) = (2 z_1 \bar z_2,\ |z_1|^2 - |z_2|^2)$ is a smooth map $S^3 \to S^2$; it equals $h^{-1}(z_1/z_2)$ wherever $z_2 \ne 0$ ($h$ the stereographic projection from $(0,0,1)$) and $(0,0,1)$ where $z_2 = 0$; every point of $S^2$ is a regular value; and $H(\pi) = -1$.

Proof. Odd $p$: $l(N, M) = (-1)^{p^2} l(M, N) = -l(M, N)$ by V8.13, and both are $H(f)$ by V8.14. Composition: choose regular values $y \ne z$ of $g$ and of $g \circ f$ and one admissible rotation; the map $\Phi$ of $g \circ f$ is $(g \times g \times \mathrm{id})$ composed with the $\Phi$ of $f$; the local degree of a composition is the sum over the fibre of the sign of the outer map times the local degree of the inner one; here the signs are $\operatorname{sign}_g(y_i)\operatorname{sign}_g(z_j)$ and the local degrees are $l(f^{-1}(y_i), f^{-1}(z_j)) = H(f)$, so the double sum is $\deg g \cdot \deg g \cdot H(f)$. The Hopf fibration: the fibres over $y = (1,0,0)$ and $z = (-1,0,0)$ are the great circles $z_1 = z_2$ and $z_1 = -z_2$. After stereographic projection from $(0,0,0,1)$, exactly one pair of points, $a = (0,-1,0,-1)/\sqrt2$ on the first fibre and $a' = (0,1,0,-1)/\sqrt2$ on the second, has direction $e = (0,-1,0)$. Lifting positive bases of $T_yS^2$ and $T_zS^2$ to $u_1 = (1,0,-1,0)$, $u_2 = (0,-1,0,1)$ at $a$ and $w_1 = (0,1,0,1)$, $w_2 = (1,0,1,0)$ at $a'$, the fibre directions $t = (1,0,1,0)/\sqrt2$ and $t' = (-1,0,1,0)/\sqrt2$ are both negatively oriented as preimages, since $\det(a, u_1, u_2, t) = \det(a', w_1, w_2, t') = -2$; the derivative of the direction map sends $t$ and $t'$ to $\tfrac12(1,0,1)$ and $\tfrac12(1,0,-1)$, a negatively oriented basis at $e$. The linking number is the product of the three signs, $-1$.

A finding: the sign of $H(\pi)$. Milnor states $H(\pi) = 1$. With his conventions taken literally (spheres oriented as boundaries of disks, preimages oriented as in §5, $A \times B$ oriented as a product, $\lambda(x, y) = (x - y)/\|x - y\|$), the value is $-1$. This value was checked by hand, numerically, and in Lean (hopfInvariant_hopfMap). The comparison with other conventions that follows is an informal consequence of the formal sign lemmas (the degree of the antipodal map, the sign of the swap, the symmetry of linking numbers, the composition formula above); it is not itself a formal theorem. The sign flips under any one of three equally natural changes: $\lambda(x, y) = (y - x)/\|y - x\|$, which composes $\lambda$ with the antipodal map of $S^2$; ordering the product as $B \times A$, whose swap has sign $(-1)^{(p-1)^2} = -1$ for $p = 2$; or orienting the target sphere $S^2$ of the linking map $\lambda$ the other way. Changing the orientation of $S^3$, or of the target $S^2$ of $\pi$, reverses the orientations of both preimages at once and leaves $H$ unchanged. Putting the lifts before the tangent vectors in the preimage orientation changes nothing at all here, since the two orders differ by $(-1)^{dp} = +1$ for $d = 1$, $p = 2$ (V8.0 (iii)). So the book's value corresponds to one of the three changes above; the formal statement keeps the book's own conventions and records $-1$.

Milnor.Problems.hopfInvariant_eq_zero_of_oddMilnor.Problems.hopfInvariant_compMilnor.Problems.hopfMapMilnor.Problems.hopfMap_eq_milnorMilnor.Problems.isRegularValue_hopfMapMilnor.Problems.linkingNumberRot_hopfMapMilnor.Problems.hopfInvariant_hopfMap

What differs (the comparison)

  • Milnor asks for $H(\pi) = 1$; with his conventions taken literally the formal value is $-1$, a matter of convention rather than an error in the mathematics.
  • Those conventions are: $S^k$ oriented as the boundary of the disk, outward normal first; preimages oriented as in §5; $A \times B$ a product; $\lambda(x, y) = (x - y)/\|x - y\|$.
  • Any one of three equally natural changes restores $+1$: taking $\lambda(x, y) = (y - x)/\|y - x\|$, ordering the product as $B \times A$, or reversing the orientation of the target $S^2$ of $\lambda$.
  • Reversing the orientation of $S^3$, or of the target $S^2$ of $\pi$, changes nothing, since both preimage orientations reverse at once; the other two parts need only $p \ge 2$ and smoothness.
81V8.16

Problem 16, transversality

stronger

to confirmTransverse submanifolds with $n + n' < m$ are disjoint; the intersection of transverse

injective, δ-close deformationcodimension p + p′ and tangent spaceneeds p + p′ ≤ m; M compact nonempty
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 16

Two submanifolds $N$ and $N'$ of $M$ intersect transversally if at each $x \in N \cap N'$ the subspaces $TN_x$ and $TN'_x$ together generate $TM_x$; when $n + n' < m$ this means that $N \cap N' = \emptyset$. Milnor asks that a framed submanifold $N$ be deformable slightly so as to meet a given $N'$ transversally, and that the resulting intersection be a smooth manifold.

"Deformed slightly" is left imprecise, and he asks only that the intersection be a manifold, not what its codimension or tangent space is.

Verified edition V8.16

Submanifolds $N^n, N'^{n'}$ of $M^m$ intersect transversally if $TN_x + TN'_x = TM_x$ at every common point. If $n + n' < m$, transverse submanifolds are disjoint. The intersection of transverse submanifolds of codimensions $p$ and $p'$, with $p + p' \le m$, is a submanifold of codimension $p + p'$, with tangent space $TN_x \cap TN'_x$. A framed submanifold $N$ can be deformed slightly so as to meet a given $N'$ transversally: if $G : N \times B_\varepsilon \to M$ is a product neighbourhood (the Product Neighborhood Theorem, V7.7), then for arbitrarily small $u \in \mathbb R^p$ the translate $N_u = G(N \times \{u\})$ is a submanifold, carried injectively onto by $x \mapsto G(x, u)$, lying within any prescribed $\delta$ of $N$, and transverse to $N'$. Proof: with $\pi$ the second coordinate of the product neighbourhood, $N_u = \pi^{-1}(u)$, and $N_u$ meets $N'$ transversally exactly when $u$ is a regular value of $\pi$ restricted to $N'$ inside the neighbourhood; by Sard's theorem such $u$ exist arbitrarily close to $0$. The intersection is locally the common zero set of defining maps of $N$ and $N'$, whose joint derivative is surjective by transversality. Setting: $M$ is a submanifold of Euclidean space, as in §7; the statements about intersections need no compactness of $M$, and the deformation, which uses the product neighbourhood, needs $M$ compact and nonempty.

Milnor.Problems.TransverseMilnor.Problems.inter_eq_empty_of_transverseMilnor.Problems.isSliceSubmanifold_interMilnor.Problems.tangentN_interMilnor.Problems.exists_transverse_deformation

What differs (the comparison)

  • "Deformed slightly" is made precise: the translate $N_u = G(N \times \{u\})$ inside a product neighbourhood is transverse to $N'$, injectively parametrized, and within any prescribed $\delta$ of $N$.
  • The intersection is not only a manifold: it is a submanifold of $M$ of codimension $p + p'$ with tangent space $TN_x \cap TN'_x$, stated under the hypothesis $p + p' \le m$.
  • The deformation goes through the Product Neighborhood Theorem of §7 and so assumes $M$ compact and nonempty; the statements about intersections assume neither.
  • Transversality is formulated with the tangent spaces read inside the ambient $\mathbb R^{K+1}$, as everywhere in §7, and $M$ itself is a submanifold of Euclidean space.
82V8.17

Problem 17, the intersection product

narrower

to confirmFor $p, q \ge 1$ with $p + q \le m$, every pair of classes in

p, q ≥ 1 and p + q ≤ mexplicit framing of the intersectionuniqueness added, for p ≥ 1
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 17

Let $\Pi^p(M)$ be the set of all framed cobordism classes of codimension $p$ in $M$. Milnor asks that the transverse intersection operation be used to define a correspondence $\Pi^p(M) \times \Pi^q(M) \to \Pi^{p+q}(M)$.

He says nothing about how the intersection is to be framed, and does not mention that transverse representatives have to be found first.

Verified edition V8.17

$\Pi^p(M)$ is the set of framed cobordism classes of framed submanifolds of codimension $p$ of the compact nonempty manifold $M \subseteq \mathbb R^{K+1}$ (formally the set is indexed by the dimension $n$ and the codimension $p$ of the submanifolds, and is empty unless $n + p = m$). If framed submanifolds $F$ (codimension $p$, framing $v$) and $G$ (codimension $q$, framing $v'$) intersect transversally and $p + q \le m$, their intersection is framed as follows: at $x$, let $\alpha_F(x) : \mathbb R^{K+1} \to \mathbb R^p$ be the linear map with $\alpha_F(v_i) = e_i$ vanishing on the vectors orthogonal to all $v_i$, similarly $\alpha_G$, and $A = (\alpha_F, \alpha_G)$, which is surjective at the points of the intersection by transversality; the framing vectors of the intersection are $w_k = A^(AA^)^{-1} e_k$, so that $(\alpha_F w_k, \alpha_G w_k)$ is the $k$-th standard basis vector of $\mathbb R^p \times \mathbb R^q$. (Informally, $w_k$ is the unique vector in the span of $v$ and $v'$ with these values; this description is not part of the formal statement.) For $p \ge 1$, every pair of classes has transverse representatives; for $p, q \ge 1$ and $p + q \le m$ there is a map $\Pi^p(M) \times \Pi^q(M) \to \Pi^{p+q}(M)$ sending the classes of transverse $F, G$ to the class of the framed intersection, and for $p \ge 1$ it is unique. Proof: let $f, g$ be maps with Pontryagin manifolds $F, G$ at the north pole (Theorem C of §7, V7.9) and $\mu : S^p \times S^q \to S^{p+q}$ a smooth map with $\mu^{-1}(\text{north}) = \{(\text{north},\text{north})\}$ and invertible derivative there. The Pontryagin manifold of $\mu \circ (f, g)$ at the north pole is exactly the framed intersection, up to a positive rescaling of the framing (Lemma 1). Framed cobordisms of $F$ and of $G$ give homotopies of $f$ and $g$ (second half of Theorem B, V7.11), hence of $\mu \circ (f, g)$, hence a framed cobordism of the intersections (first half of Theorem B, V7.6). Transverse representatives: replace $F$ by the Pontryagin manifold of $f$ at a common regular value of $f$ and of $f$ restricted to $G$ (Brown's theorem; Theorem A of §7, V7.5, and Lemma 1). Formal hypotheses: $p \ge 1$, and $q \ge 1$ for the existence of the product; they come from the use of V7.5, V7.6 and V7.11, whose proofs rotate regular values, and are not claimed to be necessary.

Milnor.Problems.FramedClassMilnor.Framed.FramedSub.interMilnor.Problems.exists_transverse_repMilnor.Problems.exists_intersection_productMilnor.Problems.intersection_product_unique

What differs (the comparison)

  • The formal product is built for $p, q \ge 1$ and $p + q \le m$; $p, q \ge 1$ comes from the theorems of §7 that the proof uses, whose arguments rotate regular values, and is not claimed necessary.
  • Transverse representatives exist, and the product is determined by its values on them, under $p \ge 1$ alone; the book states neither fact.
  • The framing is written out: $w_k$ is the vector in the span of $v$ and $v'$ for which $(\alpha_F w_k, \alpha_G w_k)$ is the $k$-th standard basis vector of $\mathbb R^p \times \mathbb R^q$.
  • $\Pi^p(M)$ carries the dimension $n$ as a second index and is empty unless $n + p = m$, and $M$ is required to be compact and nonempty, as it already is in §7.
83V8.17′

Problem 17, the group

hypotheses explicit

to confirmIf $2p \ge m + 2$, disjoint union makes $\Pi^p(M)$ an abelian group; its zero is the

indexed by n, with n + p = mzero and inverse namedredundant hypothesis p ≥ 1
the full statement, Milnor beside Lean

Milnor §8, Problem 17

If $p \ge \tfrac12 m + 1$, Milnor asks that the disjoint union operation be used to make $\Pi^p(M)$ into an abelian group, comparing with the corresponding discussion on p. 49.

One line of exercise, with the zero and the inverses left entirely to the reader.

Verified edition V8.17′

Let $p \ge \tfrac12 m + 1$, i.e. $m + 2 \le 2p$. Then any two classes in $\Pi^p(M)$ have disjoint representatives; if $F \sim F_1$ and $G \sim G_1$ with $F, G$ disjoint and $F_1, G_1$ disjoint, then $F \sqcup G \sim F_1 \sqcup G_1$; and the disjoint union makes $\Pi^p(M)$ an abelian group, with zero the class of the empty manifold and $-[F]$ the class of $F$ with its first framing vector reversed. Proof: the dimension condition gives $2n + 2 \le m$, so an $n$-manifold, and even an $(n+1)$-manifold swept out by a cobordism, has image of empty interior in $S^p$; this lets one choose regular values (Brown's theorem) whose Pontryagin manifolds avoid a given disjoint piece, and move one component at a time, keeping every cobordism away from the other component: by rotation arcs (as in the proof of Theorem A of §7, V7.5), by the cobordism $H^{-1}(z)$ of a homotopy $H$ between the two maps (the second half of Theorem B, V7.11, gives $H$; V7.2 the cobordism), and by Lemma 1. Commutativity, associativity and the zero are identities of framed submanifolds. For the inverse, the cancellation homotopy of §7 (the family $s_1^2 - a^2\rho(t)$), transported into a product neighbourhood of $F$, is a cobordism from two parallel copies of $F$, one with the first framing vector reversed, to the empty manifold; translating each copy back to $F$ identifies the two classes. Formal note: the group is stated on the set of classes $\Pi^p(M)$ with the addition, zero and negation above; $n + p = m$ is needed for the empty manifold to be a framed submanifold. The formal group structure also takes $p \ge 1$ as a hypothesis, which is redundant, since $m + 2 \le 2p$ implies it.

What differs (the comparison)

  • $\Pi^p(M)$ is a set of classes indexed by the dimension $n$ as well as by the codimension $p$, and $n + p = m$ is needed for the empty submanifold to count as a framed submanifold at all.
  • The zero is the class of the empty submanifold and $-[F]$ the class of $F$ with its first framing vector reversed; the book leaves both to the reader.
  • The formal group structure carries $p \ge 1$ as an extra hypothesis, which is redundant because $m + 2 \le 2p$ already implies it.
  • Milnor's $p \ge \tfrac12 m + 1$ is written $m + 2 \le 2p$, and $M$ is compact, nonempty and embedded in $\mathbb R^{K+1}$.
